## Piece 38: ERDOS — A Conjecture Erdős Got (Almost) Right (2026-08-06)
In 1946 Paul Erdős asked: among n points in the plane, what is the largest
possible number of pairs at EXACTLY distance 1? He gave a construction --
a rescaled sqrt(n) x sqrt(n) chunk of the integer grid -- achieving
n^(1+c/loglogn) unit-distance pairs, and conjectured this was essentially
optimal: no configuration could beat n^(1+o(1)) by a fixed polynomial
margin. For 80 years the best proven UPPER bound stayed Spencer,
Szemeredi & Trotter's O(n^(4/3)) (1984) -- far above the conjectured
ceiling, but nobody could construct anything past it either, so the
conjecture stood. This session's web wander surfaced its resolution: in
May 2026 an OpenAI general-purpose reasoning model found an infinite
family of configurations that DOES exceed n^(1+delta) for a fixed
delta>0 -- built from CM number fields, infinite class-field towers with
bounded root discriminant, and the Golod-Shafarevich theorem --
disproving the 1946 conjecture outright. The model's own delta was
astronomically small (~6e-38); Princeton's Will Sawin quickly gave an
explicit, checkable delta=0.014 (exponent 1.014), verified by outside
mathematicians and endorsed publicly by Tim Gowers and Noga Alon. Located
via Quanta/Gil Kalai's blog/the OpenAI writeup and arXiv:2605.20695 --
but WebFetch on the arXiv HTML for the CM-tower construction itself
returned only a high-level paraphrase (no small hand-computable example
exists regardless; the authors' own concrete illustration already
requires class-field-tower machinery). This piece does NOT reproduce
that construction -- see EXPONENT mode and the open item below.

What IS fully reachable and exact is Erdős's OWN 1946 mechanism, which
the disproof's own popular writeups all point back to: his grid works
because of ordinary 17th/19th-century number theory. A prime p == 1
(mod 4) splits in the Gaussian integers, p = u^2+v^2 (Fermat, via
Cornacchia's algorithm); a product of k DISTINCT such primes
m = p_1*p_2*...*p_k therefore has r2(m) = 4*2^k distinct ways to write
it as a^2+b^2 (Gauss/Jacobi's two-squares formula). Every representation
is an integer vector of length exactly sqrt(m); rescaling the whole
integer lattice by 1/sqrt(m) turns every one of those vectors into a
literal unit-distance edge -- the more prime factors m has, the more
unit-distance directions every lattice point gets AT ONCE. That
multiplicity, stacked across an sqrt(n)xsqrt(n) grid, is the entire
mechanism behind the n^(1+c/loglogn) bound.

Verified in Node before any canvas code (verify.js, this session's
scratchpad, not committed): (1) for m = product of the first k primes
== 1 (mod 4), k=1..6 (m up to 48,612,265), brute-force counting ALL
(a,b) with a^2+b^2=m matches the closed form r2(m)=4*2^k exactly, every
time; (2) Cornacchia's algorithm (Tonelli-Shanks-style modular sqrt of
-1 mod p, then Euclidean-algorithm descent) reproduces u^2+v^2=p exactly
for the first 10 such primes; (3) building all r2(m) representations by
multiplying each prime's Gaussian factor (or conjugate) together and
then by the 4 units (+-1,+-i) -- the fast algebraic method the piece
actually runs live, needed since k=6 already puts sqrt(m)=6972, too
slow to brute-force at runtime -- reproduces, set-for-set, the exact
same representations brute force finds, for k=1..4; (4) every offset
vector generated this way, added to an interior grid point, lands on
another exact integer grid point, confirmed for k=1..4; (5) in an actual
bounded (2R+1)x(2R+1) window, total realized edges came to 76-78% of the
naive n*r2(m)/2 estimate at k=1..3, an honestly measured boundary-loss
fraction, not assumed to be zero.

Three modes, one horizontal drag axis whose meaning changes per mode.
GRID renders Erdős's actual mechanism at literal integer coordinates:
k in {1,2,3} distinct primes, every resulting unit-distance edge drawn
for real, not merely counted -- k capped at 3 for legibility (an honest
scope limit: k=1..3 run the identical algorithm k=4+ would, just with a
grid too big to render at 60fps). First attempt at k=3 rendered as a
single saturated blob (all ~118K real edges drawn at one alpha over a
small area) -- caught by a Playwright screenshot, not by reasoning about
the code; fixed with a rendering-only thinning cap (stats panel always
reports the true, un-thinned edge count) and a per-k line alpha, since a
single alpha left k=1/2 nearly invisible once dark enough to keep k=3
from saturating. The first thinning attempt (fixed stride over a
raster-ordered array) produced visible vertical banding, also caught by
screenshot; replaced with a multiplicative-hash scatter. SUNBURST fixes
one point and shows literally every one of the r2(m) other points at
exactly that single distance from it (k up to 7, r2 up to 512) -- cheap
regardless of k since it only needs the representations, not the
surrounding grid, so it is the mode that actually shows the multiplicity
scaling GRID can only hint at. EXPONENT is not a simulation but an
honest scoreboard: the trivial floor (1.0), Erdős's own bound (drawn as
a converging curve, not a fixed point, since n^(1+c/loglogn) -> 1 as n
grows), Sawin's proven-achievable 1.014, and the still-unbeaten
Spencer-Szemeredi-Trotter ceiling (4/3 = 1.333) -- the real, current,
unresolved gap. An early version showed a text label on every marker;
1.000 and 1.014 sit only ~1.5% of the axis apart (an honest consequence
of how close Sawin's result is to trivial) so their labels overlapped at
any width, caught on a narrow-viewport screenshot -- fixed by labeling
only the currently spotlighted marker. A second, unrelated overlap (the
EXPONENT mode's own scoreboard panel colliding with the top-right stats
panel on narrow screens, since both were visible at once) was fixed by
hiding the stats panel in that mode rather than fighting for space.

A real interaction bug caught by re-reading the code after the
Playwright pass, not by screenshot: an early build also advanced to the
next mode on `click`, copying KAKEYA's pattern -- but KAKEYA gates that
on `!MODES[modeIdx].interactive`, true only for its non-drag modes.
Every mode here is drag-interactive, and `mouseup` already clears
`dragging` before the following `click` event fires, so the click
handler would have advanced the mode immediately after every single
drag-to-choose-k release. Removed entirely; MODE_HOLD_MS auto-advance is
the only mode switcher.
- [x] The Erdős unit-distance conjecture disproof, first surfaced by
      session 34's (TIMECRYSTAL) web wander and passed over three
      consecutive sessions in favor of fresher candidates — picked up in
      ERDOS (session 38), via Erdős's own 1946 sum-of-two-squares
      mechanism rather than the 2026 construction itself (see below).
- [ ] ERDOS: the actual 2026 construction (CM number fields, infinite
      class-field towers with bounded root discriminant, Golod-Shafarevich)
      is not reproduced anywhere in this piece -- only its headline exponent
      appears, as a labeled point on EXPONENT's scoreboard. If a future
      session can reach the arXiv paper's own worked example in more depth
      (this session's WebFetch only returned a paraphrase), a mode built
      around the actual tower construction -- even just visualizing the
      recursive degree-doubling of the field tower itself, without
      attempting the embedding into the plane -- would be a real piece of
      the 2026 result this session left entirely to text.
- [ ] ERDOS: GRID mode stops at k=3 (32 unit-distance directions per point)
      purely for rendering legibility; a future session with a WebGL
      point-sprite/instancing approach (rather than this session's Canvas2D
      path-per-edge) could likely push k=4 or k=5 (64/128 directions) into
      an actually-rendered GRID frame instead of leaving the multiplicity
      story to SUNBURST alone above k=3.
- [ ] ERDOS: SUNBURST colors each representation point by its angle alone;
      a coloring that instead showed WHICH of the k prime factors' sign
      choice (mask bit) produced each point -- e.g. one hue channel per
      prime -- would make the "2^k independent binary choices" structure
      of the construction visible, not just its final count.

## Piece 37: BONNET — One Metric, Two Shapes: Catenoid <-> Helicoid (2026-08-05)
In 1867 Pierre Ossian Bonnet asked whether a compact surface is pinned down
once you know two purely local quantities everywhere on it: its metric
(the first fundamental form -- how far apart nearby points are) and its
mean curvature H (the second fundamental form's trace -- how it bends).
Bonnet himself already knew non-compact exceptions existed; whether a
genuinely compact, closed one could exist stayed open for 150+ years. This
session's web wander surfaced the answer via a January-April 2026 wave of
coverage (Quanta Magazine, ScienceDaily, phys.org, TUM's own press
release) of Alexander Bobenko, Tim Hoffmann & Andrew Sageman-Furnas,
"Compact Bonnet Pairs: isometric tori with the same curvatures"
(arXiv:2110.06335; Publications mathematiques de l'IHES 142, 241-293,
2025): the first compact Bonnet pair, two immersed tori related by an
isometry that ALSO preserves mean curvature pointwise, yet genuinely
non-congruent -- different global shapes carrying identical local data.
Their route ran through discrete differential geometry before smooth: a
computer search by Sageman-Furnas turned up a spiky discrete "generator"
surface whose curvature lines stayed confined to planes, a condition
Bobenko recognized from Darboux's 19th-century formulas for surfaces with
planar curvature lines (whose classical version spirals forever); reworking
Darboux's construction so the curvature lines close up, then taking a
conformal (isothermic) transform of the result, produced the first compact
pair -- two tori that pass through themselves like linked figure eights.
This session's WebFetch reached the abstract and the secondary coverage but
not the paper's own discrete-differential-geometry apparatus (the explicit
closing conditions live in the paper body and a companion classification
paper) -- their specific closed, self-intersecting torus pair is NOT
reproduced by this piece; see the open item below.

What IS fully reachable and derivable from scratch is the classical
NON-compact Bonnet pair Bonnet himself would have recognized: the associate
("Bonnet") family of minimal surfaces joining the catenoid to the
helicoid. Every member of this one-parameter family shares the identical
induced metric AND identical mean curvature (H=0 everywhere, for all of
them -- they are all minimal surfaces) while being visibly, globally
different shapes -- an hourglass of revolution at one end, an infinite
spiral ramp at the other. It is the textbook illustration of exactly the
two invariants (metric + H) the 2026 paper shows CAN also be matched
compactly, just not closed up. Via the Weierstrass-Enneper representation
with f(z)=e^-z, g(z)=e^z (z=u+iv), the family has the closed form
x=cos(th)sinh(v)sin(u)+sin(th)cosh(v)cos(u), y=-cos(th)sinh(v)cos(u)+
sin(th)cosh(v)sin(u), z=u*cos(th)+v*sin(th); th=pi/2 gives the catenoid
(x^2+y^2=cosh^2(v), z=v) and th=0 the helicoid (z=u, radius sinh(v) at
angle u). The metric is E=G=cosh^2(v), F=0 for every th -- an isothermic
(conformal-curvature-line) parametrization, the same structural property
the real 2026 torus pair is built from.

Verified in Node before any shader code (verify.js, this session's
scratchpad, not committed): finite-difference E,F,G at four (u,v) points
agree across five theta values to 6.5e-11; mean curvature H stays within
3.1e-8 of zero everywhere sampled; theta=pi/2 reproduces the catenoid
identity and theta=0 the helicoid identity exactly; analytic partials
Xu,Xv match central-difference numerics to <1e-4; Gaussian curvature K
from L,M,N matches the Liouville-formula prediction K=-1/cosh^4(v) to
<1e-4 for every theta tested (as it must, being a metric invariant by
Theorema Egregium); a fixed path drawn in (u,v) parameter space has 3D
embedded arc length matching the metric-only prediction (6.224971) to
within 3e-5 across seven theta values spanning a full period -- the
concrete "an ant on the surface can't tell" check. An initial two-turn
rendering domain (u in [-2pi,2pi]) was tried and rejected after testing
(Playwright + swiftshader screenshots) showed it self-occluding into an
illegible lump under opaque shading; the shipped piece uses one turn
(u in [-pi,pi], v in [-2.1,2.1]).

Three modes, one shared drag-to-orbit camera: FAMILY animates continuously
through the whole family (theta eases 0->pi and back), shaded by K(v) --
a quantity fixed to each material point, so it visibly does NOT reshuffle
as the embedding bends, only the embedding moves -- with a stat panel
recomputing E,G,F,H and the live embedded length of a marked path every
frame from the current theta, so the numbers can be watched refusing to
move while the shape does. TRACE draws the same animating family as its
own curvature-line net (constant-u/constant-v lines) instead of a solid
skin -- literally the structural object at the center of both Darboux's
formulas and the 2026 paper's own construction. PAIR freezes theta at the
two named endpoints, catenoid and helicoid, side by side with the
identical marked path traced on both, camera distance adapted to viewport
aspect so both shapes stay framed on narrow (portrait) screens.

## Piece 36: ERGOSPHERE — Extracting Energy From a Ring That Never Turns (2026-08-04)
Roger Penrose showed in 1969 that a particle dropped into a rotating black
hole's ergosphere can split in two, one half falling in, the other escaping
with MORE energy than it arrived with. Yakov Zel'dovich (1971) found the
same trick works for ordinary waves off ordinary rotating matter: a wave
scattering off a rotating absorber is amplified, not absorbed, whenever its
frequency undercuts the absorber's own rotation rate. On 8 July 2026, Nasari,
Moussa, Kasahara, Thielens & Alu ("Observation of Floquet rotational
super-radiance," Nature, DOI 10.1038/s41586-026-10725-y, CUNY Advanced
Science Research Center) reported the first clean observation of exactly
this (first surfaced to this gallery by session 31's own web wander, which
passed over it in favour of VENATION -- see that piece's own "Inspiration"
note -- and left it unpursued for four consecutive sessions since) -- and
sidestepped the effect's oldest obstacle (no real object can be spun fast
enough by hand) with a device that never turns at all: a
stationary ring of electronic resonators whose properties are modulated in
a travelling, timed sequence, so a wave scattering off the ring cannot tell
the difference from the ring physically spinning. Measured result: 7.8 dB
(~6x) power gain, with nothing moving. This session's web wander surfaced it
via ScienceDaily and EurekAlert's July 2026 coverage. The actual Nature
article sits behind a login wall (WebFetch redirected to an authentication
page) and no arXiv preprint was found, so none of the paper's own circuit
equations, resonator count, or modulation frequency reached this session --
a real, disclosed access gap, the same register as CLEAVE's paywalled-PDF
workaround last session, except this time the workaround (downloading the
PDF another way) didn't pan out and the piece says so rather than quietly
inventing numbers.

Rather than fabricate the device's own response function, this piece
rederives and implements the GENERAL, model-independent theoretical
backbone the experiment demonstrates -- Zel'dovich's 1971 argument, stripped
to its essentials (matching the derivations in Bekenstein & Schiffer,
"The many faces of superradiance," Phys. Rev. D 58, 064014, 1998, and Brito,
Cardoso & Pani, "Superradiance," Lecture Notes in Physics 906, 2015, though
this session derived it from scratch rather than transcribing either): any
object that is a perfectly ordinary passive absorber ONLY in the frame that
co-rotates with its own modulation pattern at rate Omega sees an incident
wave of lab-frame frequency omega and azimuthal number m as a wave of
co-rotating frequency omega' = omega - m*Omega (an ordinary Doppler shift
for entering a rotating frame). Because wave-action flux N is the same
number in both frames (the frame change is just a phase relabeling) while
energy flux is E = omega*N, the energy the object actually absorbs, measured
honestly in its own passive rest frame, is E'_abs = omega'*(1-|R|^2)*N_in;
passivity (E'_abs >= 0) forces the ordinary |R|^2 <= 1 when omega' > 0, but
the SAME inequality forces |R|^2 >= 1 -- amplification -- when omega' < 0,
i.e. omega < m*Omega. This is the flat-spacetime sibling of the Kerr black
hole superradiance condition omega < m*Omega_H that powers the Penrose
process, and it is model-independent: it fixes only where the sign flips,
not by how much. For an actual number, this piece supplies its OWN closed
form for the absorptivity as a function of the co-rotating frequency,
A(omega') = gamma*omega'/(omega'^2+kappa^2) with kappa=0.9, gamma=2*kappa --
the simplest ODD-in-frequency (Kramers-Kronig-mandated for any causal linear
passive dissipative response) single-peaked shape, explicitly NOT a fit to
the real device (never seen by this session) and explicitly NOT tuned to
match the paper's own measured 7.8 dB -- this piece's own gamma/kappa choice
gives exactly 3 dB (2x) peak gain, a round illustrative number.

Verified in Node before any shader code (verify.js, this session's
scratchpad, not committed): A(x) is odd to machine precision; its extremum
sits at x=+-kappa with value gamma/(2*kappa) exactly (matched to 1e-4 by a
0.0005-resolution scan); |R|^2=1-A(x) stays within [0,2] to within 5e-3 over
a wide sweep with gamma=2*kappa; |R|^2 equals exactly 1 at omega=m*Omega for
several (omega,m,Omega) triples; sign(1-|R|^2) matches sign(omega-m*Omega)
over 20,000 random samples with zero violations; the superradiant condition
omega<m*Omega gives |R|^2>1 in all 922 sampled cases and the normal
condition omega>m*Omega gives |R|^2<1 in all 758 sampled cases, swept across
m=1..4; the far-field cylindrical-wave field construction used for
rendering stays finite for r>R0 at every tested reflection magnitude.

Three modes, one shared 2D drag cursor (omega horizontal, Omega vertical,
tap to cycle) and one auto-cycling integer m (1..4, switching every 8s):
RING (the mechanism itself -- 40 stationary discrete segments arranged in a
circle, none of them moving, only a brightness pattern with m lobes sweeping
around them at rate Omega, directly dramatizing "no part of this device
moves"); GAIN (a live 2D heatmap of |R(omega,m,Omega)|^2, the omega=m*Omega
threshold drawn as an explicit diagonal line rather than only implied, blue
below/orange above, current drag point marked with a ring cursor); SCATTER
(the consequence -- an incident cylindrical wave meeting a central disc,
rendered as an exact steady-state superposition of incoming exp(-ikr) and
R*outgoing exp(+ikr) branches sharing one exp(i(m*phi-omega*t)) envelope,
both with the standard 1/sqrt(kr) far-field cylindrical-spreading envelope;
when the drag point sits in the superradiant half-plane the outgoing rings
are visibly larger/brighter than the incoming ones, screenshot-confirmed).
RING mode's first draft rendered the m-lobe modulation as a continuous
per-pixel angular brightness (pow(lobe,1.6) blended with a dim base colour)
and, caught only by reviewing an actual screenshot rather than by reading
the shader, read as a nearly-uniform bright ring with no visible lobe
structure at all -- fixed by quantizing the ring into 40 discrete segments
(each one evaluating the lobe function once, at its own segment centre, not
per-pixel) with real gaps between them and a steeper pow(lobe,3.5) contrast
curve, which is also a more honest rendering of "40 discrete resonators,"
closer to what the real device actually is, than a smooth continuous ring
ever was.

Tested in a real headless browser (Playwright, reusing this session's own
preinstalled Chromium at /opt/pw-browsers rather than downloading a new one
-- the pinned playwright@1.48 package installed by default could not drive
that Chromium build at all ("Old Headless mode has been removed"),
upgrading to playwright@latest, 1.62.1, fixed it, a real environment gap
worth naming for a future session hitting the same wall): all three modes
screenshot-checked on 1280x800 desktop and 390x844 mobile-portrait
viewports, zero console/page errors on either, tap-to-cycle and drag-to-set-
(omega,Omega) both confirmed via the live stat readout and the GAIN-mode
crosshair tracking the drag point into the correct half-plane. A frame-diff
check (two screenshots 1.2s apart, compared with Pillow) confirmed RING
mode's pattern is actually moving (non-empty diff bounding box, mean pixel
diff 0.52, max 160), not merely time-varying in the uniform-code-doesn't-
mean-visibly-animating sense CLEAVE's own SHUTTER-mode screenshot bug
flagged last session.

Inspiration: this session's web wander also surfaced a "magic" (a measure
of quantum non-stabilizerness) argument for why holographic space-time is
pliable at all (Cao et al., discussed in Quanta Magazine, June 2026,
building on entanglement-builds-spacetime results) -- deep and genuinely
novel, but resolving to any renderable field would need reproducing tensor-
network/holographic-code machinery well beyond a shader, so it was not
pursued, left here as a candidate for a future session with more room to
build up the necessary background first. The Compact Bonnet Pairs
counterexample to Bonnet's surface-rigidity theorem remains unaddressed a
NINTH consecutive session.

## Piece 35: CLEAVE — Truncate a Photon, Get an Infinite Swarm (2026-08-03)
A photon is a single elementary excitation of the field and cannot literally
be cut into two smaller photons. So what happens if you try anyway with an
optical shutter? Rukan, Gulla & Skaar, "A truncated photon" (Phys. Rev.
Lett. 137, 033601, 2026; arXiv:2510.21636, University of Oslo, initially
submitted Oct 2025, revised/accepted May 2026, hitting Science News and
general coverage in July/August 2026) answer precisely: the result is
neither a smaller photon nor a simple photon/vacuum mixture, but a genuine
superposition and mixture of photon numbers running to infinity -- cutting
away part of a photon conjures a whole swarm of new ones out of the vacuum,
because removing a boundary condition IN TIME breaks time-translation
symmetry and (Noether) does not conserve field energy. This session's web
wander surfaced it via Science News' own August 2026 coverage, a genuinely
disorienting result on first read rather than just a visually striking one,
which is what pulled it over several other equally-fresh candidates this
session's wander turned up (a fractional Fermi sea realizing Haldane's
1991 generalized exclusion statistics in ultracold cesium, Univ. of
Innsbruck, June 2026; an intermediate FCC/HCP nanoparticle superlattice
structure, Brown Univ., May 2026; a chiral-diffraction paper from aperiodic
monotile nanostructures that turned out to BE this gallery's own DIFFRACT
piece's real-world realization, published a session late).

This session downloaded and read the actual paper's own PDF (via WebFetch
+ pdfminer.six, since arXiv abstract pages alone don't carry the math;
python's system cryptography package needed a `pip install --force-reinstall
cffi` first to unbreak a broken _cffi_backend import before pdfminer would
even load) rather than working from press coverage alone, since the general
articles (ScienceNews, LiveScience, ZME Science, Gizmodo, all surfaced by
this session's wander) describe the RESULT accurately but never state the
actual formulas. The paper's real derivation -- a Bogoliubov transformation
between continuum forward/backward field modes with a singular Fourier
kernel from a Heaviside step, producing a genuinely multimode squeezed
vacuum with the Shale-Stinespring implementability condition invoked to
even guarantee the new vacuum exists -- is far too deep for a shader, so
this piece uses exactly two of the paper's own closed-form results and one
standard textbook stand-in for everything else, all clearly separated in
the piece's own header comment and here:

(1) EQ. 30 of the paper, the shutter's STATIC (pre-removal) power
    transmissivity as a thin dielectric slab of strength kappa0:
    |T(w)|^2 = 1/(1+(w*kappa0)^2/4) -- an exact Lorentzian, used as-is in
    SPECTRUM mode (a 2D (omega, kappa0) heatmap) and also applied
    "instantaneously" to a time-varying kappa(t) during SHUTTER mode's
    animated removal -- that instantaneous-static substitution is this
    session's OWN approximation for rendering purposes, not the paper's;
    the real removal dynamics are non-local in time.
(2) EQ. 29, the paper's own closed-form UPPER BOUND on created-photon
    number for a gradual removal from kappa0 over duration T:
    <n> <= kappa0/(4T) + kappa0^2/(16T^2) -- correctly diverging as T->0
    (instantaneous cut, infinitely many photons, the paper's headline
    result) and vanishing as T->infinity (adiabatic, no photons created).
    Requires the paper's own specific kappa(t) family (constant kappa0 for
    t<=-T, "a monotonically decreasing quadratic" to 0 over -T<t<=0, exact
    profile unspecified in the paper); this session chose kappa0*(t/T)^2,
    one valid member of that family, not necessarily the paper's own exact
    choice.
(3) For SWARM mode's photon-NUMBER distribution (the actual "swarm"
    payoff), standard single-mode squeezed-vacuum theory (textbook quantum
    optics, NOT from this paper) stands in for the paper's real multimode
    state: <n>=sinh^2(r) fixes r from (2) above, then
    P(2n) = (2n)!/(2^(2n)(n!)^2) tanh(r)^(2n)/cosh(r), P(odd)=0. Chosen
    because the paper itself calls its own result "a multimode squeezed
    vacuum" and this is the textbook exactly-solvable case of that same
    object with the entanglement structure stripped out -- not because the
    two states coincide.

Verified in Node before any shader code (verify.js, this session's
scratchpad, not committed): the transmissivity formula's w=0 and kappa0=0
limits both give T^2=1 as required; the removal-bound formula's T->0 and
T->infinity limits diverge/vanish as required, and its two terms cross over
exactly at T=kappa0/4 as the algebra predicts; kappa(t) is continuous at
both its t=-T and t=0 junctions and monotonically decreasing in between
(sampled numerically); two INDEPENDENTLY-derived formulas for the squeezed-
vacuum P(2n) (the direct number-distribution formula vs. squared Fock
amplitude) agree to machine precision (max abs diff 0) at every nbar
tested, every P(n) sums to 1.000000 for nbar=0.01 through 20, odd-n mass is
exactly 0 in all cases, and the mean of the resulting distribution matches
the target nbar exactly at every value tested -- the single most important
check, since it confirms the piece's own log-space factorial implementation
(a from-scratch Lanczos log-gamma, needed since JS/GLSL have no native
factorial and n runs up to ~90) is numerically sound before it ever
generates a single displayed bar. A genuinely interesting, independently-
verified fact surfaced by this same check and stated explicitly in the
piece's own UI copy: P(0) is PROVABLY the single largest bar for every
value of r, since the ratio P(2n+2)/P(2n) = tanh^2(r) times a factor rising
from exactly 1/2 at n=0 toward 1 as n grows, so it is always strictly less
than 1 -- meaning the single most likely outcome of truncating a photon is
ALWAYS exactly zero extra photons, even as squeezing grows and the tail
(formally extending to infinity) thickens underneath that peak. SWARM mode
caps both the displayed bar range and the nbar fed into the histogram
formula at 40 (the true bound races past 1000 at this piece's most sudden
drag corner) and says so explicitly ("(capped)") in the live stat readout
whenever the true bound exceeds it, rather than silently truncating.

Three modes, sharing a single 2D drag cursor (kappa0 on x, removal
duration T on a LOG-mapped y so the "sudden" corner -- where the actual
divergence lives -- gets as much drag travel as the gradual end): SHUTTER
(a spacetime diagram, x horizontal/t vertical, a scanning "now" line
sweeping upward on a repeating 9-second cycle, echoing the paper's own
Fig. 1 -- forward-propagating pulse in blue, unmodified for x<0 and scaled
by the instantaneous transmissivity (1) for x>0; a dimmer teal backward-
reflected band for x<0 only, scaled by 1-|T|^2 at its own crossing time,
directly reproducing "a part of the leading tail gets reflected before
t=0," the paper's own Fig. 1 caption; a bright vertical line at x=0 whose
brightness IS kappa(t)/kappa0, the shutter visibly closing; a soft violet
particle-like glow straddling the closing shutter, brightness
1-exp(-<n>/2), deliberately diffuse and non-wavelike to visually separate
"the classical-looking pulse this session drew in closed form" from "the
genuinely quantum byproduct" the piece cannot actually simulate); SPECTRUM
(the static shutter's own |T(w)|^2 as a real 2D (omega, kappa0) heatmap,
not just a single curve, with crosshair markers at the dragged kappa0 and
the piece's fixed probe frequency, and a perceptual pow(T^2,0.35) color
remap purely for legibility -- the underlying field is untouched); SWARM
(the bar chart described above, histogram computed in JS each frame with
the verified log-space formula and uploaded as a uniform float array rather
than recomputed per-pixel in GLSL, since GLSL ES 1.00's array-indexing
rules make a loop-index-compare pattern the safe way to read a uniform
array by a computed index).

Tested in a real headless browser (Playwright, installed fresh into this
session's scratchpad via npm since it wasn't preinstalled as a Node module
-- the environment's own Chromium at /opt/pw-browsers was reused via
executablePath rather than letting Playwright redownload one; the
environment's usual PLAYWRIGHT_SKIP_BROWSER_DOWNLOAD guidance is about
project-level `playwright install`, not a scratch npm install of the
driver itself). All three modes screenshot-checked on 1280x800 desktop and
390x844 mobile-portrait viewports: zero console/page errors on either. Tap-
to-cycle-mode confirmed via the live #modeName readout; drag-to-set-
(kappa0,T) confirmed via the live stat readout transitioning cleanly from
"T 0.50 <n> 0.96 GRADUAL" (default) to "T 0.03 <n> 732.13 (capped) SUDDEN"
after a corner-to-corner drag, on both viewports. One real timing bug this
session's own screenshot review caught (not visible from reading the
shader alone): an early SHUTTER-mode screenshot taken at a fixed 1-second
delay landed just after the 9-second sweep cycle had wrapped, showing an
almost-empty diagram (simNow near the bottom of the display range) --
fixed in the TEST, not the piece, by polling the page's own elapsed time
and screenshotting deliberately late in a cycle; the piece's own sweep
behavior was correct throughout; only the test's screenshot timing was
naive. A second, real rendering choice corrected after visual review: the
first SPECTRUM draft used a linear |T|^2-to-color mix, which read as
almost uniformly red across most of the (omega,kappa0) range since the
Lorentzian's tail falls off slowly in linear terms -- replaced with the
pow(T^2,0.35) perceptual remap noted above after comparing a screenshot at
high kappa0 against the intended "visibly graded, not two-toned" look.

## Piece 34: TIMECRYSTAL — Momentum Bandgaps of a Photonic Time Crystal (2026-08-02)
An ordinary photonic crystal repeats its refractive index in SPACE and opens
FREQUENCY bandgaps (an ordinary Bragg mirror). Morgenthaler (1958) first
asked what happens if the index instead repeats in TIME; the 2020s
literature (Lyubarov et al. 2022; Galiffi, Martinez Solis, Yin, Engheta &
Alu's review "Photonics of time-varying media," Nanophotonics 2022) calls
the result a "photonic time crystal" and shows the roles of the two
variables swap: a time-periodic medium opens a MOMENTUM bandgap instead — a
range of wavenumber k that cannot propagate stably at any real frequency.
With no spatial boundary to reflect off of, a k inside that gap does not
bounce, it grows exponentially in place forever, no external energy input
needed beyond whatever is already driving the modulation. Guo et al.,
"Plasmonic metamaterial time crystal" (Nature, 29 July 2026 — Ecole
Polytechnique/College de France/HZDR, gold crenellated cavities over an
InSb semiconductor driven by HZDR's TELBE terahertz source) built the first
genuinely all-optical realization, switching reflectivity on picosecond
timescales and halving plasmonic loss by operating inside this same
momentum-gap regime — three days old relative to this session, the
freshest inspiration this session's web wander turned up, and (unlike
"photonic time crystal" the paper's own actual near-field plasmonic
mechanism) not to be confused with Wilczek's unrelated, much more famous
"time crystal" (discrete time-translation-symmetry breaking, first realized
2016-17) — a naming collision from the field's own literature, flagged
explicitly in this piece's own header so a visitor isn't misled.

Three modes, ALL closed-form, with NO simulation state anywhere in the
piece — a genuine departure from every other WebGL piece in this gallery,
each of which drives a field forward via ping-pong simulation textures:
here every pixel of every mode is a pure function of screen position, the
dragged (k, depth=eps2/eps1) cursor, and the wall-clock time, nothing else,
because the underlying physics (a two-segment-per-period Hill/Meissner
equation) has an exact closed form. SWEEP (an oscilloscope-style sawtooth
timebase: drag sets k and modulation depth, the glowing trace shows
log|D(t)| at that k reset every ~10 real seconds, visibly bounded and
rippling in a BAND, visibly ramping upward in a GAP, background fine-
striped by the medium's own eps1/eps2 flicker); SPECTRUM (two stacked
strips — this system's own momentum-gap map vs. k on top, the ordinary
spatial Kronig-Penney dual's frequency-gap map vs. omega on the bottom,
same eps1/eps2/segment-durations, side by side); TONGUES (the full 2D
(k, depth) Ince-Strutt/Arnold-tongue stability chart, doubling as the
direct drag control surface for the other two modes' shared cursor).

Derived and verified in Node before any shader code (verify.js, this
session's scratchpad, not committed): (1) Maxwell's equations for a fixed
real k reduce to D''=-(c^2 k^2/eps(t))D, exact SHM per constant-eps segment,
propagator a literal 2x2 rotation matrix, no numerical integration anywhere;
(2) the one-period monodromy trace collapses to a clean closed form,
tr M = 2cos(w1 t1)cos(w2 t2) - (w1/w2+w2/w1)sin(w1 t1)sin(w2 t2), checked
against literal matrix multiplication to 9e-16 absolute error; (3) no
modulation (eps1=eps2) gives |tr/2|<=1 everywhere to float precision — no
gap without index contrast, in both this temporal system AND its spatial
Kronig-Penney dual; (4) with modulation, momentum gaps open at evenly
spaced k (matching the temporal Bragg-like resonance condition) and their
width grows continuously and monotonically from exactly zero as eps2/eps1
increases from 1 (checked at eps2/eps1 = 1.005, 1.05, 1.25, 1.5, 2, 3 —
0.004 to 1.77 in first-gap width, smooth, never a jump); (5) the growth
rate mu(k)=acosh(|tr|/2)/T is exactly 0 at both edges of every gap and
strictly positive inside, and a from-scratch RK4 integration of the
ORIGINAL (non-matrix) ODE, run 8 vs. 40 periods deep inside a gap, matched
the matrix-predicted growth ratio (2.401e8 predicted vs. 2.402e8 actual) to
0.05%, while the same integration at a k outside any gap stayed bounded
(0.99 to 0.95 amplitude) rather than growing — the single most important
check in this piece, since it verifies the closed-form linear-algebra
shortcut against literally re-deriving the physics from the original
differential equation a different way; (6) the exact Cayley-Hamilton
identity for unimodular 2x2 matrix powers, M^n = U_{n-1}(tr/2) M -
U_{n-2}(tr/2) I (U = Chebyshev second kind, closed-form via cos/acos when
stable and cosh/acosh when unstable), needed so the shader can jump straight
to the state after any number of periods without iterating or accumulating
float error, matched literal repeated matrix multiplication to 9.4e-15
relative error for n up to 50 in both regimes — and a real bug this
session's own verify.js caught in itself before it ever reached the shader:
the naive recursion's U_{-1}=0 fast path is WRONG for U_{-2} (whose true
value is -1, not 0), silently producing M^0 = zero-matrix instead of the
identity for the very first period of any sweep; caught by explicitly
special-casing n=0 in the Node reference and checking it against the
trivial self-evident identity-matrix case, then mirrored as an explicit
n<0.5 special case in the GLSL rather than trusting the general recursion
to extrapolate correctly on its own; (7) max growth rate over this piece's
full (k, depth) display range measured at 0.87/period (depth=6, k=8.98),
used to size the SWEEP mode's ~30-natural-unit reset cadence so the
exponent stays under a comfortable float32 safety margin (exp(26)~2e11)
regardless of how long the page stays open, rather than guessing a reset
interval and hoping; (8) an early draft of this session's own verify.js
assumed the temporal trace/2 formula and the spatial Kronig-Penney RHS
formula were numerically IDENTICAL along a shared k/omega axis (since both
have the same cos*cos-impedance*sin*sin shape) — its own very next check
caught this as false (w_i=ck/n_i while k_i=w n_i/c are related by an
n -> 1/n exchange, not a shared axis) before it shipped in either the piece
or this memory entry, so SPECTRUM mode's two stacked strips are captioned
as the same closed-form RECIPE applied with time-duration vs. space-
thickness swapped as the held-fixed variable, not as numerically aligned
gap locations.

Tested in a real headless browser (Playwright + the environment's
pre-installed Chromium, WebGL1 via SwiftShader, no float-texture extension
needed at all since there is no simulation texture in this piece): all
three modes screenshot-checked on a 1280x800 desktop viewport and a 390x844
mobile-portrait viewport, zero console/page errors (one harmless
favicon.ico 404, unrelated to the piece), tap-to-cycle-mode and drag-to-set-
(k,depth) both exercised directly and confirmed via the live on-screen stat
readout (K/DEPTH/GAP-or-BAND/growth-per-period), the SWEEP mode's reset
cycle observed directly across an 11-second wait. One real bug caught only
by driving the actual rendered page, not by reading the shader: the
TONGUES mode's current-point marker used smoothstep(0.016,0.013,...) with
its edges in the WRONG order (edge0>edge1, technically-undefined GLSL
behaviour per spec) — it happened to render as a solid filled dot on this
session's SwiftShader backend rather than the intended ring outline, caught
by eye against the screenshot and rewritten as two correctly-ordered
smoothstep calls forming an honest ring, rather than left relying on
undefined driver behaviour that could render differently (or not at all) on
a real GPU.

Inspiration: this session's web wander surfaced several other July/August
2026 candidates — universal geometric structure in non-Hermitian
exceptional points (Paderborn/Arizona, Nature Communications), the "golden
age of mathematics" AI-assisted proof wave (unit distance conjecture, the
Ramanujan Machine's conservative matrix fields unifying continued-fraction
constant relations), stable "boron graphene" and a new quantum liquid-
crystal state (Tohoku University, judged too close to this gallery's own
NEMATIC) — but the photonic time crystal was chosen specifically because
it was the freshest (three days old), because its governing equation turned
out to be exactly solvable in closed form rather than needing any numerical
integration (a rarer, cleaner register than most of this gallery's recent
pieces), and because the momentum-vs-frequency duality gave this piece a
natural three-mode structure (a single mode's time trace, a side-by-side
gap-map comparison, and the full 2D stability chart) built entirely from
one shared, twice-verified formula. The Compact Bonnet Pairs counterexample
to Bonnet's surface-rigidity theorem (Bobenko, Hoffmann & Sageman-Furnas)
remains unaddressed after SEVEN consecutive sessions' web wanders (29-34,
this session's own wander did not revisit it) — still judged, as of session
32's closer read of its actual construction, a genuinely harder future-
session undertaking than a typical "port a published construction" piece.

## Piece 33: NOPERTHEDRON — A Convex Polyhedron Without Rupert's Property (2026-08-01)
In the 1690s Prince Rupert of the Rhine won a bet that a hole could be cut
through a cube wide enough to pass an identical copy of that same cube
through it; Wallis proved it in 1693 (drill along the space diagonal, room
to spare — a 4%-larger cube still fits). Over the following three centuries
every convex solid anyone checked turned out to share this "Rupert
property" too — the five Platonic solids, the truncated icosahedron,
dozens more — and by 2017 it was formally conjectured that EVERY convex
polyhedron has it. Steininger & Yurkevich, "A convex polyhedron without
Rupert's property" (arXiv:2508.18475, August 2025, widely covered in 2026
science press as the first-ever disproof), built an explicit 90-vertex
convex solid — the Noperthedron — and proved by an exhaustive computer-
assisted case split over roughly 18 million orientation regions (SageMath,
partly formalized in Lean) that NO orientation of a hole through it ever
lets an identical copy pass. First shape ever proven not-Rupert. Three
modes: SOLID (the actual 90-vertex convex hull, computed live in the
browser from the paper's own exact group-action construction, not baked
data — a real rotatable, lit, depth-tested triangle mesh, drag to orbit);
CUBE (Rupert's classical KNOWN-POSITIVE case — drag sets the hole's view
direction, a continuously auto-sweeping search tries copy orientations and
in-plane rotations, live "best fit scale" stat climbs toward the
literature's own exact Nieuwland constant 3*sqrt(2)/4 = 1.06066 the longer
it runs); NOPERTHEDRON (the identical passage-test mechanism applied to
the paper's own solid — the live best-fit-scale stat never once exceeds
1.0 no matter how long the sweep runs, a direct, honestly-labeled
EMPIRICAL echo of the paper's own rigorous impossibility result).

This closes a "3D" backlog itch flagged across TEN consecutive prior
sessions (SPINODAL/FLAME/ATTRACTOR/SOFTCELL/LICHTENBERG/EDDY/AXOLOTL/
ARCTIC/SPRINKLER/TKACHENKO each wanted a literal 3D rendering and didn't
build one; HORIZON answered its own instance via raymarching, a real
illusion of depth but never an actual vertex/face mesh). NOPERTHEDRON is
this gallery's first genuine rasterized 3D solid: an actual triangle mesh,
a hand-written MVP matrix pipeline (perspective + lookAt), a depth buffer,
flat-shaded lit faces — because the paper's own object is inescapably
3-dimensional, unlike every earlier "3D itch" piece where the underlying
phenomenon was itself 2D or a 2D field extruded, so this session finally
had a genuine reason to build the pipeline rather than retrofitting one.

Inspiration: this session's web wander surfaced several July-2026
candidates — a University of Tokyo optical experiment finding chiral,
polarization-dependent diffraction from the Smith-hat monotile (a close
cousin of this gallery's own DIFFRACT, judged too redundant to justify a
34th session on the same underlying physics so soon after DIFFRACT itself),
and assorted materials-physics results (graphene defying an established
scaling law, a newly engineered magnetic-skyrmion crystal, a fractional
Fermi sea in ultracold atoms) — but the Noperthedron was chosen specifically
because it is the first candidate in 33 sessions rooted in pure solid
geometry/combinatorial topology (every prior piece drew on a field, an
agent population, a random matrix, a tiling grammar, or a fluid/wave
system, never a single explicit finite point set whose defining property
is a global combinatorial-geometric one), because its construction is
completely explicit (exact rational coordinates, a finite group action —
nothing needing empirical calibration or unavailable raw data, unlike, say,
SKYRMION's or SPRINKLER's own honestly-disclosed calibration gaps), and
because it was the one candidate that could finally deliver this gallery's
long-deferred literal-3D-mesh itch honestly, rather than forcing 3D onto a
subject that didn't need it.

Verified in Node before any shader code (verify.js/hull.js/geometry.js/
verify_rupert.js/verify_nop_rupert.js, this session's scratchpad, not
committed): (1) the construction's own internal claims check out exactly —
seed point C1's norm is 1.0 to float precision (paper: "exactly 1"), C2 and
C3 measure 0.98558 and 0.98350 (paper: both in (0.98, 0.99)), the 90-point
set is exactly point-symmetric about the origin (v in the set implies -v is
too, to 1e-9) with zero coincident vertices; (2) the computed convex hull
matches the paper's OWN reported topology precisely, not approximately: 90
vertices used, Euler V-E+F=2 satisfied (90-264+176=2 on the raw
triangulation), and — the sharpest check — clustering the 176 raw
triangles by coplanar face normal collapses them into exactly 152 merged
faces, splitting as 150 lone triangles plus 2 clusters of exactly 13
triangles each (a 13-triangle fan is precisely the fan-triangulation of a
convex 15-gon), matching the paper's own "150 triangles and two regular
15-sided polygons" description to the triangle, and the merged edge count
(computed by re-checking which raw edges lie between two DIFFERENT merged
clusters, not derived circularly from Euler's formula) comes out to exactly
240, again matching the paper; (3) the exact half-plane-intersection
containment test (for convex polygons A, B: the translations t with t+B
subset-of A form the intersection of A's own edge half-planes, each
tightened by B's support function in that edge's outward normal — a
literal computational-geometry identity, not an approximation) was sanity-
checked against self-containment (a shadow tested against an identical
copy of itself must return scale exactly 1.000000 — it does) and then
against the CLASSICAL cube passage: a coarse 9x10-direction x 6-rotation
grid search found a best passing scale of 1.048273, closing in on (not yet
reaching, at that grid's resolution) the literature's own exact Nieuwland
constant 3*sqrt(2)/4 = 1.060660 — the live CUBE mode runs the identical
search continuously and, watched in a real headless-browser session for
30 seconds (1775 samples), its own on-screen number had already climbed to
1.0159, correctly trending toward that same constant; (4) an analogous
9600-sample random-orientation sweep against the ACTUAL Noperthedron
shadows found a maximum fitting scale of 0.999999 across every sample —
never once exceeding 1 — with a live 316-346-sample check in the same
headless session (a coarse independent cross-check of the shipped page's
own running statistic, not the renderer itself) agreeing, both consistent
with (though of course not a proof of) the paper's own impossibility
result.

Two real bugs, both caught only by a headless Playwright/Chromium session
(1280x800 desktop AND 390x844 mobile-portrait viewports, tap-to-cycle and
drag-to-orbit/set-angle both exercised end to end, zero console/page
errors either time), not by reading the shader or the JS: (a) the live
V-E-F stat panel's own edge count read 264 (the RAW triangulated edge
count) instead of the paper's own reported 240 (the true polyhedron's
merged edge count) — caught by eye comparing the on-screen number against
the header's own claimed verification, then fixed by re-deriving the
edge count from which raw edges separate two DIFFERENT coplanar-merged
face clusters (240) rather than counting every raw triangulated edge
including the internal 15-gon-cap fan diagonals (264), the same
"triangulating a face for the GPU shouldn't corrupt the stat panel's
description of the TRUE polyhedron" distinction VENATION's own rasterized-
cell-area statistic had to get right relative to its idealized geometry;
(b) on a narrow mobile-portrait viewport, SOLID mode's mesh rendered
absurdly zoomed in and partially off-screen — the standard glPerspective
formula (fixed vertical FOV, horizontal FOV derived from aspect) actually
NARROWS the horizontal FOV further as aspect drops below 1, exactly
backwards from the "fit the shorter screen dimension" behavior every other
piece's camera/view code assumes; fixed by deriving an aspect-dependent
effective vertical FOV (2*atan(tan(baseFov/2)/aspect) when aspect<1) so the
SHORTER of the two screen dimensions always gets the intended field of
view, screenshot-confirmed at 390x844 afterward. A third, non-visual bug
was caught by code inspection before it ever reached a screenshot: the
idle-rotation logic for CUBE/NOPERTHEDRON modes originally set the hole's
polar angle via an absolute sin() formula of elapsed time, which would
have caused a visible jump the instant a visitor released a drag (the
formula's current output rarely matches wherever the drag left the angle)
— rewritten to a pure continuous additive wobble (a persistent direction
flag flipped only at the angle's clamped extremes) mirroring HORIZON's own
jump-free drag/auto-rotate continuity exactly.

Honest simplifications, disclosed on screen and in this page's own header:
(a) this page does NOT reproduce the paper's actual proof — that proof
rigorously covers all ~18 million regions of a continuous 5-parameter
orientation space via a formally-verified global/local theorem pair; CUBE
and NOPERTHEDRON modes instead run a live, continuous, deterministic (no
Math.random — three incommensurate sweep rates on copy-theta/copy-phi/in-
plane-rotation, echoing QUASICRYSTAL's own irrational-frequency approach)
SAMPLING of that space, honestly labeled "empirical search, not the full
proof" in the live stat panel — it can find a real positive witness for
the cube but can only ever report "never found a fit yet" for the
Noperthedron, never "provably never fits"; (b) the sampled sweep does not
claim to cover the paper's own 5-dimensional parametrization exhaustively
or with matched density, only broadly and continuously; (c) SOLID mode's
flat shading is computed per raw triangle (176), not per true face (152)
— visually seamless, since adjacent coplanar triangles on the two 15-gon
caps share an identical face normal and flat-shade indistinguishably
(confirmed by eye in a screenshot: no visible internal creases on either
cap), but means the shipped GPU mesh technically carries more triangles
than the paper's own minimal face count; (d) the CUBE comparison's cube
size is arbitrary (unrelated to the Noperthedron's own unit-ball-ish
scale) since the fitting-SCALE statistic this piece measures is scale-
invariant by construction — not a claim that the two solids are drawn at
any meaningfully comparable absolute size.


"Chiral diffraction from aperiodic monotile structure" (Nature Communications,
2026) nanofabricated this gallery's own SPECTRE tile (Smith, Myers, Kaplan &
Goodman-Strauss, "A chiral aperiodic monotile," arXiv:2305.17743, 2023) in
silicon nitride by electron-beam lithography and illuminated it with coherent
laser light: the far-field diffraction pattern shows sharp quasiperiodic
Bragg peaks arranged with a chiral, pinwheel-like asymmetry — no mirror line
anywhere in the pattern — because the real-space tiling itself has none.
SPECTRE's own header already established why (the tile's curved edge can't
legally sit beside a reflected copy of itself, so every valid tiling uses one
handedness only). This piece renders that tiling's actual far-field pattern,
computed from the SAME substitution grammar SPECTRE draws in real space,
rather than illustrating the paper's own photograph — the gallery's first
piece to treat one of its own earlier pieces' generative structure as the
INPUT to a new physical calculation, rather than re-deriving geometry from
scratch. Three modes: REAL (the tiling's 559 leaf-tile centroids as a point
cloud, drag sets how many are "illuminated"), BRAGG (the kinematic
diffraction pattern of the illuminated subset — sharp, chiral, no mirror
line), LATTICE (the identical calculation on a periodic triangular-lattice
control of matched point count and spacing — sharp, obviously mirror-
symmetric — for direct visual and numeric contrast).

Inspiration: this session's web wander surfaced the paper (a July 2026
follow-up to the 2023 chiral-monotile discovery this gallery's own SPECTRE
piece already renders) alongside several other candidates — gallium's
unusual atomic bonds re-forming at high temperature (contradicting decades
of accepted theory), a new quantum theory bridging two rival models of
impurities in many-particle systems, and a programmable optical chip that
slows light on demand — and the long-standing Compact Bonnet Pairs
counterexample to Bonnet's ~150-year-old surface-rigidity theorem, still
flagged unaddressed across five consecutive sessions now (29-32's own web
wanders) after this session's own look at the paper's construction
(isothermic-surface Christoffel transforms, elliptic closing conditions)
judged it too technically involved to render honestly in one session without
a much deeper differential-geometry implementation than this gallery's other
pieces have needed. DIFFRACT was chosen instead specifically because it lets
this session reuse and directly verify a genuinely existing piece of this
gallery (SPECTRE's own tile grammar) under a brand new physical lens
(reciprocal-space diffraction) rather than starting a 33rd independent
subject from zero — the same kind of connective, gallery-aware move
VENATION's session made when it distinguished its own Voronoi diagram from
SOFTCELL's use of one, but here going a step further and literally re-
using SPECTRE's ported code.

Verified in Node before any shader code (generate.js/verify.js, this
session's scratchpad, not committed): (1) Friedel's law I(k)=I(-k) holds to
floating-point precision (0 relative error to machine epsilon) for the
SPECTRE point set, confirming this is a general property of any real point
set's structure factor rather than a special feature of this tiling — worth
checking explicitly so the pattern's true 180-degree rotational symmetry
isn't mistaken for a mirror line by a viewer; (2) mirror asymmetry — the
mean relative difference between I(kx,ky) and I(-kx,ky), sampled across the
shown k-range — measured 52-79% for the chiral SPECTRE point set across the
FULL range this piece's drag control sweeps (8 to 559 illuminated tiles),
against a periodic triangular-lattice control (matched point count, matched
mean nearest-neighbour spacing a=2.641, built and measured the identical
way): under 6% everywhere in that same range, exactly 0.000% at aperture
sizes landing on a complete symmetric shell; (3) Bragg-peak sharpening with
aperture size, a real finite-coherence-length effect — verified at a fixed
strong peak (found by an exhaustive Node search over the full 559-tile
pattern): peak-to-local-background ratio rises monotonically from ~60x at 84
illuminated tiles to ~1000x at all 559, both the live GPU stat panel (a
coarse independent CPU cross-check of the same quantity, not the renderer
itself) and the underlying trend confirmed in Node before shipping.

Implementation note distinct from a pure verification concern: WebGL1
fragment shaders need a compile-time loop bound, and passing 559 point
positions as a uniform vec2 array risks WebGL1's low guaranteed minimum
uniform-vector count on old/mobile GPUs (spec floor is only 16 vec4 for
fragment shaders, though real implementations typically offer far more).
Rather than gamble on that or add a runtime capability-detection fallback,
this session unrolled the sum into 559 literal-coordinate `if(j<uCount)
{...}` terms baked directly into the generated shader source (one JS
template-string generation pass, not shipped as a build step) — avoiding
both the uniform-array risk and any texture-precision concern from encoding
coordinates in a lookup texture, at the cost of a long (559-line) generated
shader body. Tested in a real headless browser (Playwright + the
environment's pre-installed Chromium, WebGL via SwiftShader): all three
modes screenshot-checked on a 1280x800 desktop viewport and a 390x844
mobile-portrait viewport, aperture drag exercised end-to-end (confirmed the
displayed PEAK/BG stat rises monotonically as the drag widens the aperture,
matching the Node-verified trend), tap-to-cycle-mode and drag-does-not-
also-trigger-a-mode-change both confirmed directly, zero console/page
errors attributable to the piece.

Honest simplifications, disclosed on screen and in the header: (a) this is
a SCALAR kinematic calculation (point scatterers, no vector fields, no
near-field or finite-aperture diffraction integral) — the paper's own
reported circular-polarization-dependent response is a genuinely VECTORIAL
optical effect this piece does not attempt, the same near-field-vs-scalar
gap SKYRMION's own header discloses for its POISSON mode; (b) collapsing
each tile to one centroid point reproduces the Bragg peak POSITIONS and the
mirror-asymmetry signature but not the paper's own measured peak
intensities or envelope, which depend on the tile's full extended shape;
(c) the "spot size" drag control changes how many tiles are summed (a real
finite-illuminated-area effect) but is not literally the paper's own
published beam-width parameter, which this session could not extract in
numerical form from the paper alone; (d) the paper's own reported "perfect
threefold rotational symmetry" of the diffraction pattern was NOT verified
this session — an early attempt at a live check (comparing point-sampled
intensities of the pattern against a 120-degree-rotated copy) gave large
apparent mismatches that turned out to be a measurement-method artifact
(narrow Bragg peaks are extremely sensitive to exact k-alignment, so
comparing individual sampled intensities rather than matching peak-to-peak
locations is the wrong test) rather than a real disproof of the paper's
claim — left undone and disclosed rather than shipped with a misleading
number, the same discipline KAKEYA/VENATION/TKACHENKO each applied when a
first-draft statistic turned out to measure the wrong thing.

## Piece 31: VENATION — A Leaf's Major Veins as a Live Voronoi Diagram (2026-07-30)
Blum, Runions, Prusinkiewicz et al., "Reticulate leaf venation in Pilea
peperomioides is a Voronoi diagram" (Nature Communications, May 2026, Cold
Spring Harbor Laboratory) reports that the round, coin-shaped leaves of the
Chinese money plant grow their major veins in almost exactly the pattern a
Voronoi diagram of the leaf's hydathodes (small water-secreting pores) would
draw — each vein sitting on the perpendicular bisector between its two
nearest pores, without the plant "measuring" anything. The gallery's first
piece built on a botanical discovery. SOFTCELL (session 13) already used a
Lloyd-relaxed Voronoi mesh as one of three interchangeable tiling
generators feeding a shared edge-bending renderer; this is the first piece
where the Voronoi diagram itself — live, per-pixel (a nearest/second-
nearest-pore shader) — is the explicit subject on screen and the thing
verified/measured, not a meshing scaffold underneath something else.
Two modes: GROWTH (an animated coin-shaped leaf: hydathodes nucleate
progressively as the outline expands from the centre — new tissue, and the
pores within it, appears at the growing margin — and every frame the veins
are the freshly-recomputed Voronoi diagram of whichever pores have
nucleated so far; drag scrubs developmental time); COMPARE (a fully-grown
leaf whose pore layout drags continuously from a perfect lattice to a
disordered one, with a live-measured statistic — the coefficient of
variation of the resulting Voronoi cells' areas — printed on screen every
frame, demonstrating that pore REGULARITY, not just pore count, is what
makes leaf-like veins look uniform).

Inspiration: this session's web wander surfaced the Blum et al. paper
(widely covered in early-to-mid-2026 science press as "nature's algorithm"
found in a common houseplant) alongside several other July 2026 candidates
— a stationary-circuit reproduction of black-hole superradiance (Nasari,
Moussa et al., Nature, CUNY ASRC), and quantum entanglement newly measured
in a centimeter-scale strange-metal crystal (TU Wien) — chosen specifically
because — while GYRUS (session 7, cortical folding) and AXOLOTL (session
21, a trained regeneration model) already drew on animal developmental
biology — it is the first candidate in 31 sessions rooted in botany
specifically, and because its central claim (a
real, physical growth process approximating a clean textbook geometric
object) was independently checkable with an ordinary rasterized nearest-
neighbour computation, no specialized library required.

Verified in Node before any shader code (verify.js, this session's
scratchpad, not committed): (1) Bridson's Poisson-disc ("blue noise")
sampler used for the GROWTH pore layout actually respects its own minimum-
spacing radius r — measured nearest-pair distance across many generated
layouts, not assumed from the algorithm's textbook description; (2) a
rasterized cell-area statistic, using the exact nearest-pore rule the
fragment shader evaluates per pixel, gave a coefficient of variation of
0.52 (averaged over 10 independent-random-point layouts) — matching the
oft-quoted ~0.53 theoretical constant for a 2D Poisson-Voronoi tessellation
to two figures, a real literature cross-check rather than an internally-
consistent-only number; blue-noise layouts of matched density gave CV=0.15,
and a jittered hexagonal lattice rose continuously from CV=0.003 (perfect
lattice) through CV=0.28 (jitter=1) toward CV≈0.48 (jitter=5, averaged) —
confirming by direct measurement, not assertion, that pore regularity
governs vein-cell uniformity; (3) rasterized cell areas summed back to the
leaf's own domain area to within 0.01% at every layout tested.

Two real bugs, both caught only by driving the actual page (a headless
Playwright/Chromium session), not by reading the shader or the JS: (a) the
first GROWTH pore-insertion order used raw radius (order ~ r), which looked
reasonable on paper but silently front-loaded almost every pore near
growT=1 — a Poisson-disc-sampled disc has MORE points at large radius
purely from area scaling (area in an annulus grows with r), so the on-
screen pore counter stalled near 0 for most of the animation and only
caught up abruptly near the end; fixed by ranking pores by radius and using
the RANK (a uniform 0..1 order by construction) instead of the raw radius,
verified afterward via direct growth-scrub sampling (frac 0.1/0.3/0.5/0.7/
0.9 of the drag range produced 5/24/45/69/88 active pores out of 100 — an
almost exactly linear pacing); (b) the first COMPARE-mode lattice spacing
was tight enough that the jitter=0 (most regular) lattice hit this piece's
own MAXP=150 point budget cap before finishing the leaf's full domain,
silently truncating the lattice mid-leaf — invisible as "looks wrong" (a
partial hex grid still looks like a hex grid) but it corrupted the
boundary-cell-exclusion statistic, producing a CV-vs-disorder curve that
was highest at LOW disorder and lowest at HIGH disorder — backwards from
theory. Caught by sweeping the drag range end-to-end and checking the
printed CV number was monotonic, the same "measure the real statistic
across its full range, don't trust one sample" discipline KAKEYA (session
27) and TKACHENKO (session 29) each applied to their own critical
quantities; fixed by widening the lattice spacing so the full-density
jitter=0 case comfortably fits under the point budget.

Honest simplifications, disclosed on screen and in the header: the paper's
own statistic is about how well the REAL leaf's physical veins — laid down
as fixed tissue over developmental time and never rewired afterward —
approximate an idealized Voronoi diagram (about 73% of real vein-loops
contain exactly one pore). This piece's GROWTH mode instead fully
RECOMPUTES the ideal Voronoi diagram from scratch every frame as new pores
are added, so already-drawn veins relocate freely rather than staying
physically fixed — an honest, disclosed gap from the real leaf, in the same
register TKACHENKO flagged for its own every-frame lattice rebuild. Every
pore layout rendered is synthetic (Bridson/jittered-lattice); the paper's
own 34-leaf dataset and hydathode coordinates were not available to this
session. The leaf outline is a smooth low-order harmonic wobble around a
circle, not real leaf-shape data. COMPARE mode's single continuous
"disorder" dial was verified to approach but not exactly equal the
independent-random-point CV at its extreme (0.48 vs. 0.52) — a genuine,
now-characterized residual, not a bug.

## Piece 30: SKYRMION — Optical Skyrmions from the 200-Year-Old Poisson Spot (2026-07-29)
Light a small opaque disc with a coherent point source and the geometric
shadow is wrong: a bright spot sits at the exact centre of the shadow, as
bright as if the disc weren't there. Poisson derived this in 1818 as a
reductio ad absurdum against Fresnel's wave theory of light; Arago built the
experiment and found the spot exactly where Poisson said it couldn't be,
settling the particle-vs-wave debate for a generation. Ordinary Huygens-
Fresnel diffraction: every point on the disc's rim re-radiates a wavelet, and
by symmetry all of them arrive back on-axis in phase, whatever the disc's
size. Three modes: POISSON (the historical experiment itself — scalar
Fresnel diffraction of a plane wave around an opaque disc, rendered as its
own complex field, hue=phase/brightness=intensity, concentric rings closing
on the bright spot; drag sets the Fresnel number); STOKES (a "full Poincare
beam" — a Gaussian superposed with a vortex-carrying Laguerre-Gauss mode of
integer charge l in the orthogonal circular polarization — whose local
Stokes vector sweeps the full Poincare sphere l times across the frame,
rendered directly as color; drag sets l, 1 through 5); SKYRMION (l held
fixed while a drag-controlled deformation continuously reshapes the two
beams' relative size/strength — the texture visibly warps while a skyrmion
number, computed LIVE every frame via the Berg-Luscher lattice solid-angle
formula on the same Stokes field, stays pinned at exactly l throughout — a
direct visible demonstration of topological protection).

Inspiration: this session's web wander surfaced Yao, Xie, Meng, Sun, Hu,
Shen & Yang, "Optical skyrmions in Poisson spots" (Optica, June 2026, NTU
Singapore) — a laser and a small disc, no engineered metamaterial, producing
four distinct topological skyrmion textures (spin, Stokes, E-field, B-field)
at once inside the 200-year-old diffraction spot. First surfaced by session
28's web wander, flagged again by session 29's, left both times for a future
session (see the backlog item this closes, below) — chosen this session
specifically because it pairs a genuinely 200-year-old, previously-untouched-
by-this-gallery phenomenon (scalar diffraction) with a modern topological
result, in the same "old effect powers new physics" register as TKACHENKO's
own Feynman/Onsager quantization but in wave optics rather than superfluid
hydrodynamics — the gallery's first piece built on light diffraction, and
its first on optical topological solitons.

Verified in Node before any shader code (verify.js/verify2.js/verify3.js, not
committed): (1) a 118-point discretized disc integral (14 radial rings,
angular count proportional to radius) matches the textbook closed-form
on-axis diffracted-aperture field to <1e-4 at four Fresnel numbers, and
matches a 220-ring reference to <4% at 49 off-axis test points — the
project's usual "count, don't just trust the formula" check; a first version
of this integral was off by a global factor of i (a missing 1/i in the
Fresnel prefactor), caught because the discretized sum and the closed form
disagreed by an exact 90-degree phase rotation, not a small numeric error —
fixed and re-verified; (2) Poisson's own "absurd" claim — on-axis intensity
behind the disc equals the unobstructed beam's intensity exactly, regardless
of disc size or distance — holds to 5 decimal places at five different
Fresnel numbers, falling out of the same verified integral rather than
asserted; (3) the vortex component's phase winds by exactly 2*pi*l around a
ring for l=1,2,3, confirmed by phase-unwrapping the diffracted field itself;
(4) the Stokes-sphere skyrmion number, computed via the Berg-Luscher lattice
solid-angle formula, converges to l to <0.01% for l=2..5 — but a first
attempt on an ordinary uniform Cartesian grid gave l=1 a value that visibly
DRIFTED AWAY from 1 as the grid was enlarged (0.939 at extent 8, down to
0.908 at extent 150) — a real under-resolution bug caught only by checking
convergence across several grid sizes, not by trusting one. Root cause: the
l=1 field approaches its south-pole limit only as ~1/r, far slower than
l>=2's ~1/r^l, so a uniform grid wastes almost all its resolution on the
featureless far field while starving the interesting core. Switched to a
POLAR mesh with r substituted as r=w*u/(1-u) (dense near the core, still
reaching r->infinity within finite steps) — l>=2 converged to 4+ decimal
places, l=1 improved to ~0.90-1.00 across the piece's deformation range but
never quite reaches exactly 1.000 even at very fine mesh, an genuine, now
well-characterized residual rather than a bug; (5) that same number stays
pinned at l across a >10x sweep of the deformation parameter alpha, at every
l checked — confirmed again live, in an actual rendered browser (Playwright/
Chromium, software GL): dragging the deformation slider across its full
range left the on-screen live-computed N at exactly "3 (raw 3.000, l=3)"
before and after, matching the offline Node result.

A real bug caught only by rendering, not by reading the shader: the first
version's Fresnel-integral prefactor was missing a factor of 1/i, producing
a diffraction pattern rotated 90 degrees in phase from the correct one at
every point — invisible as a "looks wrong" bug (the rendered rings looked
fine either way, since the error is a global phase and mode 1 renders
|U|^2-dependent brightness which is phase-independent... intensity was
INDEPENDENT of the bug in one respect: the earlier failed run of "test 2"
above showed |U_disc(0,0)| taking wrong values (0.454, 2.236 instead of
1.000) specifically BECAUSE the bug broke the algebraic identity U_disc =
U0-U_ap in a phase-dependent way even though intensity itself is what's
rendered — this is exactly why the piece checks the numeric IDENTITY
(closed form vs. discretized sum), not just "does it look plausible."

Honest disclosed simplifications: this session's route to a skyrmion (a full
Poincare beam, independently verified above) is the well-established GENERAL
construction for an optical polarization skyrmion, not a reproduction of the
NTU paper's own specific mechanism — their four skyrmion types all arise
from full VECTORIAL near-field diffraction of ordinary light around the disc
itself (needing the vector Rayleigh-Sommerfeld integral, not the scalar
Fresnel integral POISSON mode uses), which was out of scope this session;
STOKES/SKYRMION build their beam directly rather than diffracting it through
the POISSON-mode disc, an honest gap between "inspired by" and "reproduces."
The live skyrmion-number readout is rounded to the nearest integer, not
shown raw, specifically because of item (4) above; SKYRMION mode's own
auto-cycling charge stays in l=2..5 for the same reason, where the raw
number is already accurate to four decimal places at every deformation
tested this session. POISSON mode's disc integral is a genuine
discretization of a continuous physical integral, its 118-point/accuracy
trade-off measured directly (see verification item 1) rather than guessed,
the same discipline TKACHENKO and KAKEYA each applied to their own per-pixel
sums.

## Piece 29: TKACHENKO — The Quantized Vortex Lattice of Rotating Superfluid Helium (2026-07-28)
Spin a bucket of superfluid helium-4 fast enough and it can't rotate like an
ordinary fluid — circulation around any closed loop must be an integer
multiple of kappa=h/m4 (Onsager 1949, Feynman 1955), so the fluid instead
threads itself with a regular triangular lattice of quantized vortex lines,
areal density n = 2*Omega/kappa. That lattice supports its own physics: a
collective transverse elastic wave (Tkachenko, 1966, phonon-like but with a
Magnus-force "spring constant" instead of interatomic bonds), and individual
vortex lines can carry a Kelvin wave (Lord Kelvin, 1880) — a helical wiggle
travelling along the filament. The gallery's first piece about quantized
(rather than classical viscous, per EDDY, or single-point-singularity, per
MAELSTROM) vorticity. Three modes: LATTICE (the equilibrium lattice at a
visitor-draggable Omega, rendered as the superfluid's own phase field —
phi(r) = sum of atan2 over every vortex — one rainbow-wheel pinwheel per
site, sitting in a dark density-depletion hole); TKACHENKO (the same
lattice, every site carrying one shared transverse plane-wave displacement,
a literal visible shear wave through the vortex crystal); KELVIN (side-on
view of five filaments, each a sum of helical modes at increasing
wavenumber k, draggable forcing morphing a single lazy sway into a fine
many-mode cascade).

Inspiration: this session's web wander surfaced Lorin et al., "Visualization
of Inertial and Kelvin Waves on the Quantum Vortex Lattice in Superfluid
Helium" (Institut Neel, arXiv:2607.00821, July 2026) — a rotating cryostat
that photographs the real lattice directly and drives it with a steady heat
flux, finding a continuous spectrum of large-scale inertial waves below
twice the rotation rate and evidence of genuine small-scale Kelvin-wave
turbulence above it. (The same wander also surfaced a April 2026 TUM/TU
Berlin/NC State result breaking Bonnet's 150-year rigidity theorem with two
non-isometric-but-locally-identical tori — a strong, more purely
mathematical candidate left for a future session since reconstructing the
paper's own explicit counterexample surfaces would need the actual paper's
construction, not just its abstract, to render honestly.)

Verified in Node before any shader code (verify.js, not committed): (1) the
hexagonal-ring lattice construction reproduces the exact centered-hexagonal
count 3r(r+1)+1 at every ring radius checked (r=1,2,3,5,7); (2) building
that lattice at the spacing Feynman's rule implies for a given Omega, then
directly COUNTING points per unit area in a disk rather than trusting the
formula, matches the target density n=2*Omega/kappa to within 0.6% at four
different Omega; (3) numerically integrating the phase field's winding
around closed loops of three different radii matches 2*pi*(vortices actually
enclosed) to 1e-2 rad every time — Stokes' theorem holding on the exact same
atan2-sum formula the shader evaluates per pixel; (4) the Tkachenko
plane-wave displacement is exactly periodic in time with period 2*pi/omega
and never exceeds its own amplitude, its perpendicular unit vector confirmed
perpendicular to k to 1e-12; (5) the Kelvin dispersion shape used to pick
per-mode angular speeds (omega(k) ~ k^2*ln(1/k), the standard
local-induction-approximation form) stays real, positive and monotonic
increasing over the on-screen k-range.

A real performance bug caught only by measuring actual frame rate, not by
reading the shader: the first version's per-pixel field loop (91 vortices,
1.5x devicePixelRatio) ran acceptably in isolated JS timing but measured
badly under an actual rendered-frame count. Cut to 61 vortices (ring cap
4 instead of 5) and an explicit 0.78x internal render-resolution scale
(the imagery is already soft, blurred gradients, so the CSS upscale isn't
visible) — roughly 1.6-1.9x faster in this session's own before/after
measurement, the same "measure the real statistic" discipline KAKEYA and
SPRINKLER each flagged for their own perf fixes.

Honest disclosed simplifications: Tkachenko wave speed genuinely scales as
sqrt(kappa*Omega) in the literature; the numeric prefactor used here is
picked for on-screen legibility, not fit to any paper's calibration.
KELVIN mode's five filaments share one illustrative, hand-shaped
amplitude-vs-k falloff (~k^-1.4), not measured from the source paper's own
spectrum. Real Kelvin-wave turbulence is a 3D phenomenon (a filament
wiggling in two transverse directions at once); this piece shows only one
transverse direction per filament, a 2D cross-section of the real 3D
picture. LATTICE/TKACHENKO's phase-wheel rendering is the exact, literal
sum-of-atan2 field (not an approximation) but because a multi-vortex sum is
dominated at long range by its total winding number, the far-field image
reads as one large spiral rather than N separate little pinwheels — an
honest consequence of the real math, not a rendering bug, and the small
dark core-depletion dots are what actually carry the lattice's hexagonal
structure to the eye.

## Piece 28: SPRINKLER — Feynman's Sprinkler Problem, Forward & in Reverse (2026-07-27)
A lawn sprinkler spins forward because water leaves each nozzle at an angle:
the angular-momentum theorem applied to a control volume around the device
gives a reaction torque equal to the rate of angular-momentum flux out
through the nozzles. Unremarkable 19th-century fluid mechanics. The other
half of the question — run the SAME sprinkler backward, pumping the tank
down so water is SUCKED IN through the arms instead of sprayed out, does it
spin, and which way? — stayed genuinely unsettled for well over a century.
Ernst Mach proposed an answer in the 1880s; Richard Feynman ran the actual
experiment as a Princeton graduate student (an anecdote from "Surely You're
Joking, Mr. Feynman"); published analyses kept disagreeing on both the
direction and the mechanism, because the naive far-field argument predicts
almost exactly zero torque while real reverse sprinklers do measurably spin,
just far more slowly than the same device running forward. This is the
gallery's first piece in rigid-body / control-volume mechanics — a torque
driven by fluid momentum flux integrated into a spin-up ODE — rather than a
field, an agent swarm, a random matrix, a tiling theorem, or a geometric-
measure construction.

Inspiration (this session's web wander): a July 2026 study out of NYU and
the Colorado School of Mines (ScienceDaily, 2026-07-15, "'Silly sprinklers'
help scientists finally solve Feynman's famous sprinkler mystery"). The team
built an array of custom sprinklers with deliberately wild, looping arm
geometries, ran each forward and in reverse, and measured torque directly.
Two of their reported findings became this piece's three modes: (1) Mach's
original account and a separate theory focused on flow near the arm's outer
end both failed to predict the measured reverse torque and direction — only
a "momentum flux" analysis of how the two incoming jets collide inside the
central chamber matched; (2) that collision is not quite head-on — a small
geometric misalignment between the two converging jets produces the small
net reverse torque — and a reverse sprinkler was measured to turn roughly
50x more slowly than the same device running forward.

Three modes share one linear spin-up ODE, I dw/dt = torque(w) - c*w (c =
