in plain JS first" discipline, reused here for a fifth time): RK4-integrated
the ODE and confirmed (1) the capture/escape boundary lands within 0.3rs of
b_crit and near-critical rays' minimum radius converges on exactly 1.5rs;
(2) large-b deflection matches the weak-field formula δφ≈2rs/b (the doubled
Newtonian light-bending Einstein's 1919 eclipse expedition actually measured);
(3) the LOCAL per-pixel initial-condition formula the shader uses —
v₀=du/dφ=-u₀cot(α), α the ray's angle off the outward-radial direction at
the camera, no "start at infinity" approximation — reproduces the identical
b_crit threshold when swept against camera angle directly (bh_sim4.js).

### Per pixel: orbital-plane basis, RK4, three outcomes
eR/ePhi span the plane containing camPos and each pixel's own ray direction
(a DIFFERENT plane per pixel, not shared) — RK4-integrate (u,du/dφ) forward
in φ, a fixed 110-step budget over up to 4π of winding. Three outcomes: u
reaches 1 (r<rs, captured — black); r exceeds 40rs (escaped — sample a
procedural starfield in the direction the bent ray asymptotically points,
approximated as the initial radial direction rotated by the total swept φ);
or the path crosses the equatorial plane within the accretion disk's annulus
(3rs–9rs, 3rs being the actual Schwarzschild ISCO, not a tuned number),
depositing emission with a hot-white→ember colour ramp and a directional-
beaming tint. A ray can cross that plane more than once (capped at 2 images)
— a photon passing close behind the hole crosses once in front and once on
the far side before escaping, which is the actual mechanism that paints a
second, lensed disk image arcing above/below the shadow.

### Three real bugs, each caught by looking at the rendered image, not the
math (the math was already verified offline and was right every time)
1. **Wrong sign on the initial radial velocity.** The very first offline
   orbit-equation test (bh_sim.js/bh_sim2.js) showed EVERY ray "numerically
   blowing up" to negative u within one step, at every impact parameter
   including ones that should obviously escape untouched. Root cause: took
   the sqrt's negative branch (v₀=-sqrt(disc)) for an "incoming" ray by
   assumption, without checking which branch actually corresponds to u
   INCREASING (r decreasing) as φ increases in this parametrization — it
   was the positive branch. Fixed in bh_sim3.js; the corrected sign
   immediately reproduced the textbook photon-sphere minimum radius (1.5rs)
   and b_crit to 4 decimal places, confirming the ODE itself had been right
   all along.
2. **Disk light aliased into a large, ugly, geometric-looking sawtooth/comb
   pattern around the shadow.** Looked enough like a real bug (too regular
   to be noise, too large to be anti-aliasing) that it needed active
   debugging rather than just re-tuning brightness: a temporary shader
   build that output crossCount and usedSteps directly as colour (see
   "debug via direct value visualization" below) showed the teeth were
   crossCount's own boundary, and re-running the SAME scene at a much finer
   global DPHI shrank the teeth in lockstep with step size — ruling out a
   geometry bug and confirming genuine ANGULAR UNDER-SAMPLING of exactly
   where a ray's y crosses zero. Fixed with local bisection (6 iterations,
   re-integrating from the last known-good RK4 state) exactly at each
   detected sign change, giving sub-DPHI/64 precision only where it's
   needed instead of paying for it on every step of every ray — the
   boundary came out perfectly smooth at a fraction of the cost a globally
   finer step would have needed.
3. **The "sky" was smeared with disk light almost everywhere, not just near
   the shadow.** Isolating diskAccum alone (via a debug build returning
   only that term) showed it WAS the entire halo, not a separate bug —
   turned out the camera's FOV was simply too wide relative to the disk's
   angular size at that distance: even edge-of-frame rays had a geometric
   impact parameter smaller than the disk's own outer radius, so nearly
   the whole frame legitimately intersected the disk plane somewhere along
   its (possibly bent) path. Fixed by choosing camera distance and FOV
   together so the disk's outer edge stays within the frame, not by
   touching the crossing logic at all.

### A fourth trap that wasn't a bug: PHOTON RING mode rendering solid black
At r=2.2rs with a 71.6° FOV, EVERY pixel came back captured. A debug build
colouring captured=red confirmed it wasn't a boundary artifact — the whole
frame really was inside the capture cone. bh_check_fov.js worked out why:
the capture-cone half-angle (measured from "pointing straight at the BH")
grows to a full 90° AT the photon sphere itself (half the entire sky is
already gone there, not just very close to it) and was already ~51.6° at
r=2.2rs — comfortably wider than half this mode's own FOV. Not a bug to fix
so much as a composition to choose deliberately: pulled the camera back to
2.6rs and widened the FOV to 2.3rad (132°) so a visible escaping fringe
remains. The same growing-cone fact broke INFALL's fixed FOV even harder
partway through the fall (full blackout well before reaching the clamped
minimum radius), which needed a structural fix, not just a parameter
tweak — see below.

### Equidistant fisheye, not a tan(halfFov) pinhole
A standard planar perspective projection diverges as half-FOV approaches
90°, which INFALL needs to exceed near the end of its fall (per the capture-
cone math above). Replaced the camera ray construction with an equidistant-
angle mapping (ray angle from centre = screen radius × halfFov, linear,
no tan) that stays well-defined up to a full 360°. INFALL's own FOV now
ramps from 0.85rad to 3.0rad over the fall (t^1.7 easing) specifically so a
shrinking bright ring of the outside universe stays inside frame right up
to the clamped closest approach (1.65rs) instead of the screen going solid
black a third of the way through the mode.

### Octahedral starfield mapping produced streaked "star" artifacts
An initial procedural starfield (unit direction → octahedral 2D square →
flat hash grid, avoiding any cubemap texture asset) rendered most stars as
thick diagonal SMEARS rather than round points — caught by screenshot, not
by re-deriving the projection math, which looked correct in isolation. Root
cause: any star cell straddling the octahedral map's fold seams gets a
locally discontinuous/stretched mapping. Replaced entirely with ~70 fixed
pseudo-random points scattered directly ON the unit sphere (hashed index →
uniform sphere point via the standard z/phi construction) tested per pixel
via a sharp pow(dot(dir,starDir),K) spike — no 2D projection of the sphere
at all, so there is no seam to smear anything into. Simpler AND correct.

### Debug technique: visualize intermediate values directly as colour
Every bug above was root-caused the same way: temporarily replace the
shader's final gl_FragColor with a raw intermediate quantity (captured as
solid red, usedSteps or crossCount as a grayscale ramp, diskAccum or base
alone with the other zeroed) rather than staring at the final composited
image and guessing. This is the GPU-shader version of FLAME's own
gl.readPixels sanity check and EINSTEIN/SPECTRE's plain-JS geometry ports —
verify what the code is actually computing, not what the final blended
picture merely looks like it might be computing.

### Performance: sample budget over resolution
Every pixel costs up to 110 RK4 steps (4 derivative evaluations each), plus
up to 6 bisection sub-integrations at actual disk crossings, times 2
supersamples for antialiasing at the photon-ring/disk-image boundaries —
genuinely expensive per pixel compared to every earlier piece's fixed-size
texture pass. Internal raster capped at 660px on the long edge (browser
bilinear-upscaled to the display size, the same "trade resolution for
per-pixel cost" choice as a lower SIM resolution elsewhere in this gallery)
rather than a separate offscreen-framebuffer+blit pass, since this piece
needs no other render target anyway. Measured at under 2fps on headless
SwiftShader (software rasterization, deliberately not representative of
real GPU throughput) after cutting an initial 4x2x2 supersample down to 2
diagonal samples and MAXSTEPS from 140 to 110 — real hardware should clear
this by 1–2 orders of magnitude given SwiftShader's usual gap versus true
parallel silicon, but genuinely UNVERIFIED on real GPU hardware this
session, unlike every other performance claim here which was measured.

### Five views, one spacetime
- ACCRETION: inclined view (73°) at r=34rs, disk visibly wrapped above/
  below the shadow by lensing, slow orbital drift.
- PHOTON RING: r=2.6rs, 132° fisheye FOV — the sky itself gets smeared into
  thin wrapped bands right at the shadow's edge.
- EINSTEIN RING: a single fixed point light at world direction (0,0,1),
  tested directly against each pixel's bent escape direction (not folded
  into the procedural starfield) so the ring/arc images are legible as the
  camera's auto-rotating azimuth sweeps past alignment — confirmed visually
  forming a near-complete ring, not just theorized.
- INFALL: camera radius shrinks from 26rs to a clamped 1.65rs
  (r(t)=26-(26-1.65)t^1.6, accelerating like a real plunge), FOV ramping
  wide alongside it (see above) — the live dτ/dt readout visibly heads
  toward zero as the fall progresses.
- TOP-DOWN: near-polar view straight down the disk's axis — primary ring
  plus a fainter secondary ring from photons that looped underneath and
  back up, both visible around one shadow.
All five run the identical ODE and shader; only camera geometry and disk/
star brightness differ per mode — ATTRACTOR/FLAME/SPINODAL's own "one
equation, several honest registers" choice, reused a fourth time.

### Live readout: gravitational time dilation, not a fixed constant
dτ/dt=√(1-rs/r): a stationary observer's clock at the camera's own current
radius, measured against a distant one. Provably →0 as r→rs — the real
phenomenon measured as a number rather than only implied by the visuals,
this gallery's now-standard bar since SANDPILE's own session-8 note (same
lineage as SPINODAL's α, FLAME's box-dimension, ATTRACTOR's λ, ISING's M,
LICHTENBERG's D, GYRUS's GI). Most dramatic during INFALL, where r is
actually changing frame to frame and the number visibly falls toward zero.

### Interaction
Drag/touch to orbit the camera directly — this piece has no field or
population to perturb, only a viewpoint, so the natural touch is flying
around the hole rather than stamping or reseeding anything. Auto-rotation
(mode-specific azimuth speed) pauses while dragging and resumes from
wherever the drag left off; theta (inclination) is drag-adjustable too,
clamped to (6°,174°) to avoid the degenerate poles.

### What's honestly NOT simulated
Pure Schwarzschild: non-rotating, uncharged, exactly spherically symmetric.
No frame-dragging (needs the Kerr metric), no true relativistic Doppler
colour-shift of the disk material (only a brightness-beaming approximation
using a Keplerian tangent direction), no photon-photon or photon-disk
backreaction, no volumetric disk (razor-thin equatorial plane only).

### Inspiration
Web research into "unusual physics simulation / creative coding shader
ideas" surfaced gravitational-lensing black-hole raytracers (Antonelli's
and oseiskar's own writeups) as a genuinely different register from
everything else here — first raymarched/camera piece, first relativity,
direct answer to the standing "3D" backlog itch (SPINODAL/FLAME/ATTRACTOR/
SOFTCELL/LICHTENBERG all flagged a 3D mode as never-built for performance
reasons) via a technique naturally suited to it: a per-pixel geodesic
integration is a sparse, cheap-per-ray computation rather than a dense
volumetric field, much like a strange attractor or DBM growth is a sparse
curve/surface rather than a solid.

---

## Piece 17: SPINODAL — Phase Separation & Self-Similar Coarsening (2026-07-14)
Cahn & Hilliard (1958) wrote down the equation for a quenched binary alloy
that separates into two phases WITHOUT nucleating over an energy barrier —
inside the spinodal region of the phase diagram, a homogeneous mixture is
already unstable to infinitesimal fluctuations and decomposes everywhere
at once. Lifshitz & Slyozov (1961) then showed the resulting domains keep
coarsening forever afterward, growing as a power law L(t) ~ t^(1/3),
through the same curvature-driven bulk diffusion as Ostwald ripening.
Genuinely different register from this gallery's other reaction-diffusion
pieces (MEMBRANE's Gray-Scott, EXCITABLE's FitzHugh-Nagumo): both of those
are non-conserved activator/inhibitor systems relaxing toward a pattern;
Cahn-Hilliard conserves its order parameter exactly (matter only moves by
being transported, never created or destroyed pointwise), which makes the
governing PDE fourth-order in space rather than second — every earlier PDE
in this gallery, including LICHTENBERG's Laplace solve, has been strictly
second-order until now.

### Architecture: two ordinary Laplacian passes standing in for one
dc/dt = M*lap(mu), mu = c^3 - c - kappa*lap(c) — factored into two 5-point-
stencil shader passes (mu-pass, then update-pass reading mu) rather than
one shader computing a biharmonic operator directly, so no fragment shader
needs a wide double-Laplacian stencil. Same ping-pong-texture technique as
every earlier GPU simulation here, chained through one extra intermediate
buffer (a dedicated mu texture, recomputed whole every substep, no
ping-pong of its own needed).

### Two real bugs, neither of them the numerics
Explicit-Euler stability for a biharmonic term was verified offline first
(ch_sim.js, FLAME's own "simulate in plain JS before writing shader code"
discipline): dt*M*|8-64*kappa| <= 2 or the Nyquist mode blows up in
dozens of frames. That bound held throughout — what actually went wrong
was GPU-only. First: an early SIM=384 grid rendered as a flat,
textureless field forever. WebGL1 requires power-of-two textures for
REPEAT wrapping and 384 isn't one, so the toroidal composition texture was
silently "incomplete" and every sample read back zero — fixed by keeping
SIM strictly power-of-two (landed on 256, see below). Second, after that
fix (still at SIM=512): the pattern visibly got FINER over the first ~15
seconds instead of coarser, domain size dropping every frame, reading
exactly like the runaway blowup the stability bound warns about. It
wasn't one. Cross-checking against the SAME plain-JS reference at
matching grid size (not the smaller 96x96/128x128 grid the original
ch_sim.js run had verified stability on) reproduced the identical
trajectory — this was the genuine linear spinodal growth phase, still
short of saturation, and a denser grid just packs more repeats of the
same physical wavelength into view. The real fix wasn't numerical at all:
tune kappa up (a wider, more legible characteristic wavelength) and the
grid down to SIM=256 (verified this doesn't change the physics, only how
many wavelengths fit on screen), with LINEAR filtering — gated behind its
own OES_texture_float_linear extension check, a THIRD bug caught in the
same pass, since LINEAR-sampling a float texture is a distinct capability
from merely having float textures via OES_texture_float, and requesting
it without checking silently zeroed every read — standing in for the
resolution NEAREST would otherwise have cost the coarser grid.

### Five modes, one PDE
- CRITICAL: symmetric quench (c0=0), the textbook bicontinuous labyrinth.
- OFF-CRITICAL: c0=0.25, still inside the spinodal region (|c0|<1/sqrt3)
  so it needs no nucleation barrier either, but the asymmetric composition
  produces minority-phase droplets in a majority background instead of an
  interconnected labyrinth — same instability, different morphology.
- FINE QUENCH: smaller kappa (1 vs 3), shorter characteristic wavelength,
  a deliberately denser/finer pattern as a direct visual contrast to
  CRITICAL at the same grid.
- STIRRED: a fixed analytic cellular flow field advects the conserved
  field on top of its own diffusion ("stirred Cahn-Hilliard," cf. Berti
  et al.), stretching domains along the flow cells into filaments instead
  of letting them relax to round blobs.
- ARRESTED: mobility M cut to 0.12; same instability, same eventual fate,
  just glacially slow — reads as a faint, barely-evolving texture next to
  the other four's visible motion.

### Live readout: domain size and the Lifshitz-Slyozov exponent
L ~ area / total-variation, a threshold-free proxy for interface length
(sum of |c - c_right| + |c - c_down| over the readback grid) — deliberately
NOT a c=0 sign-crossing count, which would miss most of the boundary in
OFF-CRITICAL's asymmetric composition. Alpha is the least-squares slope of
log(L) vs log(simTime) since the last quench, LICHTENBERG's own log-log
box-counting technique aimed at a different exponent here. This is the
gallery's now-standard answer to SANDPILE's session-8 flag that a power
law "implicit in the visuals" should be measured, not just implied — same
lineage as LICHTENBERG's fractal dimension, ISING's magnetization,
ATTRACTOR's Lyapunov exponent, GYRUS's GI, and FLAME's own box-dimension.

### Interaction
Click/tap stamps a small noisy patch at the pointer, reset to near-zero
composition — a spot instantly re-melted and re-quenched, free to
decompose on its own from scratch while the rest of the field keeps
coarsening from wherever it already was.

---

## Piece 16: FLAME — Iterated Function Systems & Structural Colour (2026-07-13)
Barnsley's "chaos game" (Fractals Everywhere, 1988) and Draves' fractal
flame algorithm (his own renderer dates to 1992; the algorithm itself is
documented in Draves & Reckase, "The Fractal Flame Algorithm," flam3
project, c.2003). Picks up a genuinely different register from ATTRACTOR
(session 12): that piece iterates ONE fixed map per mode with zero coupling
between points; this piece's chaos game randomly picks among several
affine maps EVERY step, and follows each with one of Draves' nonlinear
"variation" functions (sinusoidal, swirl, horseshoe, polar, heart, disc,
spiral, julia) rather than staying purely linear. The two other defining
ideas from Draves' paper — log-density tone mapping instead of linear/
binary, and colouring each sample by which xform produced it rather than
by density alone ("structural colour") — are both implemented directly.

### Architecture: PHYSARUM's own technique, repurposed for a literal random walk
65536 chaos-game points live in a ping-ponged float texture (position.xy,
running colour-coordinate.z). A fragment shader advances every point one
step per frame: hash the point's own texcoord+frame into a weighted xform
choice, apply that xform's affine map then its variation, and blend the
colour coordinate halfway toward the chosen xform's own colour (Draves'
own update rule, c' = (c+c_xform)/2). Updated positions are then splat as
gl.POINTS with additive blending into a second float texture that decays a
little every frame — a leaky integrator standing in for "run millions of
samples before rendering," but live and continuously self-normalizing
instead of one finished long-exposure frame. Same vertex-texture-fetch +
point-splat-with-blending combination PHYSARUM introduced for pheromone
deposits, applied here to a random walk instead of a sensed one.

### A real bug caught only by looking at the actual image, not the console
The very first render showed particles frozen at 3 isolated points instead
of playing the chaos game at all. Root cause: the per-particle random
choice was `hash(uv, uFrame)`, and `uFrame` increments by exactly `1.0`
every frame — in exact arithmetic `fract(A + integer) === fract(A)`
IDENTICALLY, so the "random" seed never actually varied per particle, ever.
Every particle deterministically picked the same xform forever, so each
converged to that xform's own fixed point instead of hopping between all
of them — the nested-nearly-static-squares pattern this produces (each
particle tracing its own single map's contraction toward one point) looked
enough like real structure to be non-obvious from a single screenshot; it
only gave itself away by SIERPINSKI mode failing to form a triangle at
all. Fixed inside the shared hash() itself — `seed = mod(seed,9973.0) *
0.61803398875` before use — rather than at each of the ~6 call sites, which
also folds in ISING's own documented "seed grows unboundedly over a long
session, float32 precision degrades" fix for free, since the mod now
bounds every seed before it's ever used regardless of how large uFrame
gets over a long-running page.

### Burn-in, borrowed directly from LICHTENBERG's preRelax()
Every point starts at a uniform-random position — nowhere near any
attractor. Depositing from frame zero splats that transient convergence
trail across the whole domain, and it decays slowly enough (favoring a
living, breathing accumulation over a fast-reset one) to stay visible for
many seconds. Fixed exactly the way LICHTENBERG fixed an analogous
problem: 50 chaos-game steps run synchronously, off-screen, at every
mode-entry, before the render loop deposits a single point.

### Xform design verified offline before touching the shader
Three of five curated xform sets, tuned by intuition alone, produced
degenerate results only visible once rendered: SWIRL BLOOM collapsed to a
sub-pixel blur (swirl alone is magnitude-preserving — a pure rotation by
angle r², so with small translations every xform's own fixed point sits on
top of the others near the origin); HORSESHOE SPIRAL piled ~13% of every
sample run into a single histogram bin (all three xforms had near-zero
translation, so absent the spiral branch's own 1/r singularity there was
nothing to see); JULIA LACE converged to a mathematically perfect but
visually inert 1D circle (two xforms that are both pure similarities —
rotation + uniform scale — preserve angle exactly, so the "lace" collapsed
to a plain ring, box-dimension measured at 1.02). All three traced back to
the same root cause (fixed points too close together, or too little
asymmetry to generate real 2D texture) and were diagnosed by porting the
exact chaos-game math to a standalone Node script (flame_sim.js, same
"verify computationally before writing canvas code" discipline EINSTEIN
and SPECTRE used for their own grammar/matrix math) rather than iterating
blindly through the browser: spreading each xform's fixed point onto its
own ring fixed SWIRL BLOOM and HORSESHOE SPIRAL (SWIRL BLOOM's fix reused
directly for HORSESHOE SPIRAL once the same diagnosis applied); adding
non-uniform scale and shear to break the conformal symmetry turned JULIA
LACE's circle into genuine woven fractal texture (offline-measured box-
dimension 1.5, matching what the shipped shader itself later measured).
HORSESHOE SPIRAL's spiral/horseshoe variations also needed their division-
by-r floor raised from 1e-6 to 0.12, and the divergence-reseed bound raised
from 6.0 to 25.0 — the tiny epsilon let points landing near the true origin
blow up to r~30 and get treated as "escaped" and randomly reseeded, which
replaced the variation's own real spike-and-return behaviour (a classic
flame's feathery "spike" lines, an accepted feature of these variations,
not a runaway) with constant teleport noise flooding the frame.

### Five registers, one algorithm
- SIERPINSKI: every xform's variation forced to LINEAR — the flame
  algorithm's own nonlinear step switched off, which collapses it back to
  exactly Barnsley's original 1988 chaos-game IFS. Live box-dimension
  reads 1.60, matching the textbook Sierpinski value (log 3 / log 2 ≈
  1.585) almost exactly — the one mode this session could check against a
  known closed-form answer, and it did.
- SWIRL BLOOM: four-fold swirl variation, translations spread onto their
  own ring (see traps above) — reads as a genuine four-lobed pinwheel
  bloom, D≈1.7.
- HORSESHOE SPIRAL: horseshoe + spiral + sinusoidal, D≈1.9-2.0 — this
  session's most honest result: the spiral variation's own 1/r singularity
  keeps this parameter set chaotically mixing rather than settling into a
  low-dimensional fractal, a legitimate contrast against SIERPINSKI's
  clean textbook value rather than a flaw to keep chasing.
- JULIA LACE: two julia-variation xforms with shear-broken symmetry, D≈1.5
  — genuine interleaved woven-lace texture, not the plain circle the first
  (pure-similarity) attempt produced.
- HEART DISC BLOOM: heart + disc + sinusoidal, Draves' own warm-palette
  register (this session's closest visual match to a "classic" flame
  render) — four xforms, D≈1.9.
Structural colouring (hue driven by the running colour-coordinate, not
density) is visible directly as colour gradients ACROSS each fractal's own
branches — most legible in SIERPINSKI, where the three xforms' colours
(0.12/0.50/0.88) paint each recursive copy of the triangle in a
blue-violet-magenta gradient that visibly repeats at every scale.

### Live readout: box-counting dimension of the histogram itself
Reuses LICHTENBERG's exact box-counting technique (threshold occupancy,
cover with several box sizes, fit log-count vs log(1/size)), applied here
to this piece's OWN accumulated visit-density histogram rather than a
grown dielectric tree — distinct from ATTRACTOR's live Lyapunov exponent,
which measures orbit divergence rather than the settled pattern's fractal
geometry. Also tracks a ratcheting-up/slow-decaying observed peak density
as the log tone-map's self-normalizing exposure, the same "normalize
against the current peak, not a fixed guess" fix LICHTENBERG's phiMax
already established for this gallery.

### Interaction and verification
Click/touch bursts ~10% of the population to the pointer position; because
those points are deposited every frame WITHOUT their own burn-in (deliber­
ately, unlike mode-entry), the accumulation buffer records their multi-
frame convergence trail directly — a fading sequence of progressively
smaller, progressively more numerous nested blobs tracing the IFS's own
recursive construction outward from the click point before dissolving back
into the attractor. Verified all 5 modes (screenshot + a direct
gl.readPixels sanity check of particle radii, not just visual inspection,
after the SWIRL BLOOM / HORSESHOE SPIRAL / JULIA LACE traps above),
14-15fps under headless SwiftShader, zero console errors beyond the usual
benign favicon 404, the click-burst-and-reconverge interaction on desktop
mouse and a 390x844 mobile portrait viewport with real touch events, and
clean letterboxed aspect handling (the accumulation histogram is a square
buffer independent of window shape, sampled with an explicit
contain-and-letterbox transform rather than stretched).

### Inspiration
Web research into 2026 generative-art/creative-coding surveys turned up
nothing pointing past what this gallery already does in spirit; pivoting
to "what's a genuinely different ALGORITHM, not just a different subject"
landed on fractal flames specifically because Draves' own three
innovations (nonlinear variations, log-density histogram, structural
colour) are each a clean, checkable departure from ATTRACTOR's single-map
orbit — probabilistic multi-map selection instead of one fixed map,
accumulated-histogram tone-mapping instead of raw trajectory density, and
colour-by-construction-path instead of colour-by-age/velocity.

---

## Piece 15: SPECTRE — The Chiral Aperiodic Monotile (2026-07-12)
Smith, Myers, Kaplan & Goodman-Strauss, "A chiral aperiodic monotile,"
arXiv:2305.17743, May 2023 — the same team's own follow-up to the "hat"
tile EINSTEIN renders elsewhere in this gallery, and the paper's own
STRONGER result, chosen this session specifically to complete the
"spectre" backlog item EINSTEIN flagged honestly as left undone
("its edge geometry curves two of the hat's straight edges into a
matching S shape, a genuinely different tile boundary... needs its own
construction"). Where the hat's aperiodic tilings are only valid because
reflected copies are mixed in with unreflected ones (EINSTEIN's own live
MIRRORED readout measures that forced ~12.7%), bending the tile's edges
into a matching asymmetric curve makes a reflected copy physically unable
to edge-match an unreflected neighbour AT ALL — "strictly chiral," a
genuinely stronger and more elegant closure of the same question than
"weakly chiral, so exclude reflections by convention."

### Ported from a citable reference implementation, not re-derived
The tile's 14-vertex polygon, the 9-category substitution grammar (an
8-slot child-placement/rotation table plus a 9x8 child-category lookup),
and the alternating-direction bezier edge template are ported from
kerupani129s' own spectre-monotile-js (github.com/kerupani129s/
spectre-monotile-js, MIT License, condensed attribution kept in-file) —
the same "port a citable published algorithm rather than re-derive 2023
research geometry from scratch" choice QUASICRYSTAL, ISING, LICHTENBERG
and EINSTEIN all made for their own source models. One honest divergence
flagged in-file: this reference curves all 14 edges via one shared
alternating template rather than the paper's own hand-picked 2 special
edges — a legitimate alternative construction of the same combinatorial
grammar, kept as the reference implements it rather than re-derived from
the paper's figures, the same kind of flagged approximation SOFTCELL's
cusp heuristic and GYRUS's 2D self-avoidance already model for this
gallery.

### Two facts verified computationally before any canvas code was written
1. Handedness census. A standalone Node port of the reference's own
   matrix/grammar math (no DOM available outside a browser, so a small
   compatible 2D-affine class was written and checked against the
   reference's own documented semantics) walked the grammar and found
   every leaf tile's accumulated placement matrix carries the SAME
   determinant sign at a given recursion depth — always, with no
   per-branch exceptions across any of the 9 root categories tested
   (depth 1/3/5: all-negative; depth 2/4: all-positive; one shared
   FLIPPING factor applies uniformly per substitution level, so depth
   parity alone decides the sign for literally every leaf). Handedness is
   UNANIMOUS, never split — this piece's live readout reports the real
   per-build census (leaves.filter(det<0).length), not a hardcoded 0%,
   and would say MIXED if it ever found otherwise.
2. Edge self-mirror check. Sampling the straight LINE edge template
   against its own mirror image gives exactly zero difference (a line
   segment is trivially its own reflection, max|orig-mirrored|=0.0 over
   11 sample points); sampling the curved BEZIER template (control points
   at y=+0.5) against ITS mirror (control points negated to y=-0.5) gives
   a real nonzero difference (max diff 0.75, a full half-edge-length
   bulge flip). This is the actual geometric mechanism behind "strictly
   chiral": a reflected spectre's boundary genuinely cannot coincide with
   an unreflected neighbour's edge under the curved template, while
   nothing stops it under the straight one — TURTLE mode's own straight
   edges COULD in principle also tile with mixed reflections (not built
   as its own mode here, since demonstrating it would need a second,
   non-recursive periodic construction out of scope this session — see
   backlog below); SPECTRE mode's curved edges cannot, by this measured
   fact alone, independent of any rule imposed on the construction.

### The silhouette surprise: root category doesn't change the outer shape
Unlike EINSTEIN's four metatile roots (H/T/P/F), which each root the
recursion in a genuinely different overall silhouette, this grammar's
`generateKeyPoints` computes ONE shared quadrilateral per recursion level
that every one of the 9 categories' supertiles occupies identically —
switching root category changes ONLY which categories populate the
interior, never the outer boundary (verified: bounding-box width/height
match to sub-pixel precision across categories 1-8 at a fixed depth).
This ruled out "one mode per silhouette" (EINSTEIN's own approach) and
led directly to GRAMMAR mode below — the one place root/category choice
actually produces a different-LOOKING result, because it's colored BY
category rather than shaped by it.

### Five registers, one grammar
- SPECTRE: BEZIER_CURVE edges, root Delta, depth 3 (559 tiles) — the
  headline strictly-chiral tiling, uniform teal hue.
- TURTLE: LINE (straight) edges, same root/depth — a direct paired
  contrast against SPECTRE using the IDENTICAL recursive structure
  (same matrices, same category assignment), isolating edge geometry as
  the only variable; the straight-edge boundary reads visibly more
  angular/crystalline next to SPECTRE's smoother puzzle-piece curves.
- MYSTIC: BEZIER_CURVE, root Gamma (category 0, the one root whose base
  case is a genuine glued PAIR of tiles rather than a single one) — the
  paired Gamma1/Gamma2 children are rendered in the reference's own
  green/cyan accent colors, making the base-case construction detail
  visible directly rather than only narrated; confirmed via screenshot
  that mystic pairs appear as adjacent green/cyan tile couples scattered
  through an otherwise neutral field, exactly matching the construction.
- GRAMMAR: BEZIER_CURVE, root Xi, every leaf colored by its own
  categoryID via a golden-ratio hue step (9 distinct hues, LICHTENBERG's
  STORM-mode technique reused for a different purpose) — verified via a
  category histogram (Node script) that all 9 categories appear with
  meaningful counts (8 to 110 leaves each, not a degenerate 1-2-color
  result) before committing to this as a mode; makes the substitution
  grammar's own recursive internal structure visible directly, a concrete
  realization of EINSTEIN's own backlog idea ("colour tiles by which
  rule produced them"), adapted from 29 patch-rules to 9 categories.
- HEXAGON: the IDENTICAL 9-category grammar, unmodified, run on a plain
  6-vertex hexagon instead of a spectre (`useMystic:false`, matching the
  reference's own createHexagons(), which doesn't special-case category
  0 as a glued pair at all) — demonstrates directly, not just asserts,
  that the recursion is agnostic to which physical shape occupies each
  category slot; renders as a clean fractal-boundaried honeycomb patch.

### Growth-rate and depth choice
A leaf-count growth-rate script (same discipline as EINSTEIN's own
per-metatile-family test) found this grammar grows ~7.9x per level
uniformly across all 9 roots (faster than EINSTEIN's four hat-metatile
families, ~6.85x for H) — with no integer depth landing in EINSTEIN's own
~700-1200 target range (depth 2 gives 62-71, depth 3 gives 488-559, depth
4 gives 3842-4401). Depth 3 was picked as the best available compromise
(559 leaves for categories 1-8, 488 for Gamma/HEXAGON's category-0-as-leaf
variant, both a comparable order of magnitude to EINSTEIN's range even if
not an exact match) rather than forcing depth 4's ~4x-denser patch through
Canvas2D bezier fills without first checking performance headroom.

### Rendering: cached per-leaf screen matrices, not per-frame path rebuilds
Each of the 3 leaf shapes (spectre-bezier, spectre-line, hexagon) is built
into ONE shared Path2D in local tile space, once, via a faithful
transliteration of the reference's own alternating-reversed-edge
EdgeShape.generatePath logic (verified separately via a Node continuity
trace: the traced boundary visits all 14/6 actual vertices in order with
zero closure error before this logic was trusted inside the real
renderer). Per leaf, only a single combined local-to-screen DOMMatrix-
equivalent is cached once per mode-build/resize (view pan/zoom composed
with the leaf's own placement matrix); the render loop calls
ctx.setTransform with that cached matrix and fills/strokes the ONE shared
Path2D directly — no per-frame Path2D allocation or matrix recomputation,
the same "cache once per mode-build, only reveal-alpha/pulse are
time-dependent" discipline EINSTEIN, SOFTCELL and ATTRACTOR all
established for their own per-frame economy. Stroke width is compensated
by 1/pxScale before the transform so it reads as a constant screen pixel
regardless of the view's own zoom level.

### Verification
Headless Playwright/Chromium confirmed all 5 modes build with the exact
leaf counts/handedness the Node scripts predicted (559/559/488/559/496,
handedness unanimous in every case, matching the verified parity fact),
39-47fps fully revealed, zero console/page errors, correct behavior at a
390x844 mobile portrait viewport (UI wraps to a stacked readable column,
tiling fits and centers correctly), the click/touch ripple firing
correctly, and clean re-initialization (fitView + cached screen matrices
recomputed, leaf count unchanged) across a live resize mid-animation on
both desktop and phone viewports.

### Left honest, not built
The paper's own hand-picked "only 2 special edges curve, the other 12
stay straight" construction is not implemented — this piece uses the
ported reference's own "all 14 edges curve via one shared alternating
template" convention instead (flagged above and in-file). A genuine
non-recursive PERIODIC tiling by mirrored straight-edge Mystic pairs
(the actual reason "weakly chiral" matters — Tile(1,1) with straight
edges can ALSO be tiled boringly and periodically if reflections are
allowed, which the curved edge specifically forecloses) was considered
as a 6th mode but not built this session, since it needs a genuinely
different, non-substitution lattice construction rather than a parameter
change to the existing grammar — a concrete, well-scoped backlog item
below rather than an approximated stand-in.

---

## Piece 14: EINSTEIN — The First Aperiodic Monotile (2026-07-11)
Smith, Myers, Kaplan & Goodman-Strauss, "An aperiodic monotile,"
Combinatorial Theory 2024 / arXiv 2303.10798 (this session's web-research
find, again deliberately searched for a recent pure-mathematics shape
discovery rather than a physics-simulation register, following SOFTCELL's
own precedent last session). In March 2023 this team found the first
single shape — "the hat" — that tiles the infinite plane edge-to-edge but
NEVER periodically, closing the open "einstein problem" (German ein Stein,
"one stone," the field's own decades-old pun on the physicist's name) that
runs back through Penrose's 1974 two-tile aperiodic set and Wang tiles
before that. This piece renders the paper's own construction algorithm
live: a substitution/inflation system that recursively assembles four
named metatiles (H, T, P, F) into ever-larger versions of themselves,
which is how the paper actually proves the tiling exists and extends to
infinity, not a visual approximation of the result.

### Ported from a citable reference implementation, not re-derived
The metatile outlines, the hat tile's own 13-vertex polygon, and the
29-rule edge-matching table that assembles one substitution "patch" from
four same-level metatiles are ported from Craig Kaplan & David Smith's
own reference visualizer (github.com/isohedral/hatviz, BSD-3-Clause;
attribution comment with condensed license text kept in einstein.html).
These numbers ARE the published construction — porting the reference's
own encoding was the faithful choice over re-deriving 2023 research
geometry from the paper's proof from scratch this session, continuing
the same "port a citable published algorithm" approach QUASICRYSTAL,
ISING and LICHTENBERG already used for their own source models. The
algorithm was verified in a standalone Node script (checking leaf-tile
counts and bounding boxes per recursion level, and that all six
constructPatch/constructMetatiles helper functions round-trip cleanly)
before any canvas rendering code was written — the same "verify the
numerical assumption directly" discipline ISING and LICHTENBERG both
flagged as a general lesson.

### Picking a recursion depth empirically, not guessing
The four metatile families grow at very different rates per substitution
level (their own children counts differ: H starts with 4 hat tiles, T
with 1, P and F with 2 each), so a single shared "level N" produces wildly
uneven final tile counts across the four roots. The growth-rate test
script measured leaf-tile counts through level 6 (roughly 6.85x per level
for the H family) and picked each mode's own level so all four land in
the same ~700-1200 hat-tile range: H/P/F at level 3 (1156/658/770 hats),
T needs level 4 to reach the same range (1156 hats) since its metatile is
the smallest of the four. This mirrors GYRUS's own empirical calibration
of node count against gyrification index — measure the real relationship
first, don't guess a shared constant.

### The chirality fact this piece measures live
The paper proves something sharper than "an aperiodic monotile exists":
every valid hat tiling MUST include reflected (mirror-image) copies of
the tile mixed in with unreflected ones — no tiling using only
unreflected hats is possible. The reference algorithm already tracks
this for free: reflected instances are labelled H1 (vs. plain H/T/P/F)
at construction time, and this piece detects the same fact independently
by checking the determinant sign of each leaf tile's accumulated
transform (negative = reflected) — the two methods agree exactly, a
built-in cross-check. The live MIRRORED-percentage readout in the UI is
computed from this per-frame census, not a hardcoded label, continuing
GYRUS/ISING/LICHTENBERG/ATTRACTOR/SOFTCELL's "measure the real
invariant" discipline; H's own census reads 12.7% mirrored (147/1156).
Mirrored tiles are rendered in a complementary accent hue so the fact is
visible directly in the tiling, not just in the readout number.

### Five registers, one substitution engine
HEXAD/WEDGE/RHOMB/PINWHEEL root the identical recursive construction at
the H/T/P/F metatile respectively — same algorithm, four different
overall silhouettes (hexagonal, triangular, parallelogram, asymmetric
pentagon) purely from which metatile seeds the recursion, each its own
mode hue with the mirrored-tile accent overlaid. SUPERTILE instead
overlays every intermediate recursion level of the H root simultaneously
as unfilled stroked outlines colour-coded by depth, the recursive
self-similar hierarchy the other four modes only ever show the leaf
level of — this needed a second gather function (gatherOutlinesAtDepth)
returning each level's own metatile-boundary polygons rather than only
leaf hat outlines. A caught pacing mistake worth flagging: the first cut
revealed hierarchy layers coarsest-first (root outline appearing before
any fine structure), which back-loaded the mode's most informative
detail to the very end of its growth window and, worse, meant a mid-mode
screenshot during testing showed only 2-3 of the 4 levels — reversed to
reveal finest-first (mirroring the actual inflation direction: small
hat/metatile groupings appear immediately, the single unifying root
outline arrives last), verified by re-testing with a direct mode-jump
script rather than trusting the first screenshot's timing.

### Growth pacing and interaction
Since the tiling itself is a fixed combinatorial object (no forces, no
PDE, nothing to integrate), the only asked-for questions were how to
make a static structure feel alive and how to keep ~1200 flat-filled
13-gons at interactive framerate. Answered the first by ordering each
leaf tile's reveal by its distance from the tiling's own centroid and
fading tiles in over the mode's first 16s (an honest "watch the
recursion assemble" pacing choice, not a structural growth process
unlike NAUTILUS's real chambered growth), plus a continuous low-amplitude
per-tile brightness pulse once settled. Answered the second by caching
each leaf's world-space polygon points once per mode-build rather than
walking the metatile tree every frame — the combinatorial structure
never changes after a mode is built, only reveal-alpha and pulse
brightness are time-dependent, so per-frame work is a flat iteration
over a precomputed array. Click/touch drops a transient localized
brightness flash via a distance/age falloff, the same ripple-interaction
shape as SOFTCELL and CHLADNI. Measured 60fps with HEXAD's 1156 tiles
fully revealed via headless Playwright.

### Left honest, not built
The "spectre" tile (the same team's May 2023 follow-up, a chiral variant
that needs NO reflected copies at all — the still-stronger discovery)
is not implemented this session; its edge geometry curves two of the
hat's straight edges into a matching "S" shape, a genuinely different
tile boundary from the ported hat_outline that would need its own
construction rather than a parameter tweak of what's here. Flagged
honestly below rather than approximated.

---

## Piece 13: SOFTCELL — Edge-Bending & the Geometry of Seashells (2026-07-10)
Domokos, Regős & Regős, "Soft cells and the geometry of seashells," PNAS
Nexus, Sept 2024 (this session's web-research find, surfaced while
searching for genuinely new 2024-2026 mathematical-shape discoveries
rather than the usual generic "generative art trends" results): almost
nothing in nature tiles the way classical geometry tiles — hexagons,
bricks, Voronoi froth are built from flat faces meeting at sharp corners,
but nautilus shell chambers, onion skin, muscle fascicles, zebra stripes
and epithelial tissue all fill their plane with "soft cells" instead, the
authors' new geometric class proven to have a minimal irreducible corner
count (exactly TWO per cell in 2D, ZERO in 3D) reachable from any sharp
tiling by an explicit "edge-bending" deformation. This piece IS that
deformation, applied live and continuously, not a one-shot illustration
of the end state — a global softness t oscillates 0..1 forever, and every
non-cusp vertex of every tiling face is rendered as a fillet (two points
pulled in along its adjacent edges, joined by a quadratic Bezier through
the original corner) whose radius scales with t. t=0 reproduces the
plain sharp polyhedral tiling the paper starts from; t=1 approaches the
fully soft tiling it proves such tilings deform into. Nothing here is a
physical simulation (no forces, no PDE, no timestep integration) — it is
the first piece in this gallery about a geometric deformation of shape
rather than a dynamical system evolving through time, and (after GYRUS)
only the second piece built on Canvas2D vector paths rather than a WebGL
field or gl.POINTS particle swarm.

### The shared-vertex trick that makes seams free
Two faces sharing an edge must fillet that edge identically from both
sides or the tiling shows visible cracks at every seam. This session's
solution needed no explicit neighbor-agreement pass: fillet radius is a
pure function of (vertex position, adjacent edge length), and every
tiling is built through one shared mesh structure (verts[] + faces[] of
vertex indices) via a snap-to-grid vertex dedup (meshVert() rounds to a
1/20000 grid and reuses the index for near-coincident points) — so two
faces independently computing "my corner's fillet" from the same shared
vertex and the same shared edge always agree, automatically. This is
what let three structurally very different generators (analytic hex
lattice, Lloyd-relaxed Voronoi clipping, analytic nested log-spiral
chambers) all feed the identical renderer with zero special-casing.
Organic irregularity comes from a smooth deterministic domain warp
(warp(x,y,time) — trig functions of position and clock only, no random
per-vertex jitter) applied to vertex positions at DRAW time, after the
mesh is built — random per-vertex jitter was tried first in the plan and
rejected before writing any code, since two faces sampling independent
random jitter at a shared vertex would immediately crack the seam;
warping as a function of position alone sidesteps this because a shared
vertex always warps to the same place no matter which face reads it.

### Cusp selection: an honest heuristic, not a derivation
The paper's own combinatorial proof of which corners survive as cusps
is not implemented; this session's stand-in is simpler and cruder — for
each face, the two vertices FARTHEST apart (by squared distance, O(k^2)
per face, k small) are permanently marked as that face's two cusps, and
only those two are ever left unfilleted at any t. This is a genuine
approximation (flagged the same way GYRUS's 2D self-avoidance and
EXCITABLE's kernel choice are, elsewhere in this gallery), but it
reproduces the paper's actual invariant faithfully: the "CORNERS/CELL"
UI readout counts designated-cusp vertices directly from the live face
data every frame and reads exactly 2.00 always, not a hardcoded label —
same "measure the real invariant, don't just imply it" discipline as
GYRUS's live GI, ISING's magnetization, LICHTENBERG's fractal dimension,
and ATTRACTOR's Lyapunov exponent.

### Four registers through one engine
- NAUTILUS: nested logarithmic-spiral chambers built analytically
  (r(theta) = r0 * exp(b*theta), b=0.18, ~Nautilus pompilius), septa at
  fixed angular spacing (9/whorl), growing outward continuously for the
  first ~24s of each 40s mode-hold then holding. The real recursive
  chambered structure, not a pie-slice toy: since cos/sin are 2*PI-
  periodic, a chamber's INNER wall at angle theta is just the spiral
  evaluated one whorl earlier (r(theta - 2*PI)) at the SAME (x,y)
  direction — no coordinate shift needed — which is exactly what makes
  chamber k's inner wall land, via the shared-vertex dedup, precisely on
  chamber (k - chambers-per-whorl)'s outer wall: the two faces that are
  genuinely adjacent one whorl apart, discovered as a clean consequence
  of the periodicity rather than needing to be special-cased.
- TISSUE / STRIPE: both Lloyd-relaxed Voronoi froth (brute-force O(n^2)
  half-plane clipping per site against every other site's bisector, 2
  relaxation passes moving each point to its cell's centroid) — cheap
  enough at n~130-150 to not need Fortune's algorithm. STRIPE computes
  the same algorithm in an x-squished metric (points and the clip bound
  both compressed in x by 2.6, un-squished only when building final mesh
  vertices) so cells elongate into the zebra-stripe register the paper
  cites explicitly by name; TISSUE runs the identical code unsquished
  for the epithelium/muscle-fascicle register.
- COMB: a plain analytic axial-coordinate hex lattice — the crystalline
  register the paper's own soft cells are explicitly contrasted against.

### Traps hit this session
- Nautilus scale blowup: an early version grew the spiral to 4.35 whorls
  before checking the actual radius the exponential reaches — r0=0.05,
  b=0.18 at 4.35 whorls gives r~6.8, six times the intended view extent.
  Capped growth analytically instead (NAUT_THETA_CAP = ln(maxR/r0)/b for
  a chosen max radius) rather than picking a whorl count by eye.
- Septa reading as visually straight even at t=0.98: the first nautilus
  build gave each septum only 2 vertices (one long straight edge), so
  filleting could only nibble its very ends — correct per the algorithm
  but visually flat. Fix: inserted interior radial sample points along
  each septum so filleting has room to bow the middle, not just round
  the tips; one interior midpoint (a single clean bow) reads better than
  three (a faceted multi-kink) at this piece's chamber scale.
- Fixed BOUND left black gaps top/bottom on tall phone aspect ratios
  (checked at 390x844): the tiling extent was sized for a square-ish
  viewport. Fixed by recomputing BOUND from the actual canvas aspect on
  every resize (debounced 250ms) and rebuilding the current tiling —
  nautilus is exempt, being a single centered object rather than a
  plane-filling tiling, so margin around it on unusual aspects is
  expected, not a bug.
- A distant-site culling optimization in the Voronoi clipper (skip pairs
  farther than a fixed threshold) was written, then found to incorrectly
  cull legitimate neighbors under STRIPE's anisotropic squished bound
  (its threshold assumed a roughly square bound) — removed entirely
  rather than fixed, since n~150 is cheap enough (~180k clip ops) for
  brute-force O(n^2) to not need the optimization in the first place.
- Verified via headless Playwright/Chromium screenshots at desktop
  (1200x800) and phone (390x844) viewports, forcing mode transitions and
  softness extremes directly through the page's own top-level bindings
  (a classic non-module <script> exposes its let/const/function
  declarations to page.evaluate()) rather than waiting out the full 40s
  mode-hold; confirmed 60fps steady-state even fully grown, zero console
  errors, and the CORNERS/CELL readout at exactly 2.00 in every mode.
- Inspiration: web research this session specifically avoided repeating
  the "physics simulation" register nearly every prior piece uses (RD,
  CA, agent-based, wave interference, growth, sandpile, swarm, spin,
  dielectric breakdown, chaos) and searched instead for recent (2024+)
  pure-mathematics discoveries; soft cells stood out for being both
  genuinely novel (a new named geometric class, not a decades-old
  physical model like every earlier piece) and immediately visual
  (an explicit deformation between two recognizable end states).

---

## Piece 12: ATTRACTOR — Strange Attractors & Sensitive Dependence (2026-07-09)
Five classic 2D chaotic maps, each point (x,y) fed back through a fixed
nonlinear formula once per frame forever: CLIFFORD/Pickover (popularized
c.1990: x'=sin(ay)+c*cos(ax), y'=sin(bx)+d*cos(by)), DE JONG (Peter de
Jong, Computer Recreations, Sci. Am. July 1987: x'=sin(ay)-cos(bx),
y'=sin(cx)-cos(dy)), SVENSSON (Johnny Svensson's de Jong variant:
x'=d*sin(ax)-sin(by), y'=c*cos(ax)+cos(by)), IKEDA (Kensuke Ikeda, 1979 —
a real physical model of light circulating in a nonlinear-medium ring
cavity: t=0.4-6/(1+x^2+y^2), x'=1+u(x cos t - y sin t), y'=u(x sin t + y
cos t)), and HOPALONG/MARTIN (Barry Martin, Aston University, nicknamed
by A.K. Dewdney and popularized in his "Wallpaper for the Mind",
Scientific American, Sept. 1986: x'=y-sign(x)*sqrt(|bx-c|), y'=a-x).
Ruelle & Takens coined the term "strange attractor" in 1971 for exactly
this phenomenon: a bounded, fractal, non-periodic set a dissipative
system settles onto regardless of where it started, while any two
nearby starting points on it separate exponentially forever after —
order (a fixed invariant shape) and disorder (unpredictability) holding
simultaneously, the "novel and unexpected" hook this session's web
research kept surfacing independent of which specific map it looked at.

### Why this piece is architecturally different
Every earlier piece in this gallery couples its simulated units to one
another — PHYSARUM's agents share a pheromone field, SWARMALATOR's
oscillators couple all-to-all, ISING's spins flip against local
neighbors' energy, LICHTENBERG's growth cells share one solved potential
field, MEMBRANE/LENIA/EXCITABLE diffuse through a Laplacian kernel. Here
there is NO coupling of any kind: each of ~147,000 points is a fully
independent copy of the exact same formula, unaware any other point
exists — the simplest possible per-fragment update this gallery has ever
run (read only the texel's own value, no neighbor sampling, no VTF-summed
population loop). And yet, with zero communication between them, they
collectively trace out one single, intricate, self-similar structure —
the attractor's invariant measure emerges purely from the geometry of one
equation's phase space, not from any interaction. It's also the first
piece where the live measured quantity needs no GPU pixel readback at
all: ISING's M, SWARMALATOR's R, and LICHTENBERG's D all read back an
actual simulated field, but the largest Lyapunov exponent here is tracked
via a tiny separate two-point "shadow" trajectory computed entirely in
JS (the classic Benettin et al. renormalization method: a reference point
and a shadow point epsilon apart both iterate the same map every frame;
the running average of log(separation/epsilon), renormalizing the shadow
back to epsilon after every step, converges to lambda) — fully decoupled
from the ~147k points the GPU actually draws. A positive lambda is
chaos's own fingerprint, and reads positive for every mode (see the
Hopalong trap below for why that took a second attempt).

### Rendering: PHYSARUM's own technique, reused for a different quantity
Positions live in a ping-ponged float texture exactly like every other
piece, splatted via gl.POINTS (VTF read of position, PHYSARUM's exact
method) additively into a decaying accumulation buffer with the same
"persistent-structure channel + fast-decay flash channel" and the same
1-exp(-k*x) tonemap PHYSARUM established for its pheromone trails —
reused here for density of independent trajectories instead. The
accumulation buffer is recreated on resize at a capped resolution that
always matches the canvas's own aspect ratio (unlike PHYSARUM's fixed
512x512 trail buffer, which physarum's own toroidal abstract pattern
tolerates being non-uniformly stretched to fill any window — an
attractor's recognizable shape would visibly warp under the same
treatment, so this piece needed ISING's "recreate the sim buffer to match
canvas aspect on resize" approach instead of PHYSARUM's "fixed square,
stretch on draw" one).

### The bounding-box trap: don't guess where an attractor sits, measure it
Four of the five maps are trivially bounded (Clifford/de Jong/Svensson —
every output is a sum of sin/cos terms; Ikeda — a damped rotation with
spectral norm u<1), but none of them are centered at the origin or sized
to fit a comfortable view by construction, and Hopalong's boundedness
isn't obvious from the formula at all. Rather than eyeball a scale/
center per mode and iterate by screenshot (this gallery's usual fallback
for tuning traps), this session precomputed each mode's real bounding
box with a headless Node script — 1000-iteration warmup discarding the
transient, then 200k-300k iterations recording min/max x and y — and
derived cx/cy/halfw/halfh and a FIT=0.82 margin scale directly from the
measured numbers. Verified worth doing: Hopalong's box (halfw=halfh~129,
centered near (4.3,2.9)) is nothing a human would have guessed from the
formula alone, and Svensson's box is a 3:1 elongated rectangle (halfw
7.56 vs halfh 2.4) — an honest reflection of the actual attractor's
shape, not a bug, rendered as a wide flat band rather than force-fit into
a square.

### The Hopalong trap: "chaotic-looking" parameters aren't always chaotic
First build used Hopalong's own textbook "default" parameters (a=-55,
b=-1, c=-42, cited on Wikibooks). It rendered a strikingly regular
16-fold "flower of rings" pattern — and the live Lyapunov readout came
back at lambda=0.000, not a rounding artifact: re-running the same
two-trajectory calculation standalone in Node confirmed lambda really is
~1.5e-5, i.e. genuinely zero. The Hopalong/Martin map family is NOT
chaotic for every parameter choice — some points sit on quasi-periodic
invariant loops (KAM-torus-like behavior) that merely LOOK fractal-ish
from the density of many incommensurate rings, without any actual
sensitive dependence. This is a real, useful negative result (a
Wikibooks "pretty default" picked for a nice static image, not for being
inside the chaotic regime) but broke this piece's own "every mode's
lambda is measurably positive" claim, so it was swapped for a
Softology-gallery-sourced parameter set (a=7.16878197155893,
b=8.43659746693447, c=2.55983412731439) verified via the same standalone
Lyapunov check to read lambda~0.056 before being adopted — the smallest
of the five modes' exponents (weak chaos, not strong), an honest
reflection of this map family sitting close to the chaotic/quasi-periodic
boundary rather than deep in either regime. General lesson: a "looks
chaotic" render is not evidence of positive lambda — check the number, not
the picture, before trusting a parameter set found in a gallery/blog.

### The multistability trap: not every click lands in the chaotic basin
Initial interaction design reseeded the ENTIRE population into a tight
cluster at the click point (screenToWorld's inverse of the same view
transform used to render). Testing a click in IKEDA mode, off in visibly
empty dark space away from the spiral, reproducibly made the WHOLE
attractor fade to black over several seconds and never come back: a
standalone Node trace of the exact click-mapped world coordinates showed
the point converging not onto the chaotic attractor but toward a slowly-
drifting orbit far outside the attractor's own measured bounding box
(around (2.95,4.15) against a box centered near (0.68,-0.67) with
halfw~1.03) — a real, separate basin the Ikeda map is known to have
alongside its chaotic sea, not a bug in the map or the click math.
Reseeding 100% of the population there left nothing behind to keep
painting the spiral, so it decayed away with nothing replacing it. Fixed
two ways at once: (1) the position-update shader now reseeds only a
random 40% of the population per click (decided per-texel via a GPU
hash, not a JS loop), so even an unlucky click leaves most of the
population still tracing the real attractor — the piece can never go
fully dark from one click; (2) screenToWorld clamps its result to 60% of
the mode's own measured half-extent (not the full box — testing found
the outer 40% is itself a rarely-visited fringe that can border
non-chaotic territory) before handing it to the reseed. Verified via
repeated headless clicks at the same "bad" screen coordinate before and
after the fix: before, a small cluster of points froze in place for 8+
real seconds while the rest of the structure visibly decayed to near-
black; after, the same click is fully absorbed back into the spiral
within 1-2 seconds with the rest of the structure never dimming. General
lesson for any future piece whose per-point dynamics are a nonlinear map
with more than one attracting set: a "reseed the whole population
somewhere the user chose" interaction needs either a verified-safe
target region or a partial-population fallback (ideally both) — chaotic
maps are not guaranteed globally single-basin just because their MAIN
attractor is well-documented and pretty.

### 5 modes (30s hold, full reseed + cleared accumulation buffer on every
switch, all converging from the same canonical safe start (0.1,0.1) —
verified per-mode via the same bounding-box precompute, not assumed safe)
- CLIFFORD: Pickover attractor, a=-1.4,b=1.6,c=1.0,d=0.7 — woven-ribbon
  shape, lambda~0.2-0.3
- DE JONG: a=2.01,b=-2.53,c=1.61,d=-0.33 — a lacy curved "wave" shape
  with fine filamentary detail throughout, lambda~0.3-0.5
- SVENSSON: a=1.4,b=1.56,c=1.4,d=-6.56 — a wide elongated ring/torus,
  lambda~0.4-1.6 (the highest and most variable of the five)
- IKEDA: u=0.9 — a genuine optical-resonator spiral with fine internal
  banding, lambda~0.5-0.6
- HOPALONG: a=7.169,b=8.437,c=2.560 — a granular three-lobed cluster
  with visibly chaotic (not smooth) texture, lambda~0.05-0.12, the
  weakest chaos of the five (see the Hopalong trap above)

### Interaction
Click/touch reseeds 40% of the population into a tight, screen-relative-
sized cluster at the (clamped) pointer position — verified via headless
click mid-mode in both CLIFFORD (dense/saturated mode) and IKEDA (sparse/
high-contrast mode): the reseeded fraction visibly fans out and re-
threads itself back into the existing structure within roughly a second
for nearby clicks, while a click landing far from the current structure
shows a slower, honest multi-second convergent "chase" proportional to
how far it has to travel before the exponential-separation phase can
fan it across the shape — not an instant snap, and not a bug.

### Verification
Headless Playwright + SwiftShader confirmed: ~13fps (measured
side-by-side with PHYSARUM at ~12fps under the same harness — this
sandbox's SwiftShader is simply slower than whatever environment recorded
this gallery's earlier "~30fps" figures; not a piece-specific
regression), no console errors beyond the same benign favicon 404 every
page in this gallery gets, all 5 modes producing visually and numerically
distinct attractor shapes (verified via direct screenshot at full
30s-hold density, not just formula inspection) with correct scale/
centering from the bounding-box precompute (nothing clipped or off-
frame), the live Lyapunov readout positive and stable for every mode
after the Hopalong parameter fix, the click/touch partial-reseed
interaction verified safe against the worst discovered case (IKEDA's
competing basin) after the fix, a 390x844 mobile portrait viewport with
real touch events keeping the UI unclipped, and clean re-initialization
across a portrait-to-landscape resize with no leaked textures/
framebuffers and no console errors.

### Inspiration
This session's web research into GPU-accelerated strange-attractor
rendering confirmed Clifford/de Jong/Ikeda/Hopalong are all real,
well-documented, GPU-friendly chaotic maps already used elsewhere in
generative art (Ricky Reusser's Observable notebook, Paul Bourke's and
Softology's parameter galleries, a QC20/DeJong-Attractor WebGL reference)
without needing a fundamentally new rendering architecture — the novelty
here is architectural (zero interaction between simulated units, a first
for this gallery) and measurement-based (a GPU-readback-free live
Lyapunov exponent), not a new rendering technique. Chosen over the
gallery's own standing backlog items (a triangular-lattice Ising
antiferromagnet, a Brusselator reaction-diffusion variant, a true Penrose
tiling) because every one of those is a variation on an EXISTING
architectural register in this gallery (stochastic lattice, local
diffusion, closed-form wave superposition respectively), whereas
zero-interaction independent chaotic trajectories had never been shown
from this angle before.

---

## Piece 11: LICHTENBERG — Dielectric Breakdown & Fractal Discharge (2026-07-08)
The dielectric breakdown model (Niemeyer, Pietronero & Weismann, Phys.
Rev. Lett. 52, 1033, 1984): an insulator sits between a grounded
conductor and a distant charged boundary. Solve Laplace's equation for
the electric potential everywhere OUTSIDE the grounded structure; the
structure then grows by converting one adjacent insulator cell into
conductor with probability proportional to phi_i^eta, where phi_i is
that candidate cell's own local potential. Growth changes the boundary
of the very equation being solved, so potential and shape co-evolve —
a single exponent, eta, sweeps the resulting morphology from compact
(Eden-model-like, dimension near 2) through the DLA universality class
(eta=1, dimension ~1.71) to a few racing dendritic channels (eta>2,
dimension near 1) — the real Lichtenberg figures burned into wood,
etched into acrylic, and the mechanism behind lightning's own branching.
This was flagged as a backlog item three sessions running (8, 10) and
passed over each time as a "runner-up" in favor of an architecturally
fresher register; this session finally built it, per last session's own
note to "just build it next time it's on the table" rather than
re-research it again.

### Why this piece is architecturally different
Every earlier piece either diffuses a field through fixed local
neighbors (MEMBRANE/LENIA/EXCITABLE's Laplacian kernel), topples an
integer threshold (SANDPILE), flips a spin against a LOCAL energy change
(ISING), or moves an agent/node through a force law (PHYSARUM/CHLADNI/
GYRUS/SWARMALATOR) — none of them solve a genuine boundary-value
problem. Here every candidate growth cell's probability depends on a
potential field SOLVED ACROSS THE ENTIRE DOMAIN via Jacobi/Gauss-Seidel
relaxation between growth events, not a fixed local rule. It's also the
first piece where separately-seeded structures actively compete through
shared PHYSICS rather than shared geometry: because every seed's growth
draws on the same solved field, a structure that races ahead measurably
lowers the potential (and so the odds) seen by every OTHER structure's
frontier, anywhere on the lattice — a real screening effect, the reason
a dominant lightning leader suppresses its own side branches once it
breaks free (see STORM mode below). Relaxation reuses ISING's
checkerboard (red-black) sublattice update verbatim — same technique,
different equation: there it decided stochastic spin flips, here it
converges a deterministic Laplacian roughly 2x faster than a naive
same-buffer Jacobi pass.

### The convergence trap: Gauss-Seidel is too slow to solve from scratch
every frame
First build ran relaxation entirely inside the per-frame loop (5-9
sweeps/frame, ISING's own budget) starting from an all-zero potential
field on every mode reseed. Growth stalled to a near-standstill for the
first several seconds of every mode — a point-like seed sat at ~68
converted cells after a full 28s hold in the worst case. Root cause:
Gauss-Seidel error decays on a timescale of order (grid size)^2 sweeps,
not sweeps-per-se — propagating a boundary condition fully across a
~190px domain to the accuracy needed near a point seed takes on the
order of tens of thousands of sweeps, far more than any reasonable
per-frame budget affords without stalling growth for the whole visible
hold. Fixed by moving convergence OFF the render loop entirely: a
`preRelax()` burst of 3000 synchronous sweeps (6000 draw calls) runs
once, with no rendered frame in between, the instant a mode reseeds —
measured at ~6ms wall-clock even under SwiftShader software rendering,
imperceptible as a pause. Per-frame relaxation afterward only has to
track the SMALL incremental change from each growth step, not re-solve
the whole domain from scratch. General lesson for any future piece
needing a real boundary-value PDE solve (not just a local diffusion
step): convergence cost that would stall a render loop can often be
paid once, synchronously, at the moment of reseed instead — the same
"pay it once up front" idea SANDPILE/ISING already used for per-frame
relaxation-step budgets, just moved to a different point in the
lifecycle.

### The normalization trap: absolute phi^eta never escapes a point seed
Even after fixing convergence, high-eta modes (LEADER, eta=2.5) still
never left their seed: the ACTUAL local potential right next to any
small conductor is small in absolute terms purely from 2D electrostatics
(a log(r) profile that rises slowly near r=r0, regardless of how well
converged the solve is) — phi~0.02-0.09 near a point seed, and 0.02^2.5
is already on the order of 1e-5, a probability no reasonable growRate
constant recovers even though those exact cells are the STRONGEST
candidates currently available anywhere on the frontier. The fix:
normalize growth probability against the CURRENT frontier's own peak
potential (p = growRate * (phi_i/phiMax)^eta, phiMax refreshed from a
cheap CPU readback every ~24 frames — the same throttled cadence as the
fractal-dimension readout below) rather than the raw 0-1 scale, which is
dominated by an uninteresting geometric fact (distance to the fixed
outer boundary) that has nothing to do with the actual competition
between candidate cells. This is closer to the real Niemeyer-Pietronero-
Weismann normalized rule (p_i = phi_i^eta / sum_j phi_j^eta over the
frontier) than an arbitrary probability floor would have been, and
explains eta's role correctly: RELATIVE field concentration between
competing frontier cells, not absolute field strength. General lesson
for any future growth-probability-from-a-solved-field piece: check
whether the interesting physics is in the relative comparison, not the
absolute scale, before concluding a rule "doesn't work" from raw values
alone.

### The screening trap: single-pixel holes trapped forever
Compact/low-eta growth (CORONA) filled with permanent salt-and-pepper
pinholes rather than resolving to a clean rough-edged blob. Root cause:
a cell surrounded on all 4 sides by grounded (phi=0) tree cells has its
own Jacobi-averaged potential relax to EXACTLY 0 — no probabilistic path
can ever fire it, since 0^eta=0 regardless of growRate. This is a real
lattice-discretization artifact (a genuinely enclosed insulator pocket
IS a Faraday-cage interior with zero field in continuum electrostatics
too), but the discrete grid can't otherwise heal it. Fixed by folding
any cell touching the tree on 3+ of its 4 sides into the conductor
outright (deterministic, not probabilistic) — an honest resolution of a
degenerate case, not a fudge, since such a cell has no reachable field
gradient left to distinguish it from the tree it's already boxed inside.

### The runaway-fill trap: DBM has no natural stopping rule without one
Without an explicit stop condition, every mode eventually converts
almost the entire domain to conductor (CORONA reached 99%+ fill given
enough time) — measurably WRONG for a fractal growth model, whose whole
point is a SPARSE structure spanning the domain, not a solid fill with
occasional holes. Real dielectric breakdown has an obvious stopping
point that a naive simulation loop doesn't: the instant the growing
structure first touches the charged far boundary, the gap has fully
bridged, the potential difference collapses, and growth (the physical
process being modeled) is simply OVER — nothing after that moment is
still "breakdown," it's just a completed spark sitting there, the same
way a photographed Lichtenberg figure doesn't keep burning. Fixed with
an explicit `brokenDown` flag, set the instant any tree cell is found
occupying a geometric "sky" boundary cell (checked on the same
throttled ~24-frame readback as the fractal-dimension/phiMax scan), which
permanently freezes the growth pass for the rest of that mode's hold —
this single fix also resolved the "STORM's screening competition needs
a decisive moment" question for free: the FIRST leader to bridge the gap
wins and freezes every other competing leader simultaneously, exactly
matching how a real multi-leader lightning strike resolves (once one
channel completes the circuit, the driving voltage across the gap
collapses for all of them at once, not just the winner).

### Live fractal-dimension readout via box-counting
SANDPILE flagged its own power-law avalanche statistics as "implicit in
the visuals, not measured" back in session 8 — this piece closes that
exact gap for a different critical phenomenon. Standard box-counting:
cover the occupancy grid with boxes of side 2/4/8/16/32px, count how
many boxes contain at least one tree cell at each scale, fit
log(count) vs log(1/size), the slope is D. Cheap enough (a few full
passes over a <=190x190 readback array, gated behind the same
WEBGL_color_buffer_float try/catch guard ISING/SWARMALATOR already use)
to run on the same ~24-frame throttled cadence as the phiMax/breakdown
scan. Reads correctly across the eta range: CORONA (eta=0.35) ~1.8-1.9,
FRACTAL (eta=1.0) ~1.6-1.8 (close to the paper's own DLA figure of
~1.71, with early-growth box-counting bias — few boxes at the largest
scale — pulling small structures' apparent D down slightly, an honest
statistical-estimator limitation noted here rather than hidden), LEADER
(eta=2.5) ~1.2-1.3, ARC ~1.4-1.5, STORM (5 independent small trees)
~1.2-1.3 — a single number that visibly tracks eta exactly as the
underlying theory predicts, verified via direct headless screenshots at
each mode, not just trusted from the formula.

### Palette and rendering
Distinct per-mode duotone matching the physical register: CORONA's warm
ember (deep red-black to orange-white) for a compact discharge; FRACTAL's
classic amber/gold-on-near-black for the literal wood-burn Lichtenberg
look; LEADER's electric blue-white on deep indigo for sparse racing
channels; ARC's violet/magenta plasma-arc look for the point-to-plane
spark gap; STORM's per-owner hue-rotated teal base (a golden-ratio hue
step per seed id) so five independently-colored competing leaders are
visually distinguishable at a glance, the color itself carrying the
"which structure is winning" information. A cheap 4-tap neighbor-density
glow gives every branch a soft luminous halo instead of a single hard
pixel line; a decaying per-cell "just grown" flash (same channel-reuse
trick as ISING/SANDPILE's activity pulse) makes the active growing tip
visibly brighter than aged branch, fading over about a second — this
also surfaced a real bug (see below) where the decay was accidentally
being skipped entirely for already-grown cells via a stray `max()`.

### Bugs caught by screenshot, not just by "it compiles"
1. `max(flash, self.b)` in the GROW shader's owner-cell branch always
   evaluates to `self.b` (since decayed `flash = self.b*0.94 <= self.b`
   for positive values), silently pinning every grown cell's glow at
   full brightness forever instead of fading — invisible in the shader
   compile/link step, obvious the instant a 25s-hold screenshot showed a
   flat, undifferentiated solid-color blob with no visible "recent
   growth" gradient. Fixed to just use the decayed `flash`.
2. ARC mode's seed initially placed at pixel-space `py = H*0.92`
   assuming that meant "near the bottom of the visible canvas" — it
   actually rendered near the TOP, because array row index and clip-space
   Y run in opposite directions for a directly-uploaded ping-pong texture
   (row 0 of the JS `Float32Array` maps to uv.y=0, which the vertex
   shader's un-flipped `p*0.5+0.5` places at the BOTTOM of the rendered
   quad — so a LARGE row index ends up near the TOP of the screen, not
   the bottom). This collided the seed directly with the geometric "sky"
   (uv.y>0.93) boundary, causing breakdown to fire almost immediately
   with a single-digit-pixel fill — caught by a diagnostic screenshot
   showing the seed already sitting at the top of frame, not by any
   console error. Fixed by placing the seed at `H*0.08` instead. General
   lesson matching ISING's own sin-hash lesson: verify a spatial
   assumption by screenshot, don't trust which way an axis "should" run.

### 5 Modes (32s hold, full reseed per switch — eta and boundary geometry
are both structural regime changes, not continuously-interpolable
parameters, the same reasoning SANDPILE/ISING/SWARMALATOR already
established)
- CORONA:  eta=0.35, radial single seed — compact ember-glow discharge
- FRACTAL: eta=1.0,  radial single seed — the paper's own headline DLA
  universality-class result, dimension ~1.71
- LEADER:  eta=2.5,  radial single seed — sparse racing channels,
  dimension approaching 1
- ARC:     eta=1.6,  point-to-plane (grounded needle vs. a charged
  plate) — a literal spark-gap bolt reaching across the domain
- STORM:   eta=1.3,  5 simultaneous radial seeds sharing one solved
  field — demonstrates screening/competition directly; the first to
  reach the boundary wins and freezes every other leader at once

### Interaction
Click/touch plants a new grounded seed (rival spark) at the cursor,
competing with whatever already grew via the SAME shared solved field —
verified via headless click mid-growth: the rival cluster visibly grew
its own independent fractal branch and, in one run, reached the boundary
first, freezing the original (larger, longer-running) structure before
it finished — the screening effect demonstrated interactively, not just
narrated.

### Verification
Headless Playwright + SwiftShader confirmed: ~30fps, no console errors
(aside from the same benign SwiftShader/driver notices every other piece
in this gallery also emits), all 5 modes producing visually and
numerically distinct fractal morphologies tracking eta correctly via the
live box-counted D readout, the breakdown-freeze firing correctly in
every mode (verified via the FILL/D readout going static and a
`BREAKDOWN` label appearing), click/touch rival-seed competition on
