      same single-projection simplification this gallery's other
      genuinely-3D pieces (CAUSTIC, BONNET, NOPERTHEDRON) already
      disclose for their own subjects. A real volumetric/raymarched
      rendering showing the weave's threads actually crossing between
      depths, not just morphing as you drag a z-slider, was not
      attempted.
- [ ] LOOM: whether the real paper's own "optical manipulation" extends to
      writing a CHOSEN new over/under sign at a target crossing (not just
      erasing toward "plain," the only interaction this piece's LASER
      mode implements) was not confirmed either way by this session's
      read of the article -- see this session's own Piece 45 entry above.
- [ ] Still open, flagged again and not chased this session: the
      Sylvester-Gallai conjecture's reported first AI-only proof
      (LeanMind, IAS/DeepMind, Coq-verified), the matrix-multiplication
      exponent omega dropping below 2.3727 via tensor decomposition +
      deep RL, a wandering supermassive black hole exposed by tidal
      disruption 30,000 light-years from its host galaxy's center (Univ.
      of Maryland) -- all first surfaced by session 44's web wander,
      still unpursued three sessions later -- and, new this session, the
      Brown/Michigan nanoparticle superlattice stabilizing an elusive
      intermediate phase between two common metallic crystal structures.

## Piece 46: VALLEY — A Magnetic Field Picks Which Two Flavors Condense (2026-08-14)
This session's web wander ("novel physics discovery August 2026", "new
mathematics discovery 2026 geometry pattern visualization", a broader
"novel and unexpected" sweep) surfaced several candidates before settling:
UCLA's room-temperature ballistic phonon rays (already CAUSTIC, session
44), a Voronoi pattern in a money-plant leaf (already VENATION), a second
independent Bonnet-pairs-style counterexample (BONNET already covers this
territory), a Tokyo group's "Smith hat" quasicrystal diffraction
experiment (too close to this gallery's existing DIFFRACT/SPECTRE/
EINSTEIN aperiodic-monotile cluster to clear the novelty bar), a claimed
AI-found Jacobian-conjecture counterexample (interesting but a bare claim
with no reachable paper this session could verify, and math-not-physics-
visual), and the LHC's oxygen-neon collision evidence (not enough
distinctive visual mechanism beyond what COULOMB/existing pieces already
show). Settled on: on 4 August 2026, Lawrence Berkeley National
Laboratory / UC Berkeley (Ruishi Qi, Qize Li, Jiahui Nie, Ruichen Xia,
Haleem Kim, Hyungbin Lim, Jingxu Xie, Takashi Taniguchi, Kenji Watanabe,
Michael F. Crommie, Allan H. MacDonald, Feng Wang) published "Two-
component exciton condensates in an electron-hole bilayer," Nature
(2026), DOI 10.1038/s41586-026-10636-y -- the first clear evidence of a
MULTICOMPONENT exciton Bose-Einstein condensate, 60+ years after Keldysh
first proposed exciton BECs. Checked against this gallery's own memory
(grep for "exciton|condensate|BEC|valley" across every existing piece and
this file) before committing -- nothing else here touches multicomponent
solid-state BECs or TMD valley pseudospin, so this cleared the novelty
bar cleanly.

Nature itself redirected WebFetch to a login wall (the same paywall
pattern CAUSTIC and ERGOSPHERE hit in earlier sessions), but the
underlying preprint -- "Observation of two-component exciton condensates
in an excitonic insulator," arXiv:2603.15443 -- is open access, and
WebFetch reached it as raw PDF bytes. pypdf was not present in this
session's base image; installed it mid-session (along with a cffi
reinstall to fix a broken cryptography/rust binding) specifically to
extract the paper's full text rather than settle for secondary coverage
-- a first for this gallery, which has usually had to work from abstracts
and press coverage alone when the primary source was paywalled (see
LOOM's and CAUSTIC's own header notes on this exact limitation). The
extracted text supplied the paper's own EXACT mean-field Hamiltonian
(their Eq. 2-3, and a fully-worked closed-form two-flavor Ginzburg-Landau
reduction in the Methods), every constant used in their own calculation
(gc=3, gv=6 g-factors; aB=1.543nm, Ry=66.64meV from their stated
epsilon=7/me=0.6m0/mh=0.4m0; gH=8*pi*d, d=1 a.u. for the real ~2nm hBN
spacer), and their own explicit admission that two further parameters --
exchange constant gX=1 and zero-field splitting Delta=1ueV -- are
"difficult to determine for strongly interacting excitons" and were
chosen by the authors themselves to match their own measured saturation
field and phase-IIA width, not derived from first principles (their own
Hartree-Fock estimate gets the SIGN of gX wrong, which they note is a
known HF failure mode). This piece uses their exact chosen values, not a
fit of its own.

What is computed live, every frame, not looked up or precomputed: the
full four-flavor (KK, K'K', KK', K'K -- electron/hole valley combinations
locked by TMD spin-valley coupling) constrained quadratic minimization of
their own Hamiltonian, solved exactly via brute-force active-set
enumeration over all 15 nonempty subsets of 4 density variables (each a
tiny linear solve) -- a genuine constrained QP solver, not a lookup
table. This was verified twice this session: first in a Python scratch
script (not committed) implementing the paper's own reduced two-flavor
closed forms by hand, which correctly reproduced the IIA/IIB crossing
(58mT at nx=0.5e12 cm^-2, inside the paper's own quoted "several tens of
millitesla" window) but WRONGLY predicted phase I (full polarization)
never occurs at any field -- an actual algebra bug this session caught
(using 2*gH instead of the correct 2*(gH+gX) in the single-flavor
branch's Hartree coefficient) and fixed by re-deriving from the paper's
raw Eq. 2 rather than trusting a first pass; second, after porting to JS,
a full sweep across the piece's own live density range confirmed the
JS solver exactly reproduces the corrected Python numbers (26mT->68mT for
the IIA/IIB crossing and 152mT->1.07T for the IIB->I saturation, across
nx0=0.1 to 0.7e12 cm^-2 -- both trends and magnitudes matching the
paper's own reported "IIA persists to higher B with increasing nx" and
"~1T" saturation scale). A second bug surfaced only in-browser (Playwright
screenshot testing caught real pageerrors this session's static review
missed): the QP solver had no "nothing condenses" candidate, so branches
restricted to a flavor pair that becomes fully unfavorable (e.g. the IIA
branch evaluated deep in the high-field/high-density corner) returned
null instead of zero density, crashing LEVELS mode's per-frame sweep --
fixed by adding the trivial all-zero state as an explicit candidate, and
separately by recognizing that the LEVELS mode's three "candidate branch"
curves (mirroring the paper's own Fig. 3b presentation) need branch-local
constrained minimization ONLY, not full 4-flavor KKT feasibility against
excluded flavors, which is a materially different computation from the
true global ground state used everywhere else in the piece -- both are
now separate, clearly-named functions (solveQP for the true minimum,
branchValue for candidate-branch display).

What is NOT the paper's own data, disclosed in the piece's own header and
UI: the density slider sets a target zero-field density nx0, converted to
a chemical potential via the paper's own S=(mu+Delta)/(gH+gX/2) relation;
because the underlying model is grand-canonical (fixed mu, not fixed
particle number), density can drift once inside phase I as field keeps
rising -- shown honestly as a live "n(B)" readout next to the target
value rather than hidden. Only B>=0 is modeled, using the real B->-B,
K<->K' symmetry the paper's own antisymmetrized raw data already assumes.
The BKT dome readout uses the paper's own exact dilute-limit formula
(kB*T_BKT = 0.65 hbar^2/mx * nx) below nx0=0.2e12 cm^-2 where they say it
"matches well," smoothly interpolated -- disclosed as an interpolation,
not measured data -- toward their reported 1.8K ceiling near the Mott
density above that, since the real curve's sublinear shape in between was
not given in closed form. The paper's own reported ~60-degree TWIST-ANGLE
dependence (phase IIA weakens away from 60 degrees and vanishes near 0
degrees, confirmed intrinsic via two dedicated multi-region devices) is
not modeled at all -- this piece always assumes the near-60-degree
device the paper's main text focuses on. The model is strictly T=0
mean-field, matching the paper's own 10mK base measurement conditions
deep below T_BKT; no finite-temperature thermal/normal-fluid fraction is
simulated.

Three modes. LATTICE: a patch of the MoSe2/hBN/WSe2 bilayer, each
electron-hole dipole assigned to whichever flavor its own persistent
random threshold falls into given the live n1:n2:n3:n4 mix (smooth
reassignment as the mix shifts, no reshuffling flicker); every dipole
sharing a flavor pulses in EXACT lockstep -- a deliberately literal
rendering of what "condensate" means, one shared macroscopic phase --
while the two populated flavors of a two-component phase pulse
independently of each other. PHASE: the live-computed B-vs-density phase
diagram (a 220x130 grid, each cell an independent exact QP solve, cached
per resize), all three regions colored by which flavor set genuinely
minimizes the real Hamiltonian, with the Mott density and the paper's own
quoted reference field annotated. LEVELS: the three candidate branch
energies plotted against field (Fig. 3b's own logic), the true global
minimum highlighted as the lower envelope, showing the abrupt kink of the
first-order IIA->IIB jump next to the smooth, continuous entry into the
IIB->I saturation -- the paper's own two-transitions-of-different-CHARACTER
point made visually, not just stated.

- [ ] VALLEY: the 60-degree twist-angle dependence the paper reports as
      intrinsic (confirmed via two dedicated multi-region devices, not
      device-to-device noise) is not modeled at all; a fourth mode or a
      twist-angle drag axis that weakens/removes phase IIA as the angle
      moves away from 60 degrees toward 0 (where the paper says only IIB
      and I survive) would dramatize a real, verified structural
      dependence this piece currently just asserts in its header text.
- [ ] VALLEY: strictly T=0 mean-field; no finite-temperature run showing
      the condensate's own susceptibility signatures fading into a
      featureless paramagnetic state above T_BKT (the paper's own Fig. 4a,
      a full (B,T) susceptibility map) is attempted -- this piece only
      ever shows a single informational T_BKT(nx) number, never the
      actual temperature-dependent physics.
- [ ] VALLEY: the piece never visualizes WHY gX>0 favors two-component
      condensation over Hartree-Fock's own (wrong-sign) ferromagnetic
      prediction -- the paper flags this as itself an open, unresolved
      theoretical puzzle ("likely due to Hartree-Fock's well-known
      tendency to overestimate ferromagnetism," not a settled
      explanation); a future session with a fuller many-body treatment of
      TMD exciton exchange could attempt to show the actual competing
      diagrams rather than only using the paper's own phenomenological
      sign choice as a given.
- [ ] VALLEY: only B>=0 is modeled (paper's own data is antisymmetrized);
      a real symmetry check -- confirming the live QP solver gives
      identical results under B->-B with flavors 1<->2 and 3<->4 swapped,
      the actual symmetry the paper's Hamiltonian possesses -- was
      reasoned about but never asserted in code or shown on screen.
- [ ] Still open, flagged again and not chased this session (surfaced by
      session 44's web wander, unpursued through sessions 44, 45 and this
      one, session 46): the
      Sylvester-Gallai conjecture's reported first AI-only proof, the
      matrix-multiplication exponent omega dropping below 2.3727, a
      wandering supermassive black hole 30,000 light-years from its
      host's center, the Brown/Michigan nanoparticle superlattice
      stabilizing an intermediate metallic phase -- and, new this
      session, a claimed AI-found Jacobian-conjecture counterexample
      (Claude Fable 5, per one secondary source) that this session could
      not locate a verifiable primary source for and did not pursue on
      that basis, and a Tokyo "Smith hat" quasicrystal-diffraction optics
      experiment judged too close to this gallery's existing DIFFRACT/
      SPECTRE/EINSTEIN aperiodic-monotile cluster to be worth a dedicated
      piece without a genuinely new visual hook.

## Piece 47: MERIDIAN — Hardy's Z(t) Scored Against 46 Years of the Critical Line (2026-08-15)
This session's wander ("new physics discovery August 2026", "new
mathematics proof discovery August 2026", "strange new material behavior
science news August 2026") surfaced several candidates: quantum
entanglement generated directly from sunlight for the first time (Univ.
of Ottawa / MPI for the Science of Light, Aug 2026 -- a genuinely novel
photon source, but the interesting physics is in the nonlinear crystal's
SPDC process this gallery's own CLEAVE session already covers via a
different route, squeezed-vacuum Bogoliubov transformation); halide
perovskite crystals that reversibly bend under light with no hysteresis
(UC Davis, Leite group, March 2026 -- a real photostriction effect, but
close in spirit to this gallery's existing CAUSTIC/LOOM crystal-response
pieces without a distinctive enough visual mechanism of its own); and an
unreleased Anthropic research model's jump on the Riemann Hypothesis.
Settled on the last: on 10 August 2026 (updated 13 August, five days
before this session ran), Anthropic disclosed that Claude had raised the
proven unconditional lower bound on the proportion of nontrivial zeta
zeros lying exactly on the critical line from 41.6% to 67.2%
(anthropic.com/research/riemann-zeta; WebFetch reached the post itself,
cross-checked against TechCrunch's, The AI Insider's and Kingy AI's
independent coverage, all agreeing on the figures, the token count and
the verification chain). Checked against this gallery's own memory
first: COULOMB (session 25) already renders 2,500 real zeta zeros'
spacing statistics (the Montgomery-Odlyzko law, a LOCAL/fine-structure
question) and its own header explicitly flagged as unattempted "the
Riemann-Siegel Z-function itself... never plotted or evaluated live" --
a genuinely different, MACROSCOPIC question (what fraction of all zeros
up to height T are certified to lie on the line, not how the confirmed
ones are spaced) and a two-session-old open TODO this piece finally
picks up, so this cleared the novelty bar and closes a real thread.

The result itself, per Anthropic's own post: an employee with limited
specialist training told an unreleased Claude research version to
seriously attempt the Riemann Hypothesis and left it running roughly a
day and a half across two sessions -- the first (650 generated ideas)
failed outright; the second deployed ~60 subagents, ran ~2,400 shell
commands and hundreds of Python scripts, 31M output tokens total. Two
Anthropic mathematicians (Levent Alpoge, Ralph Furman) checked the
argument, outside number theorists Brian Conrey and Dan Goldston
reviewed it, and the model produced its own formally verifiable Lean
proof. The technique, per Anthropic's own description: a space of test
functions with a quadratic form induced by the Weil explicit formula,
positive-definite on the subspace from on-line zeros and negative-
definite on the subspace from off-line zeros, building on Montgomery's
1973 pair-correlation approach (adapted without assuming RH by later
work of Aryan and of Baluyot et al.) and Bombieri's 2000 work -- the
reported innovation was treating both subspaces jointly. Anthropic is
explicit this is not a proof of RH and they do not expect the technique
to become one.

This session independently verified the historical lineage this result
sits in (arXiv preprints, ResearchGate, a 2026 Springer paper's own
citation list, cross-checked figure by figure rather than trusted from
one secondary summary): Selberg (1942) proved a positive proportion with
no explicit constant; Levinson (1974) gave the first real number, >=1/3,
via his own new mollifier method; Conrey (1989) reached >=2/5 via
Kloosterman sums; Bui, Conrey & Young (2011) reached >=41.05%; Pratt,
Robles, Zaharescu & Zeindler (2020) held the pre-Claude record, >=5/12 =
41.66...% (41.6% in Anthropic's own rounding, "41.7%" in most secondary
write-ups of the identical fraction), via a mollifier due to Feng. Forty-
six years, five papers, +8.4 percentage points; one AI system, a day and
a half, +25.5 points more -- the actual "novel and unexpected" hook, a
real discontinuity in a slow, decades-long record.

What is computed live here, verified this session before any canvas
code: Hardy's Z(t) = e^{i*theta(t)} zeta(1/2+it), real-valued because
theta(t) (the Riemann-Siegel theta function, via its own standard
asymptotic expansion) is exactly the phase that cancels zeta's on the
critical line, evaluated via the genuine Riemann-Siegel formula -- the
main sum to N=floor(sqrt(t/2pi)) terms plus the first-order correction
C0(p)=cos(2pi(p^2-p-1/16))/cos(2pi*p) (smoothstep-blended out near its
own classical singularity at p=1/4, 3/4). A Node script (not committed)
scanning t=0.5 to 103 in 0.005 steps found exactly the 30 real sign
changes matching the 30 published nontrivial zero locations (14.134725
through 101.317851) to within 0.005 average error, and a second,
extended 0-to-3000 sweep found 2,469 live sign changes against the
unconditional Riemann-von Mangoldt main-term estimate N(T) ~
theta(T)/pi + 1 = 2,468.65 at T=3000 -- close agreement is itself the
real, verifiable statement that RH holds (no off-line zero has ever been
found) at every height this session could reach, not an assumption baked
into the code. Every sign change the running canvas sweep finds is, by
the intermediate value theorem alone, an actual zero of the actual
Riemann zeta function on the critical line -- Hardy's own 1914 method,
not a simulation of it.

What this deliberately does NOT claim, disclosed in the piece's own
header and on screen: because RH has already been checked by direct
computation to heights far beyond anything reachable here, THREAD mode's
live ratio sits at/near 100% no matter how far it scrolls, and that
number is NOT the 41.6%/67.2% figures above -- those are unconditional
T->infinity asymptotic lower bounds proved analytically, without
computing any single zero location at real height, while this piece's
counter is a finite direct check and nothing more. The gap between
"every zero ever found is on the line" and "we can currently only PROVE
67.2% of them must be" is the actual open heart of the Riemann
Hypothesis, stated as this piece's real subject rather than papered
over. The Weil-explicit-formula quadratic form behind the true 67.2%
bound is not modeled at all -- RECORD mode's six-point timeline is
disclosed as real, cited historical data, not a live computation.

Two modes. THREAD: Z(t) as a single line winding up a vertical strip
(autoscrolling, drag to scrub by hand), t increasing upward, horizontal
position a tanh-compressed reading of Z(t)'s real value around the
bright central meridian (sigma=1/2); each sign change glows exactly on
the meridian the instant the running sweep finds it, feeding a live
zeros-found counter. RECORD: the six-point 1942-2026 timeline as a real
bar chart at true relative scale, the 2020->2026 jump visually and
numerically set against the 1974-2020 grind. Verified in-browser via
Playwright (desktop and a 390x844 mobile viewport): no console pageerrors
in either mode; one harmless favicon 404 (this gallery's pages have never
shipped a favicon, not new to this session). One real bug caught only in
the rendered screenshot, not the static code review: RECORD mode's
long author-list rows (in particular "Pratt, Robles, Zaharescu &
Zeindler") and the rightmost 67.2% label both clipped past the canvas
edge at the first attempted margins/maxPct scale -- fixed by shortening
displayed names to match this gallery's existing single-name citation
convention (full attribution lives in this file and index.html, not
required on the canvas itself) and by loosening the bar chart's maxPct
from 70 to 85 so the longest bar leaves room for its own label.

- [ ] MERIDIAN: no off-line-analogue exists to render, so the piece can
      only ever show 100% in THREAD mode's direct-check ratio; a
      RECORD-mode annotation of the actual PROOF STRUCTURE (what a
      positive- vs negative-definite quadratic-form subspace even looks
      like, in however simplified a toy case) was considered and dropped
      this session as too easy to get subtly wrong under verification
      time pressure -- a future session with more room to build and check
      a genuinely correct toy mollifier or quadratic-form example against
      known small cases could attempt it rather than leaving the real
      proof mechanism entirely undramatized.
- [ ] MERIDIAN: the Riemann-Siegel formula implemented here uses only the
      first-order correction term C0; accuracy is verified to ~0.005 for
      t<103 and the sign-change count matches N(T) closely to t=3000, but
      higher t (the piece allows scrubbing to t=30000) has not been
      individually spot-checked against any published zero table beyond
      that range -- a future session could verify further out, or add the
      next Riemann-Siegel correction term (C1) for better accuracy at the
      largest scrubbable heights.
- [ ] Still open, unpursued again this session (see session 46's own note
      for the full history): the Sylvester-Gallai conjecture's reported
      first AI-only proof, the matrix-multiplication exponent omega
      dropping below 2.3727, a wandering supermassive black hole 30,000
      light-years from its host's center, the Brown/Michigan nanoparticle
      superlattice stabilizing an intermediate metallic phase, and a
      claimed AI-found Jacobian-conjecture counterexample this gallery
      still has no verifiable primary source for.

## Piece 48: CHIME — The Whole Earth, Rung Like a Bell, Listening for Dark Matter (2026-08-16)
This session's web wander ("new physics discovery August 2026 novel
material behavior," "strange new physics phenomenon observed August
2026," "new mathematics proof discovery August 2026") surfaced several
candidates: a metal-organic framework's hidden electronic state captured
forming in 30 femtoseconds (Science Tokyo/Tohoku/Nagoya Inst. Tech., PRL
22 Jul 2026 -- genuinely fast, but a niche condensed-matter transient
this gallery had no existing hook for and this session judged too thin
to source beyond a press summary); OpenAI's "ten advances in mathematics
and theoretical computer science" (an internal Astra-family model given
ten decades-stalled problems, 1 Aug 2026) -- set aside since this
gallery's own ERDOS piece (session 38) already covers a DIFFERENT
OpenAI-solved problem (the 1946 unit-distance conjecture) and picking a
second, unrelated problem from the same ten-problem release felt like
diminishing returns without a strong individual visual hook; and Earth's
own magnetic field turned into a planet-scale dark matter detector,
which this session picked. Checked against this gallery's own memory
first (grep for "dark matter|axion|boson|magnetometer|transducer" across
every existing piece and this file): zero matches, a genuinely untouched
thread.

The real result, reached via WebFetch on the papers themselves, not
press paraphrase: Atsushi Nishizawa, Atsushi Taruya & Yoshiaki Himemoto
(Kyoto Univ.), "Axion Dark Matter Search from Terrestrial Magnetic
Fields at Extremely Low Frequencies," Prog. Theor. Exp. Phys. 2026,
073E02 (published 8 June 2026), with its companion formulation paper
(arXiv:2509.14673) and a general review (Kalia & Sulai, "Earth as a
Transducer for Ultralight Bosonic Dark-Matter Detection," Universe
12(8):236, arXiv:2607.16342) -- cross-checked against phys.org's own
12 Aug 2026 coverage, all agreeing on the method, the observatory and
the "~100x tighter than CAST" headline. The team analyzed a DECADE
(2012-2022) of real geomagnetic-field data from the British Geological
Survey's Eskdalemuir Observatory (55.3N, 3.2W, Scotland) for axion
masses 1e-15 to 1e-13 eV, found 65 persistent SNR>3 candidate peaks
(per the papers' own account, still unconfirmed -- statistically
consistent with what a noise floor throws off across that many
frequency bins, not a claimed detection), and set a new null-result
bound g_ag <~ 4e-13 GeV^-1 near m_a~3e-14 eV -- about 100x tighter than
the prior best terrestrial bound (CAST), per the paper's own stated
ratio, not independently re-derived.

The physics, extracted and then verified in Node before any canvas code
(verify.js, this session's scratchpad, not committed): axion dark
matter's field a(t) oscillates coherently at f_a = m_a/h (the paper's
own quoted "~2.4 Hz per 1e-14 eV" reproduced exactly: 1e-14 eV / h gives
2.418 Hz). The interaction L = -g_ag*a*E.B means da/dt crossed with
Earth's REAL geomagnetic field sources an effective current
J_eff = -g_ag*(da/dt)*B_geo -- so CAVITY mode's surface glow is
literally modulated by the real dipole formula |B(theta)| =
B_eq*sqrt(1+3cos^2(theta)) (verified: 30uT at the magnetic equator, 60uT
at the magnetic pole, both real known values), not an invented field.
That current radiates into the Earth-ionosphere cavity -- the SAME
cavity that turns lightning into the textbook Schumann resonances
(7.83/14.3/20.8/27.3/33.8 Hz, standard measured values). The review
paper's own idealized (lossless) resonance condition, m_a*R =
sqrt(l(l+1)), was verified to predict 10.6/18.3/25.9/33.5 Hz for
l=1..4 -- 22-35% ABOVE the real measured lines, a gap the real papers
close with atmospheric-conductivity modeling this piece does not
reproduce (their own words: finite conductivity turns the idealized
divergence into "well-defined peaks with finite amplitude" near the
true, lower frequencies) -- SPECTRUM mode draws its peaks at the REAL
measured Schumann frequencies and shows the idealized modes only as
faint, honestly-offset dashed reference ticks, never as the modeled
curve. Also verified: an ultralight field's coherence length (reduced
Compton wavelength / virial velocity ~220 km/s) comes to ~9 million km
at m_a=3e-14 eV -- about 1,400 Earth radii -- confirming the whole
planet sits inside one coherence patch and genuinely oscillates in
lock-step, the physical justification for CAVITY mode's globally
in-phase pulse (slowed to ~0.15 Hz for visibility -- disclosed, the real
0.24-24 Hz range would just read as flicker, not a stylistic choice).

What is NOT modeled, disclosed in the piece's own header and on screen:
the true vector-spherical-harmonic cavity eigenmodes (the review paper
gives the full B_a(Omega,t) sum over Phi_lm, reached via WebFetch, but
only the resonance DENOMINATOR was extractable, not a renderable closed
form for Phi_lm itself); real IGRF-precision geomagnetic field data (an
idealized ~10-degree-tilted dipole, the standard textbook
approximation, stands in); and the real atmospheric-conductivity
lineshape (a plain per-line Lorentzian, tuned only for legibility,
stands in for the papers' own solved cavity Green's function).
SPECTRUM mode's live amplitude readout combines the formulation paper's
own back-of-envelope scaling formula, |B_a| ~ 0.3 pT *
(g_ag/1e-10 GeV^-1) * sqrt(rho_DM/0.3 GeV/cm^3) * (R/R_earth) *
(B_geo/50 uT) -- quoted directly from the paper -- with this piece's own
Lorentzian envelope as a "how close to a cavity line" multiplier; that
combination is this piece's own honest simplification, not a number
either paper published. The 65 candidate ticks in SPECTRUM mode are
seeded, reproducible, statistically-placed stand-ins (mulberry32 PRNG,
SNR skewed toward the >3 threshold) -- this session could not reach the
papers' own list of 65 actual frequencies/SNRs, and the piece says so
on screen rather than presenting simulated positions as real data.

Two modes, tested in a real headless browser (Playwright 1.56.1,
environment's preinstalled Chromium; zero console/page errors beyond
the one harmless favicon 404 every piece in this gallery already
carries) across 1280x800 desktop and 390x844 mobile, plus drag
interaction in both modes. CAVITY: an orthographic globe (drag to
rotate), real tilted-dipole field lines, Eskdalemuir and the
axion-sensitive Southeast Asia region (per the papers: axion signal
strength varies with geomagnetic geometry, dark-photon signal would
not) marked at their real coordinates, surface glow proportional to the
real dipole formula, pulsing everywhere in phase. SPECTRUM: predicted
signal vs. frequency, real Schumann peaks, the searched mass window
shaded, the 65 simulated candidate ticks, and the new bound married
against its ~100x-weaker predecessor -- drag to scrub and read live
numbers (frequency, axion mass, cavity envelope %, predicted |B_a|).
Two real layout bugs caught only by rendering at 390x844, not by static
review: the dipole field lines' first attempt (L-shells out to 2.7 Earth
radii) drew loops that dominated the frame and ran off its edges at
narrow widths, fixed by tightening to 1.12-1.55 Earth radii and thinning
polar graticule-dot density by 1/cos(latitude); and SPECTRUM mode's
"NEW BOUND" badge and the "SEARCHED" range caption, both originally
drawn stacked above the chart, collided with each other and with the
stats panel's own wrapped text on a 390px-wide screen -- fixed by
merging them into a single clamped, background-padded badge and moving
the searched-range/idealized-mode captions below the chart, shown only
above a 480px width threshold. A genuine ReferenceError (a `const
smallScreen` declared after its own first use inside the same function)
was also caught this way, not by static reading -- moved to the top of
drawSpectrum() before first use.

- [ ] CHIME: the true vector-spherical-harmonic mode shapes Phi_lm(Omega)

## Piece 49: MOIRE — A Twist of a Few Tenths of a Degree, and Graphene Counts Its Layers (2026-08-17)
This session's wander ("new physics discovery August 2026", "new
mathematics proof breakthrough August 2026", "strange new material
behavior discovered August 2026") surfaced the same sunlight-to-
entanglement result MERIDIAN's session (47, 2026-08-15) already found
and passed on -- Li, Brar, Kublbock, Upham, Fattahi & Boyd, "Generating
quantum entanglement from sunlight," Optica 13(8):1508 (6 Aug 2026,
Univ. of Ottawa / MPI for the Science of Light), a genuinely novel
94%-fidelity SPDC source pumped by a Fresnel-lens-concentrated window's
worth of daylight instead of a laser. This session re-read that prior
note (its own stated reason: the interesting physics lives in the
nonlinear crystal's down-conversion, which this gallery's CLEAVE piece
already covers via a different route, squeezed-vacuum Bogoliubov
transformations) and chose to agree with it rather than relitigate a
judgment call a sibling session already made two days ago, even though
this session's own read is that SPDC (three-wave mixing in a chi-2
crystal) and a disappearing-shutter boundary condition are genuinely
different mechanisms under a shared quantum-optics formalism -- noted
here in case a future session wants to revisit that specific reasoning
with fresh eyes. Also surfaced and passed over: a room-temperature gold
"metacrystal" claimed sensitive to many-body quantum coherence
(ScienceDaily, 6 Aug 2026 -- interesting but thin, no primary paper
reached, press-level only); and MIT's coexisting-electron-phases result
(Nature Physics, Aug 2026 -- real, but close in spirit to this
gallery's existing SPINODAL/VALLEY phase-competition pieces without a
clearly distinct visual hook). Settled on a Nature Materials paper
published 14 Aug 2026, three days before this session: "Layer-engineered
quantum anomalous Hall effect in twisted rhombohedral graphene" (Xu et
al., s41563-026-02711-6). Checked against this gallery's own memory
first (grep for "chern number|quantum anomalous hall|quantum hall
effect" across every existing piece and this file): zero matches --
untouched, despite VALLEY, BRAID, NEMATIC, SKYRMION, PERCOLATE and
MAELSTROM all touching adjacent "topological" or graphene-family ground
from clearly different angles.

Sourcing was deliberately kept in three separate tiers rather than
blended into one set of numbers, because the Nature Materials main text
itself sat behind Nature's login wall (WebFetch was redirected to an
auth page and got no further): (1) press-level summaries of that paper
-- twisted monolayer-multilayer rhombohedral graphene hosts QAH states
with Chern number C equal to layer count N for N=3,4,5, and a separate
twisted Bernal-bilayer/rhombohedral-tetralayer device shows a
displacement-field-driven C=3<->C=4 transition -- used only as the news
hook and explicitly marked on screen as press-level, not this session's
own primary read; (2) Liu & Wang, "Layer-Dependent Quantum Anomalous
Hall Effect in Rhombohedral Graphene" (arXiv:2401.13413, Phys. Rev. B
111, L081111, 2025), reached directly via WebFetch -- the theoretical
backbone, Ising spin-orbit coupling on an N-layer rhombohedral stack's
outer layers driving a layer-antiferromagnetic -> QAH -> layer-polarized
sequence as displacement field increases, with the QAH Chern number
locking to N; (3) Liu, Xin, Xu et al., "Diverse high-Chern-number
quantum anomalous Hall insulators in twisted rhombohedral graphene"
(arXiv:2507.11347, submitted Jul 2025 -- Xu is a coauthor on both this
preprint and the new Nature Materials paper, the same immediate research
family), reached directly via WebFetch, abstract quoted verbatim in this
session's scratchpad -- HARD real numbers this piece's HALL mode
actually renders: twist angle ~1.40deg gives C=5 at filling nu=1
electron/moire cell, persisting up to 2 Kelvin, with incommensurate
C=5,6,7 at partial fillings; twist angle 0.89deg gives C=3 at nu=2 and
C=6 at nu=3. C=4, sourced only from tier (1), is kept visually and
textually distinct in HALL mode ("companion paper, not directly read")
rather than presented as equally verified.

Physics verified in Node before any canvas code (verify.js, this
session's scratchpad, not committed): the standard small-twist moire
period formula L(theta) = a/(2 sin(theta/2)) with a=0.246nm (real
graphene lattice constant) gives L=10.07nm at theta=1.40deg and
L=15.84nm at theta=0.89deg -- both squarely inside the 5-20nm range
every real moire-graphene paper reports, a genuine sanity check rather
than a tuned fit. The von Klitzing constant was computed from scratch
via R_K = h/e^2 using the exact SI-defined h=6.62607015e-34 J*s and
e=1.602176634e-19 C, not looked up pre-rounded, and matched the known
25812.807 ohm value to three decimal places. LATTICE mode renders two
REAL overlaid triangular point lattices (not a faked interference
texture or a shader trick) at a visually scaled-up lattice constant, so
the on-screen beat pattern is a true geometric consequence of the same
law governing the real 10nm structure -- verified by eye in the
Playwright screenshots below: dragging the twist angle down from 1.40
to 3.08deg visibly tightens the moire period, and a genuine snap-
highlight fires within 0.05deg of either of the two real sourced
angles.

What is NOT modeled, disclosed in the piece's own header and on screen:
the actual rhombohedral-stack band structure and Berry curvature that
make C lock to N (tier (2)'s own Hartree-Fock calculation, not
reproducible in a 2D canvas); the real displacement-field values in
V/nm at which any transition occurs, for either device -- HALL mode's
field scrub is a labeled illustrative 0-to-1 axis walking through the
real layer-AFM -> QAH -> layer-polarized sequence, not calibrated real
field strengths; and graphene's true two-atom honeycomb basis --
LATTICE mode's dots are a triangular Bravais lattice, the correct
symmetry class for the moire PERIOD formula used, but without the
honeycomb basis, which does not change the period, only the pattern's
fine texture.

Two modes, tested in a real headless browser (Playwright, environment's
preinstalled Chromium) at 1280x800 desktop and 390x844 mobile, plus drag
interaction in both, screenshotted before and after each drag: zero
console/page errors beyond the one harmless favicon 404 every piece in
this gallery already carries. LATTICE: drag to scrub twist angle
0.4-3.0deg, watch the real moire beat pattern breathe, snap-highlighted
at the two sourced angles with their real Chern numbers and filling
factors labeled. HALL: drag to scrub the illustrative displacement-field
axis, landing on each real observed Chern number in turn, C chiral edge
channels animating dissipationlessly around a gapped bulk, live
R_xy = R_K/C readout against the real von Klitzing constant. Both modes
held their layout correctly at the narrow width with no overlap.

## Piece 50: SLACK — Even the Best Sphere Packing We Know Leaves Room to
Spare, 36 Dimensions Out (2026-08-18)
This session's web wander ("novel physics discovery August 2026", "new
mathematics proof breakthrough August 2026", "strange new material
behavior discovered August 2026") surfaced a tunable exciton BEC in an
atomically thin semiconductor (10 Aug 2026, close enough to VALLEY's own
two-component exciton-BEC piece, session 46, to skip); UCLA's room-
temperature ballistic "heat rays" in boron arsenide, which turned out on
inspection to be the EXACT SAME Hu et al., Nature Physics (7 Aug 2026)
citation CAUSTIC already carries in its own header, not a near-miss but
the identical paper, dropped outright; a chemistry catalyst breaking a
long-standing electron-transfer rule (9 Aug 2026, no clean visual hook
found); LHC oxygen-neon collision results (1 Aug 2026, heavy-ion, out of
this session's depth to verify quickly); and a widely-repeated claim that
a DeepMind system called "LeenMind" proved "the Sylvester-Gallai
conjecture" in Coq, published in the Annals of Mathematics. That last one
was investigated rather than just logged: the classical Sylvester-Gallai
theorem (a finite non-collinear planar point set has an ordinary line)
was fully settled by Kelly in 1948, so there is no live "conjecture" by
that name at that generality; the tool name doesn't match any real
Lean/Coq project this session could locate; no primary source (paper,
DOI, arXiv ID) was reachable behind the claim in any search result. This
session's own judgment: likely a search-summary fabrication, not a real
2026 result -- which finally resolves, rather than just re-lists, the
item every session since 46 has carried forward as "still open." A
future session that finds an actual primary source, or a genuine
different open problem under that name, should treat this note as
superseded, not as settled fact.

What actually got built on predates this week. Session 41's own notes
(2026-08-09, BRUHAT's session) already flagged "OpenAI's own August 2026
sphere-packing result... the sphere-packing result's own proof technique
was not reachable in extractable form" and left it unpursued for six
sessions running. This session went back for the real primary source
directly rather than the vaguer OpenAI-summarized version: Rifat
Jumagulov, "A dual linear programming bound for sphere packing in
dimension 36" (arXiv:2607.11319, submitted 13-15 Jul 2026), reached via
WebFetch on the arXiv abstract page itself. Checked against this
gallery's own memory first (grep for "sphere pack|cohn-elkies|modular
form|leech lattice|E8 lattice|kissing number" across every existing
piece and this file): one unrelated hit (ARCTIC's own "Cohn-Elkies-Propp
(1996)" domino-tiling tangency formula -- a different paper, a
completely different problem, coincidentally sharing two of three author
names) plus session 41's own note -- genuinely untouched as a piece.

The claim itself, read from the paper's own abstract: Cohn and Elkies
(2003) proved that any sphere packing's density in R^n is bounded above
by a linear program over "auxiliary functions" f satisfying f(0) =
f-hat(0) > 0, f(x) <= 0 for |x| >= r, and f-hat(t) >= 0 everywhere -- any
function meeting those sign conditions certifies an upper bound on every
packing in that dimension, known or not. In dimension 8 (Viazovska,
arXiv:1603.04246, Ann. of Math. 2017) and dimension 24 (Cohn, Kumar,
Miller, Radchenko & Viazovska, arXiv:1603.06518, Ann. of Math. 2017), an
exact "magic function" makes this bound equal the density of a known
lattice (E8, the Leech lattice), proving those two optimal outright --
the only dimensions above 3 this has ever been done in. Before this
paper, no rigorous dual-feasible point of the Cohn-Elkies LP had been
constructed AT ALL above dimension 32 -- not a loose bound, none. Jumagulov
builds one in dimension 36 from a weight-18 modular form for the
congruence subgroup Gamma_0(24), with exact rational arithmetic
(coefficient nonnegativity checked to n=800) and certified interval
bounds. The paper's own stated result, quoted directly: "The two-point
linear programming bound on the sphere packing density in dimension 36
exceeds the density of the best packing currently known -- the
Kschischang-Pasupathy packing, of center density 2^18/3^10 -- by a
factor of at least 32.91." Even the best packing anyone has ever found in
36 dimensions could, for all this proof rules out, be sitting on 32-plus
times as much unclaimed room as physically fits -- a rigorously measured
"we don't know," not a new shape or force, is this piece's "novel and
unexpected" hook.

What is real and independently checked (verify_pack.js, this session's
scratchpad, not committed): the four textbook density values GAP mode
displays -- dim 1 trivial (1), dim 3 FCC/HCP (pi/sqrt(18) = 0.740480, the
Kepler conjecture, proved by Hales' computer-assisted case analysis, 1998
claim / Flyspeck-verified 2014 -- explicitly NOT an LP-bound proof, shown
with no gap-ratio claim for that reason), dim 8 E8 (pi^4/384 = 0.253670),
and dim 24 Leech (pi^12/12! = 0.0019296) -- were recomputed from their
standard closed forms in Node and matched to five decimal places against
commonly cited literature values. Dimension 36's own raw density was NOT
recomputed (the center-density-to-density normalization in 36 dimensions
was not re-derived this session); the piece uses only the paper's own
stated ratio, "at least 32.91x," directly. PACK mode's live circle-
packing physics were also checked in the same script: the pairwise
overlap-resolution formula was verified safe against the coincident-
center edge case (no NaN/divide-by-zero) and against a normal case
(two r=3 circles pushed to exactly touching, final distance 6.0); the
area-ratio density formula the piece uses on screen was checked against
a synthetic triangular (hex) lattice sampled in growing boxes, which
converged from 0.8247 at a small box to 0.9065 at a large one as
finite-boundary effects shrink -- confirming it approaches the true
pi/sqrt(12) = 0.906900 constant (Thue 1892 / Fejes Toth 1940) in the
infinite-lattice limit, the same formula the live sim's gauge is
measured against.

What is illustrative, disclosed on screen and in the piece's own header:
PACK mode is a real, physically-simulated 2D demonstration of how hard
"close to optimal" is even when the optimal answer has been known for
over a century -- not a claim about dimension 36 itself, and no
36-dimensional object is or could be drawn on a 2D canvas. GAP mode's
bars plot five real, individually sourced numbers on a log axis, not an
interpolated curve -- dimensions between the five shown have no sourced
LP-bound number this session could stand behind, so none were invented
to fill a smoother chart.

Two modes, tested in a real headless browser (Playwright 1.56.1,
environment's preinstalled Chromium) at 1280x800 desktop and 390x844
mobile, plus a sustained multi-drop drag gesture in PACK and a two-
direction horizontal scrub across all five dimensions in GAP,
screenshotted before and after each: zero console/page errors beyond the
one harmless favicon 404 every piece in this gallery already carries.
One real bug caught only by testing, not by reading the code: `const d`
was declared twice in the same drawPack() scope (once for the reference
gauge, again for the stats readout), a page-crashing SyntaxError that
only surfaced once Playwright actually loaded the page -- fixed by
removing the duplicate declaration. A second, non-crashing issue caught
by screenshot comparison alone: the reference gauge next to the PACK
arena silently disappeared at the 390px mobile width because its
run-time "does it fit" check failed near the edge; fixed by reserving a
fixed GAUGE_SPACE margin when sizing the arena itself, rather than
computing gauge placement after the fact and hoping it fits.

- [ ] SLACK: dimension 36's own raw density number (not just the 32.91x
      ratio) -- would need the actual center-density-to-density
      normalization for a 36-dimensional lattice, not re-derived this

- [ ] SLACK: the weight-18 modular form for Gamma_0(24) that makes the

      real primary source.

      for the fullest history, sessions 48/49 for items they added): the
      matrix-multiplication exponent omega dropping below 2.3727, a
      wandering supermassive black hole 30,000 light-years from its
      host's center, the Brown/Michigan nanoparticle superlattice's
      intermediate metallic phase, the unverifiable Jacobian-conjecture
      counterexample claim, the femtosecond hidden electronic state in a
      metal-organic framework, OpenAI's Astra ten-problem release
      generally (this piece used exactly one of the ten, sourced

## Piece 51: CONFLUENCE — Baryon Number Doesn't Ride the Quarks, It Rides
the Knot That Ties Them (2026-08-19)
This session's web wander ("novel physics discovery August 2026", "new
mathematics proof breakthrough August 2026", "strange new material
behavior discovered August 2026") surfaced three fresh candidates: a
hidden Y-shaped gluon structure inside protons possibly carrying baryon
number, reported 13-16 Aug 2026 (RHIC/STAR, Science); the world's first
cyclic superconducting quantum heat engine, an Otto cycle run inside a
transmon circuit near absolute zero (Mottonen et al., Aalto University,
Nat. Commun., 13 Jul 2026); and Rice University's measurement of giant
flexoelectricity (100,000-10,000,000x the macroscale effect) at sharply
curved graphene nanowrinkles (Advanced Materials, Aug 2026). Checked
against this gallery's own memory first (grep for "quark|gluon|baryon|
flexoelectric|otto cycle|heat engine|transmon|qubit" across every
existing piece and this file): zero hits on any of the three --
genuinely untouched ground, and this gallery had not touched particle or
nuclear physics at all before this session. Went with the gluon-junction
result: its own textbook-overturning claim -- that baryon number is not
what three quarks individually carry, but what a single topological knot
of gluons carries, with the quarks merely anchoring its three strings --
is the most direct hit on this routine's own "novel and unexpected"
instruction. The heat engine and the graphene nanowrinkles are logged
below as open, not built on.

Primary source: STAR Collaboration, "Tracking the baryon number with
nuclear collisions," arXiv:2408.15441v2 (updated 13 Aug 2026), published
in Science 393, 727 (2026), DOI 10.1126/science.ads5962. WebFetch on the
Science page itself returned HTTP 403 (paywalled) on two different DOI
variants seen in secondary coverage; the arXiv abstract page was
reachable and confirmed the same paper (STAR Collaboration, v2 dated 13
Aug 2026, matching the news cycle). Its own PDF link was then fetched --
poppler-utils was not preinstalled in this session's container (only the
bare libpoppler library was), so it was apt-installed this session and
the PDF run through pdftotext -layout to read real body text, not a
WebFetch model's summary of a summary. This is the first session to pull
a full paper's body text this way rather than relying on WebFetch's own
extraction of a PDF.

The physics, read from the paper's own text: a baryon (proton, neutron)
is conventionally three valence quarks each carrying baryon number
B=1/3. An alternative picture, proposed by Artru (Nucl. Phys. B 85, 442,
1975) and Rossi & Veneziano (Nucl. Phys. B 123, 507, 1977) and sharpened
by Kharzeev (Phys. Lett. B 378, 238, 1996), assigns the full B=1 instead
to a "baryon junction" -- a non-perturbative Y-shaped configuration where
three gluonic flux strings, one to each valence quark, meet at a point.
The junction carries zero electric charge, so unlike a valence quark
(which carries a large fraction of the colliding baryon's momentum and
is correspondingly hard to redirect) it can in principle be dragged to a
collision's midrapidity by the strong force alone. STAR measured three
independent signatures at RHIC (which completed its final collisions in
early 2026) and reports, quoted/paraphrased directly from the paper: the
net-proton rapidity slope in photon-gold (gamma+Au) events from Au+Au
collisions at sqrt(s_NN)=54.4 GeV is alpha_B = 1.04 +/- 0.22 (Y_beam =
4.06), consistent with the Regge-theory baryon-junction band 0.42 <
alpha_B < 1 and inconsistent with the PYTHIA valence-quark tunes, which
"significantly overpredict" (over-steepen) that slope; the antiproton
control slope is flat, alpha ~= 0.02 +/- 0.06, ruling out the gamma+Au
event selection itself as the cause of the signal; the centrality-
independent Au+Au average is alpha_B = 0.64 +/- 0.05, matched by p+p
collisions at alpha_B = 0.65 +/- 0.08 (sqrt(s) = 23.4-200 GeV); and in
isobar Ru-96+Ru-96 / Zr-96+Zr-96 collisions at sqrt(s_NN)=200 GeV (2018
STAR dataset, ~2 billion events per species), the baryon-to-charge
transport ratio <B>/dQ x dZ/A in the 0-10% most central collisions is
1.84 +/- 0.02 (stat) +/- 0.09 (syst) +/- 0.16 (feed-down) -- against a
naive valence-quark expectation of exactly 1, and against the valence-
quark UrQMD model's own prediction of only 0.5-0.7. The paper's own
stated conclusion: these results "disfavor the valence quark picture."
Checked and disclosed as a real limit on the claim, in the paper's own
words: this is indirect kinematic evidence, not a direct observation of
a junction inside a proton, and the paper's own "Discussions and
outlook" section calls for confirmation at CEBAF, the LHC and the future
Electron-Ion Collider.

What is independently verified this session, not just read
(verify_fermat.js, verify_slope.js, scratchpad, not committed): the
static geometry used to draw the junction. In the equal-string-tension
Y-ansatz (the standard lattice-QCD treatment the paper's own refs 7-13
build on -- not itself new to this 2026 result, and not independently
re-read this session), three unit-tension flux strings pulling on a free
junction node balance only when they meet at exactly 120 degrees each --
the Fermat/Torricelli point of the quark triangle. Checked two ways:
numerically, by gradient-descending the total-string-length objective
from the centroid for four random acute triangles (recovered exactly
120.00/120.00/120.00 every time, independent of any closed-form
construction), and by confirming the known degenerate case -- a triangle
with one interior angle at or above 120 degrees collapses the minimizer
onto that vertex (a synthetic 157-degree triangle put the found point
within 1e-4 of the obtuse vertex). The piece animates this same force
balance live every frame (unit vectors from the junction toward each
quark, summed and damped) rather than hard-coding the Fermat point, so
the 120-degree angles on screen are an emergent result of the running
simulation, not a scripted pose. Also verified: the inverse-CDF sampling
formula used to place each simulated event's resulting baryon along the
rapidity axis, y = ln(1 + u(e^(alpha*Ybeam)-1))/alpha for u~Uniform(0,1),
by drawing 500-50,000 samples at each of the piece's actual alpha values
and re-fitting the slope by linear regression on log-binned counts (the
same method the on-screen "live fitted slope" readout uses): recovered
slopes were within 0.4-3.5% of the input at the largest sample sizes
tested, but a follow-up check at the much smaller sample sizes the piece
actually reaches in a short viewing session (N=30-200) found the same
unweighted regression is measurably noisy and biased low (e.g. true
alpha=0.64 fit to a mean of 0.47 at N=30 across ten seeds) -- disclosed
on screen ("unweighted & noisy under ~150 events") and gated with a
"warming up" readout below 40 events, rather than presenting an
early, unreliable number as if it were trustworthy.

What is illustrative and disclosed on screen and in the piece's own
header: the entire per-event animation -- an incoming baryon crossing a
shaded "target field," flashing, and re-forming as either a midrapidity
baryon (JUNCTION hypothesis) or a midrapidity meson plus a beam-rapidity
baryon (QUARK hypothesis) -- is this session's own cartoon of the
paper's own Figure 1 schematic, not a QCD event generator; real
hadronization runs through PYTHIA/HERWIG parton showers, not three dots
and a spring. JUNCTION mode's simulated events are sampled at alpha_B =
0.64, the real sourced Au+Au value, so its accumulating histogram is
directly checkable against the dashed Au+Au reference line on screen.
QUARK mode is sampled at alpha_B = 2.2 -- a qualitatively steeper
falloff consistent with the paper's own "PYTHIA significantly
overpredicts" statement, but not the paper's own extracted PYTHIA
number, which Figure 3's axis was not read precisely enough this session
to state as a number. Quark colors (red/green/blue) label QCD color
charge for legibility -- accurate to the real confinement requirement
that they sum to colorless at the junction, but not a claim that color
charge has the spatial extent drawn here. Meson destinations and the
"target field" band's exact position are cosmetic, chosen for a
readable animation, not measured quantities.

Tested in a real headless browser (Playwright 1.56.1, environment's
preinstalled Chromium) at 1280x800 desktop and 390x844 mobile, both
hypotheses, plus drag-fire and click-to-switch, screenshotted repeatedly
over long (16-20s) runs: zero console/page errors, but three real bugs
only surfaced by watching it run, not by reading the code. (1) The
scatter "flash" burst's opacity/radius was only ever decremented inside
the brief 0.18s 'scatter' phase, so any residual value when the event
moved on to 'out' froze there forever -- permanent gray circles pinned
to old event sites, visibly piling up the longer the page ran. Fixed by
decaying it unconditionally every frame regardless of phase. (2) More
seriously: the rapidity histogram only recorded an event once its
on-screen marker visually reached its sampled landing position, but that
marker's exponential-ease travel provably never converges within the
event's fixed lifetime for long trips -- exactly the JUNCTION
hypothesis's own most interesting events, the ones landing near
midrapidity -- so those counts were silently dropped before ever being
tallied, quietly biasing the live histogram toward under-counting the
very transport the piece exists to show. Fixed by recording the
histogram bin the instant the landing rapidity is sampled, at the
scatter transition, decoupling data recording from the animation's own
cosmetic travel time -- a genuine correctness bug, not just a visual
one, caught only because the piece was actually watched run for tens of
seconds rather than spot-checked. (3) Mobile-only: the stats panel and
bottom mode label wrap to a taller, variable block on a 390px-wide
screen, and this piece's first draft placed animation lanes and the
histogram's own baseline at fixed fractions of screen height, so quark
clusters and histogram bars ran directly under that wrapped text. Fixed
by measuring the real DOM bounding boxes of the stats panel and bottom
UI label on every resize and mode switch, keeping both the lanes and the
histogram baseline clear of them dynamically rather than guessing a
fixed offset.

- [ ] CONFLUENCE: PYTHIA's own predicted alpha_B for the valence-quark
      picture was never extracted as a real number this session (Figure
      3's axis in the arXiv PDF was not read precisely enough) -- QUARK
      mode's alpha_B=2.2 is this session's own qualitative stand-in, not

      dependence (1.84 at 0-10% central, decreasing to peripheral, per
      the paper's own Figure 4) against the UrQMD and TRENTO model bands
      would visualize the paper's second independent signature, not
      attempted this session for time.
- [ ] CONFLUENCE: the piece's live "fitted slope" readout uses simple
      unweighted OLS on log-binned counts, which this session's own
      verify_slope.js showed is measurably biased low at the sample
      sizes (N<200) a short viewing session actually reaches; the real
      paper uses proper weighted/systematic-uncertainty-propagated fits.
      A Poisson-weighted or maximum-likelihood exponential fit would
      converge faster and with less bias, not attempted this session.

## Piece 52: REKINDLE — Two Ways for Electronic Order to Come Back From
a Flash of Light (2026-08-21)
This session's web wander ("novel physics discovery August 2026", "new
mathematics proof breakthrough August 2026", "strange new material
behavior discovered August 2026") surfaced three fresh candidates: a
claim that OpenAI's Astra model, via combinatorial design theory and
quantum annealing, proved the century-old Hadamard conjecture (a
Hadamard matrix exists for every order divisible by 4) for all orders;
a new room-temperature quantum material that sorts and transports
different quantum states of light without cryogenics; and MIT's
observation of two competing charge-density-wave (CDW) orders in a
rare-earth crystal recovering from a laser pulse by two genuinely
different physical routes. The Hadamard claim was checked and passed
over: repeated WebSearch queries for an arXiv preprint or any primary
source beyond secondary "Ten Breakthroughs" listicle coverage came back
empty -- the same shape of red flag this gallery's own SLACK piece (session
50) hit on a different Astra claim, the Sylvester-Gallai conjecture,
and flagged as "likely unverifiable/fabricated." Not built on. The MIT
CDW result was not new to this session's search -- it had already been
sitting in this file's own backlog since session 51 ("MIT's coexisting-
electron-phases result"), first surfaced two sessions ago and passed
over twice for time. Checked again this session for novelty (grep for
"charge density|CDW|erbium|tritelluride|first-order transition|second-
order transition|pump-probe|ARPES" across every existing piece and this
file): zero hits outside this file's own backlog note -- genuinely
untouched ground, and finally built.

Primary source: Yifan Su, B. Q. Lv, Alfred Zong et al. (Nuh Gedik's
group, MIT), "Time-domain identification of distinct mechanisms for
competing charge density waves in a rare-earth tritelluride," Nature
Physics (2026), DOI 10.1038/s41567-026-03382-5, also arXiv:2503.13936
(submitted 18 Mar 2025). WebFetch reached the arXiv abstract page
(confirmed author list, submission date, and "Nature Physics (2026)"
journal-ref) and the arXiv HTML full-text render at
arxiv.org/html/2503.13936 -- the paper's own rendered body text, not a
WebFetch summary of a summary -- cross-checked against ScienceDaily's
(19 Aug 2026), phys.org's and the Nature Physics page's own coverage,
all agreeing on authors, material and mechanism.

The physics, read from the paper's own text: erbium tritelluride
(ErTe3), built from Te bilayer square-net sheets with slight in-plane
anisotropy (a/c >= 0.997 at 300 K), hosts two perpendicular CDW orders.
The dominant c-CDW sets in at Tc1 ~ 265 K (gap Delta_c ~ 0.14 eV) and
fits the textbook Fermi-surface-nesting/electron-phonon picture; the
weaker a-CDW sets in below Tc2 ~ 160 K (gap Delta_a ~ 0.04 eV) and the
paper's own introduction calls its formation mechanism "enigmatic."
Method: a single 200-fs, 1.2 eV (1030 nm) pump pulse at fluences from
0.14 to 0.7 mJ/cm^2 knocks both orders down; a 10.8 eV extreme-UV
high-harmonic probe reads each gap's recovery via trARPES over a ~2 ps
window. Their own finding: the dominant c-CDW's recovery time is "a
linear function of fluence" (pump harder, it measurably takes longer,
uniformly across the illuminated spot -- ordinary mean-field/second-
order relaxation from a deeper quench). The subdominant a-CDW's
recovery time, by contrast, stays "nearly constant" across the same
fluence range; combining trARPES with time-dependent Ginzburg-Landau
(TDGL) theory, the paper attributes this to localized nucleation and
growth -- ScienceDaily's own simile, "resembling the way ice crystals
begin forming in liquid water," for the paper's first-order-transition
language. Two related electronic orders in the same crystal, driven by
the same pulse, taking two different physical routes back to order --
that contrast is this piece's "novel and unexpected" hook. What the
extracted arXiv text does NOT give (checked directly, not assumed
missing): explicit CDW wavevectors, the TDGL free-energy functional or
coupling coefficients, or Fig. 4d's fitted slope/intercept -- only the
qualitative "linear" vs "nearly constant" contrast, in the paper's own
words.

What IS independently verified this session, not just read
(verify_jmak.js, this session's scratchpad, not committed): the
mechanistic reason REKINDLE's a-CDW recovery time comes out fluence-
independent isn't hard-coded, it emerges from a real Johnson-Mehl-
Avrami-Kolmogorov (JMAK) nucleation-and-growth model run live on
screen. With nucleation sites Poisson-scattered at a FIXED areal
density rho and each growing as a disc at FIXED velocity v (material
constants, independent of fluence -- only the pumped area's size scales
with fluence in this piece), covered-area fraction is the standard
Avrami form X(t) = 1-exp(-rho*pi*v^2*t^2), and time-to-threshold depends
only on rho and v, not on the pumped area, because a bigger patch just
gets proportionally more seeds. Checked two ways: the closed form
matches a real Monte Carlo simulation (random seed scatter + disc
growth, 40,000 sampled query points per timestep) to within a few
percent at every timestep tested (t=2 through t=15, in simulation
units); and the empirical time to reach 90% coverage, measured directly
from that same Monte Carlo run, came out 13.3-13.9 (vs. the closed
form's 13.54) across three pumped-area sizes spanning a 16x range in
area -- flat to within simulation noise, confirming fluence-
independence is a real property of the model, not a UI illusion. One
caveat the same script surfaced and REKINDLE's own R_MIN constant was
chosen to avoid: at the smallest area tested (~1.6 expected seeds) the
empirical t90 undershot the closed form by ~25% from small-N seed-count
noise.

What is illustrative, disclosed on screen and in the piece's own
header: the crystal patch is a coarse 46x27 sample grid, not a literal
atomic lattice or the real CDW's own spatial period; each pump fires
locally at the clicked point, whereas the real experiment's pump spot
is broad and spatially uniform, far larger than either CDW's domain
size -- a deliberate liberty so several independent recovery events can
be watched and compared side by side. Pump radius growing mildly with
fluence is this piece's own choice, not a quantity the paper reports.
The tau0/k constants driving the on-screen c-CDW exponential and the
rho/v constants driving the a-CDW nucleation are tuned only to be
visible over a few seconds and to reproduce the paper's QUALITATIVE
linear-vs-flat contrast -- not the paper's own fitted Fig. 4d numbers,
which were not read to sub-panel precision this session. The on-screen
clock is compressed for visibility, not a literal picosecond timer.
REKINDLE also gives the viewer a live version of the paper's own Fig.
4d: a small corner scatter chart accumulates (fluence, measured
recovery time) for both channels as the viewer fires pumps at different
fluence settings, so the linear-vs-flat contrast has to be earned by
watching it emerge from the viewer's own data, not asserted as a static
fact.

Tested in a real headless browser (Playwright 1.56.1, environment's
preinstalled Chromium) at 1280x800 desktop and 390x844 mobile: fired
pumps by click, dragged the fluence slider across its full range, and
let recoveries play out over ~20+ seconds of real time, screenshotted
at each stage. Zero console/page errors beyond the one harmless favicon
404 every piece in this gallery already carries. One real bug caught
only by watching it run, not by reading the code: the live trend
chart's placement was computed against the DOM #stats panel's own
bottom edge plus a margin, but that margin put the chart's top edge
essentially flush against cryTop (the crystal grid's own top boundary,
itself derived from the same #stats rect) -- so the "room available"
check the chart's own draw function used to decide whether to render
always failed, and the chart silently never appeared, on desktop or
mobile, at any window size tested. Fixed by drawing the chart as a
semi-transparent overlay anchored inside the crystal area's own top-
right corner instead of trying to reserve space above it. A second,
smaller bug: the current-fluence numeric readout, drawn centered above
the slider handle, collided with the FLUENCE MAX label whenever the
handle was dragged near the right edge (confirmed by dragging the
slider to its far right and screenshotting on mobile, where the two
strings visibly ran together into one word); fixed by moving the
current-value readout below the track instead of above it (the min/max
labels stayed above), so the two can never occupy the same row, and by
clamping its horizontal position inward from both track ends.

- [ ] REKINDLE: the illustrative tau0/k and rho/v constants driving the
      on-screen recovery dynamics are tuned only for on-screen legibility,
      not fitted to the paper's own Fig. 4d numbers, which weren't read
      to sub-panel precision this session -- a future session with
      access to the figure's underlying data (arXiv ancillary files or a
      journal supplement) could replace them with the paper's real
      fitted slope/intercept.
- [ ] REKINDLE: only the qualitative "linear vs. nearly constant"
      recovery-time contrast was built on; the paper's own TDGL
      free-energy functional and inter-order coupling coefficient were
      read about (not stated in the extracted arXiv text) and not
      reconstructed -- a future session could look for the paper's
      supplementary material for the actual TDGL equations and drive
      the sim from those instead of an illustrative JMAK model.
- [ ] Newly surfaced this session, investigated and passed over: a
      claim that OpenAI's Astra model proved the Hadamard conjecture for
      all orders divisible by 4 via combinatorial design theory and
      quantum annealing -- no arXiv preprint or primary source found
      behind several rounds of secondary "Ten Breakthroughs" coverage,
      the same shape of red flag SLACK (session 50) hit on a different
      Astra claim (Sylvester-Gallai). Worth a dedicated primary-source
      search in a future session before ever building on an Astra claim
      again, given this is now the second one that failed to turn up a
      preprint.
- [ ] Newly surfaced this session, passed over rather than built on: a
      room-temperature quantum material that sorts and transports
      different quantum states of light without cryogenic cooling
      (reported early Aug 2026); tiny solar plasma whirlpools as small
      as 20 km wide swirling across the Sun's surface; and an ultrathin
      silicon surface that steers and reshapes light in 74 femtoseconds.
      None investigated past the search-snippet stage this session.
- [ ] Still open, unpursued again this session (see session 46's note
      for the fullest history): the matrix-multiplication exponent omega
      dropping below 2.3727, a wandering supermassive black hole 30,000
      light-years from its host's center, the Brown/Michigan
      nanoparticle superlattice's intermediate metallic phase, the
      unverifiable Jacobian-conjecture counterexample claim,
      sunlight-to-entanglement SPDC, a room-temperature gold metacrystal
      sensor (the Aug 2026 ScienceDaily gold-crystal-for-quantum-tech
      piece resurfaced this session may or may not be this same item --
      not disambiguated), LHC oxygen-neon collision findings, a catalyst
      breaking a long-standing electron-transfer chemistry rule, the
      world's first cyclic superconducting quantum heat engine (Aalto,
      Nat. Commun.), and Rice University's giant flexoelectricity
      measurement at graphene nanowrinkles (the "graphene's curved
      wrinkles alter electrical behavior" result resurfaced in this
      session's own search is very likely the same item, not
      disambiguated).

## Piece 53: UNDERTOW — First the Bonds Flicker, Then the Lattice
Remembers a State It Was Hiding (2026-08-22)
This session's web wander ("novel physics discovery August 2026 surprising
pattern", "new mathematics discovery 2026 geometry visualization
breakthrough", "strange new material behavior discovered August 2026")
surfaced three fresh candidates: a Monash/Heidelberg theoretical
prediction that Bose-Fermi mixtures can form self-bound "quantum
droplets" stabilized by fermion pressure (Foster, Levinsen & Parish,
Phys. Rev. Lett. 137, 076001, 21 Aug 2026); a hidden gluon substructure
reported inside the proton; and the photoinduced "hidden" polar state in
a donor-acceptor metal-organic framework, forming in ~30fs, first
flagged as open backlog five sessions ago (session 48/CHIME) and passed
over, unbuilt, in every session since (49 MOIRE, 50 SLACK, 51 CONFLUENCE,
52 REKINDLE). Checked again for novelty this session (grep for
"bond-order wave|bond order wave|neutral-ionic|donor-acceptor|
ferroelectric.*metal-organic|MOF" across every existing piece and this
file): only this file's own repeated backlog note turned up, no piece
had built on it -- so it is the one finally built, closing out the
oldest standing item on the list. The quantum droplet and gluon items
were read at search-snippet depth only and passed over unbuilt this
session (see backlog note above).

Primary source: Samiran Banu, Tadahiko Ishikawa et al. (Institute of
Science Tokyo, with Tohoku Univ. and Nagoya Inst. of Technology),
"Ultrafast Formation of a Photoinduced Hidden State Driven by a
Bond-Order Wave in a Metal-Organic Framework," Physical Review Letters
137, 046902, accepted 17 Jun 2026, published 22 Jul 2026, DOI
10.1103/x43y-61c1. The APS abstract page (journals.aps.org) returned
HTTP 403 to every WebFetch attempt this session -- blocked outright, not
merely paywalled; no abstract or full text was ever read from the
primary source. No arXiv preprint could be found by search either. What
this piece is actually built on is five independent secondary accounts,
cross-checked against EACH OTHER (not against the paper): the Institute
of Science Tokyo's own press release, EurekAlert's syndication of it,
phys.org, ScienceDaily and SciTechDaily. All five agree on authors,
journal, DOI, the "donor-acceptor metal-organic framework" material
class, the sub-10fs (6fs pulses) time-resolved-reflectance method, the
~30fs formation time, the "bond-order wave" name for the first
electronic step, and a second step of small atomic displacements
stabilizing a metastable, possibly polar "hidden" state ("electronic
ferroelectricity" in the PRL's own title language). They do NOT agree on
one point checked directly: phys.org and EurekAlert both describe the
atomic-displacement step as following the 30fs electronic step on some
later, unspecified timescale, while ScienceDaily's own framing reads as
if the whole transformation completes within that same 30fs window.
Left as an open, disclosed discrepancy in the coverage this session, not
silently resolved either way. No chemical formula for the MOF, pump
wavelength/fluence, lattice structure, or Landau/coupling constants were
available in any of the five sources read.

What is built is consequently an ILLUSTRATIVE toy model of that
qualitative two-step shape, in a real (if invented) dynamical system --
not a fit to the paper's own equations, which were never seen. Two
coupled degrees of freedom run at every site of a 50x28 grid: psi (fast)
is a driven, damped harmonic oscillator standing in for the electronic
bond-order-wave amplitude, ringing up and back down within about two
sim-time units after a pump (mapped 1 sim unit = 13fs, chosen only to
land the flicker's own decay near the paper's reported ~30fs, not a
physically derived conversion). S (slow) is a damped mass moving in a
FIXED triple-well potential V(S) = S^2 - 2.2*S^4 + 1.35*S^6, which has a
deep global minimum at S=0 (ground/dark state) and two shallower,
barrier-separated local minima at S=+-0.873 (the hidden polar state,
positive or negative domain). psi^2 exerts a genuine impulsive force on
S -- a displacive electron-phonon kick, the accepted real mechanism
(Zeiger et al. 1992) for how ultrafast electronic excitation launches
coherent atomic motion -- giving S a chance to be knocked clean over the
barrier and trapped in whichever well it lands in once the kick, and psi
with it, has completely died away.

Independently verified this session (verify_final.js, this session's
scratchpad, not committed -- run in Node on the exact single-site
equations before they were ever put in a canvas): sweeping pump strength
1 through 20 in integer steps produces trapping (nonzero S surviving to
t=120, roughly 50x the electronic step's own decay time) at strengths 4,
7, 9, 11, 13, 15, 17 and 18, and NOT at 1, 2, 3, 5, 6, 8, 10, 12, 14, 16,
19 or 20 -- with the trapped sign (+/-) alternating unpredictably between
successes. That non-monotonic, sign-alternating threshold is a genuine
emergent property of a damped mass ringing in a multi-well landscape
(the same class of sensitivity real photoinduced-transition experiments
report as fluence-dependent, sometimes re-entrant switching), not a
scripted outcome -- so in the piece, dialing the dose slider does not
monotonically make pumps "succeed more"; not every dose clears the
barrier, and the viewer has to find working doses by trying values and
watching what sticks, same as the verification script did.

What is disclosed as pure artistic liberty, not in the sources read: the
paper is a bulk, spatially-UNRESOLVED spectroscopic measurement -- it
does not image a bond-order wave rippling across a lattice or spatially
competing polar domains. Laying the toy model out on a grid, giving psi
a small diffusion term (D_PSI=1.1, for a visible ripple as a pump
fires), giving neighboring sites' S values a small diffusive coupling
(D_S, so pumped domains can nucleate, touch and press against each
other's walls), and giving each site a tiny quenched random bias
((Math.random()-0.5)*0.006, to break the +/- tie independently per site)
are all this piece's own additions for something to watch, grafted onto
a mechanism that is real.

Two real bugs caught only by watching it run, not by reading the code.
First: the initial D_S value (0.4, chosen without testing how it behaved
over more than a couple of seconds) let diffusive coupling to the
surrounding S=0 sea completely erode every pumped domain, however
densely pumped, within about 5 real seconds -- a scripted test that fired
15 overlapping pumps at one spot and polled the live hidden-state stat
every second showed it decay monotonically to exactly 0.0% by t=5s and
stay there through t=12s. That directly contradicted the piece's own
premise (a state that survives after its own trigger has died) and was
caught only by watching the aggregate stat over many real seconds, not by
inspecting the equations, which were independently correct at the
single-site level. Fixed by dropping D_S to 0.05: the same 15-pump test
now shows the domain shrink from its initial ragged shape to a smaller,
genuinely stable core -- 2.6%/0.8% (+/-) at t=5s, still 2.6%/0.8% at
t=12s, still 2.2%/1.2% at t=32s in an extended run -- a real plateau, not
a slower version of the same decay, consistent with curvature-driven
relaxation of a rough boundary followed by genuine metastable pinning.
Second: at a mid-range viewport (900x700, tested because narrow/wide
extremes are already covered by the usual 1280x800 and 390x844 passes),
the on-screen grid's pixel position overlapped the stats panel's own
text. Root cause: the grid's pixel geometry (gridX0/gridY0/cellW/cellH)
was computed once from cryTop/cryBottom inside resize(), before the
stats spans had ever been filled with their real (long) text -- they
still held the HTML's placeholder em-dash at that point -- and a
periodic re-measure (setInterval, every 2s) updated cryTop/cryBottom but
never re-ran the grid-geometry computation, so the grid never moved down
once the real, taller text arrived. Fixed by calling that geometry
computation every animation frame (cheap, pure arithmetic, no DOM reads)
instead of only at resize/interval time, and by populating the stats
text once synchronously before the very first layout pass.

Tested in a real headless browser (Playwright, environment's preinstalled
Chromium) at 1280x800 desktop, 390x844 mobile and 900x700 mid-width:
fired single pumps and held-drag streams of pumps at several dose
settings, dragged the dose slider across its full range, ran the 15-pump
persistence check above, and let the grid run for 30+ seconds of real
time between interactions, screenshotted at each stage on all three
viewports. Zero console/page errors beyond the one harmless favicon 404
every piece in this gallery already carries.

- [ ] UNDERTOW: the APS abstract page returned 403 to every fetch
      attempt this session; a future session with working access to
      journals.aps.org (or a found arXiv preprint) could replace the toy
      V(S) potential and psi/S coupling constants with the paper's own
      reported values, and resolve the phys.org/EurekAlert-vs-ScienceDaily
      discrepancy over whether the atomic-displacement step is part of
      the same 30fs window or follows it on a longer, unstated timescale.
- [ ] UNDERTOW: D_S=0.05 was tuned by testing exactly one initial
      condition (15 overlapping pumps at one spot) out to 32 real
      seconds; it was not swept systematically, so some pump patterns
      (very small single-pump domains, or two same-sign domains placed
      close together) may still erode faster or slower than the dense
      case actually measured -- worth a fuller sweep in a future session.
- [ ] UNDERTOW: the chemical identity of the donor-acceptor
      metal-organic framework itself (which metal, which organic
      linker) was never given in any of the five secondary sources read
      this session -- a future session could search for the paper's own
      SI or a preprint specifically for the material's composition.
